Decentralized Multi-Authority Attribute-Based Inner-Product Functional Encryption: Noisy and Evasive Constructions from Lattices †
Jiaqi Liu, Yan Wang, Fang-Wei Fu

Abstract
We initiate the study of multi-authority attribute-based functional encryption for noisy inner-product functionality , and propose two new primitives: (1) multi-authority attribute-based (noisy) inner-product functional encryption ( 𝖬𝖠 ​ - ​ 𝖠𝖡 ​ ( 𝖭 ) ​ 𝖨𝖯𝖥𝖤 \mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE} ), and (2) multi-authority attribute-based evasive inner-product functional encryption ( 𝖬𝖠 ​ - ​ 𝖠𝖡𝖾𝗏𝖨𝖯𝖥𝖤 \mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE} ). The 𝖬𝖠 ​ - ​ 𝖠𝖡 ​ ( 𝖭 ) ​ 𝖨𝖯𝖥𝖤 \mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE} primitive generalizes the existing multi-authority attribute-based inner-product functional encryption schemes by Agrawal et al. [ 9 ] , by enabling approximate inner-product computation under decentralized attribute-based control. This newly proposed notion combines the approximate function evaluation of noisy inner-product functional encryption ( 𝖨𝖯𝖥𝖤 \mathsf{IPFE} ) with the decentralized key-distribution structure of multi-authority attribute-based encryption. To better capture noisy functionalities within a flexible security framework, we formulate the 𝖬𝖠 ​ - ​ 𝖠𝖡𝖾𝗏𝖨𝖯𝖥𝖤 \mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE} primitive under a generic-model view, inspired by the evasive 𝖨𝖯𝖥𝖤 \mathsf{IPFE} framework by Hsieh et al. [ 32 ] . It shifts the focus from pairwise ciphertext indistinguishability to a more relaxed pseudorandomness-based game. To support the above notions, we introduce two variants of lattice-based computational assumptions: • The evasive 𝖨𝖯𝖥𝖤 \mathsf{IPFE} assumption ( 𝖾𝗏𝖨𝖯𝖥𝖤 \mathsf{evIPFE} ): it generalizes the assumption introduced in [ 32 ] to the multi-authority setting and admits a reduction from the evasive 𝖫𝖶𝖤 \mathsf{LWE} assumption proposed by Waters et al. [ 44 ] ; • The indistinguishability-based evasive 𝖨𝖯𝖥𝖤 \mathsf{IPFE} assumption ( 𝖨𝖭𝖣 ​ - ​ 𝖾𝗏𝖨𝖯𝖥𝖤 \mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE} ): it is an indistinguishability-based variant of the evasive 𝖨𝖯𝖥𝖤 \mathsf{IPFE} assumption designed to capture the stronger security guarantees required by our 𝖬𝖠 ​ - ​ 𝖠𝖡 ​ ( 𝖭 ) ​ 𝖨𝖯𝖥𝖤 \mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE} scheme. We present concrete lattice-based constructions for both primitives supporting subset policies, building upon the framework of [ 44 ] . Our schemes are proven to be statically secure in the random oracle model under the standard 𝖫𝖶𝖤 \mathsf{LWE} assumption and the newly introduced assumptions. Additionally, we demonstrate that our 𝖬𝖠 ​ - ​ 𝖠𝖡 ​ ( 𝖭 ) ​ 𝖨𝖯𝖥𝖤 \mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE} scheme can be transformed, via standard modulus switching, into a noiseless 𝖬𝖠 ​ - ​ 𝖠𝖡𝖨𝖯𝖥𝖤 \mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE} scheme that supports exact inner-product functionality consistent with the 𝖬𝖠 ​ - ​ 𝖠𝖡𝖨𝖯𝖥𝖤 \mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE} syntax in [ 9 , 28 ] . This yields the first lattice-based construction of such a primitive. All our schemes support arbitrary polynomial-size attribute policies and are secure in the random oracle model under lattice assumptions with a sub-exponential modulus-to-noise ratio, making them practical candidates for noise-tolerant, fine-grained access control in multi-authority settings.



I
Introduction

Functional Encryption
(
\mathsf{FE}
), introduced by Boneh, Sahai, and Waters
[
18
]
, is a versatile cryptographic paradigm that extends the capabilities of traditional public-key encryption schemes. It facilitates fine-grained access control over encrypted data, enabling authorized users to compute specific functions of the plaintext without recovering the entire plaintext. More formally, in an
\mathsf{FE}
scheme, each secret key
\mathsf{sk}_{f}
is associated with a particular function
f
. Given an encryption
\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{mpk},\mathbf{x})
of a message
\mathbf{x}
encrypted under the master public key
\mathsf{mpk}
, decrypting with
\mathsf{sk}_{f}
reveals only the value
f(\mathbf{x})
without leaking any additional information about the message
\mathbf{x}
. The inherent property of selective disclosure in
\mathsf{FE}
makes it particularly valuable in applications involving sensitive or confidential data.
For example, in healthcare settings,
\mathsf{FE}
allows researchers to aggregate statistics from encrypted patient records while maintaining the confidentiality of individual data entries.

The standard security framework for
\mathsf{FE}
is
indistinguishability-based security
(
\mathsf{IND}
). In this model, an adversary attempts to distinguish between the encryptions of two selected messages
\mathbf{x}_{0}
and
\mathbf{x}_{1}
, while being allowed to query secret keys
\mathsf{sk}_{f}
for functions
f
such that
f(\mathbf{x}_{0})=f(\mathbf{x}_{1})
. This restriction ensures that the adversary gains no additional information beyond the outputs of permitted functions. The security guarantee requires that the adversary remain unable to distinguish between the ciphertexts, even when given access to multiple such keys.

Inner-Product Functional Encryption.
A notable subclass of
\mathsf{FE}
schemes designed for computing linear functions is known as
Inner-Product Functional Encryption
(
\mathsf{IPFE}
), which has become an active area of research over the past decade. The study of
\mathsf{IPFE}
began with the work of Abdalla et al.
[
1
]
and has since been extensively studied in a series of works
[
2
,
10
,
25
,
8
,
13
,
23
,
5
,
35
,
3
,
41
,
4
]
. In an
\mathsf{IPFE}
scheme, a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}_{\mathbf{u}}
encrypts an
\ell
-dimensional vector
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{R}^{\ell}
over some ring
\mathcal{R}
, while a secret key
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathbf{v}}
for a vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{R}^{\ell}
enables the computation of the inner-product function
f_{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot)=\langle\cdot,\mathbf{v}\rangle
. Decrypting
\mathsf{ct}_{\mathbf{u}}
using
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathbf{v}}
yields the value
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\rangle
, without leaking any other information about the message
\mathbf{u}
.
\mathsf{IPFE}
provides a powerful cryptographic tool for selective computation over encrypted data, ensuring that sensitive information remains protected.
Notable applications include secure data analysis, privacy-preserving machine learning, and privacy-enhanced database queries. In addition,
\mathsf{IPFE}
serves as a foundational building block for more advanced cryptographic primitives, such as
\mathsf{FE}
for quadratic functions
[
34
,
29
]
and
attribute-based encryption
(
\mathsf{ABE}
)
[
43
,
32
]
. These extensions further enhance the versatility and applicability of functional encryption.

Building on
\mathsf{IPFE}
, Agrawal
[
7
,
11
]
introduced
Noisy Inner-Product Functional Encryption
(
\mathsf{NIPFE}
), an extension that incorporates noise into the computation process. Unlike the standard
\mathsf{IPFE}
scheme in
[
1
]
, which supports the
exact
evaluation of inner products,
\mathsf{NIPFE}
enables the computation of
approximate
inner products with an additive noise term of the form
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\rangle+e
, where
e
is a small-norm noise term. This relaxation allows decryption to return an approximate result, which improves flexibility in noisy settings. The security guarantee of
\mathsf{NIPFE}
ensures that for any two plaintext vectors
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
, and key vectors
\mathbf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{v}_{m}
, if
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{j}\rangle\approx\langle\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{j}\rangle
holds for each
j
, the ciphertexts of
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
remain computationally indistinguishable, even when the adversary is given the secret keys corresponding to
\mathbf{v}_{j}
. The security property is achieved by leveraging the noise term
e
, which effectively “smudges” small differences in the inner-product computations, thereby preventing the adversary from extracting meaningful distinguishing information.
\mathsf{NIPFE}
provides a security guarantee in applications in which a certain level of noise tolerance is acceptable, balancing between utility and privacy.

Attribute-Based Inner-Product Functional Encryption.

Attribute-Based Inner-Product Functional Encryption
(
\mathsf{AB}\text{-}\mathsf{IPFE}
), introduced by Abdalla et al.
[
6
]
, is an advanced cryptographic primitive that combines the access control capabilities of
\mathsf{ABE}
with the inner-product computation functionality of
\mathsf{IPFE}
. This hybrid notion enables fine-grained access control over encrypted data while supporting privacy-preserving computations on ciphertexts. In a
ciphertext-policy

\mathsf{AB}\text{-}\mathsf{IPFE}
scheme, each ciphertext is associated with a set of attributes, and each secret key corresponds to an access policy defined over the attribute universe, following a structure similar to that of a ciphertext-policy
\mathsf{ABE}
. In the dual setting, namely the
key-policy

\mathsf{AB}\text{-}\mathsf{IPFE}
scheme, ciphertexts are associated with access policies, and secret keys are associated with attribute sets. In either case, decryption is allowed only when the attributes satisfy the access policy. Unlike traditional
\mathsf{IPFE}
,
\mathsf{AB}\text{-}\mathsf{IPFE}
allows inner-product computations on encrypted data only when decryption is authorized, making it particularly useful for applications such as privacy-preserving machine learning, secure data analytics, and encrypted search. By integrating the strengths of
\mathsf{ABE}
and
\mathsf{IPFE}
,
\mathsf{AB}\text{-}\mathsf{IPFE}
offers a powerful tool for secure data sharing and processing in multi-user environments, with practical relevance to applications such as cloud computing, the Internet of Things (IoT), and healthcare systems.

Multi-Authority Attribute-Based Inner-Product Functional Encryption.

Most prior work on
\mathsf{ABE}
or
\mathsf{AB}\text{-}\mathsf{IPFE}
focuses on the single-authority setting, where a central trusted authority is the only entity responsible for validating user attributes and issuing the corresponding secret keys. However, in many practical scenarios, it is more natural for multiple separate authorities to independently manage and authorize different subsets of attributes for users.
Multi-Authority

\mathsf{ABE}
(
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABE}
)
[
36
,
40
,
26
,
27
,
44
]
addresses this limitation by decentralizing the authority structure in an
\mathsf{ABE}
system, allowing multiple independent authorities to operate and manage attribute-based access control. This decentralization improves the scalability and flexibility of the system. Motivated by the need for secure and privacy-preserving computation in decentralized environments, recent efforts have explored the integration of
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABE}
and
\mathsf{IPFE}
into a unified framework known as
Multi-Authority Attribute-Based Inner-Product Functional Encryption
(
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
). Agrawal et al.
[
9
]
first highlighted the potential of combining multi-authority systems with functional encryption by extending the attribute-based component of
\mathsf{AB}\text{-}\mathsf{IPFE}
to a multi-authority setting.
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
is a significant cryptographic framework that unifies the decentralized access control of
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABE}
and the computation functionality of
\mathsf{IPFE}
.

Informally, in an
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
scheme, each authority generates its own master secret key and is responsible for issuing secret keys associated with attributes it governs. Let
\mathsf{ct}_{f}(\mathbf{u})
be a ciphertext encrypting a plaintext vector
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{R}^{\ell}
under an access policy
P
. A user holding secret keys corresponding to a vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{R}^{\ell}
and a set of attributes
A
can recover the inner product
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\rangle
if and only if
A
satisfies the policy
P
(i.e.,
A
is
authorized
); otherwise, the ciphertext reveals no information about
\mathbf{u}
.

I-A

Related Works

The
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
scheme proposed in
[
9
]
lifts the
\mathsf{AB}\text{-}\mathsf{IPFE}
construction in
[
6
]
to the multi-authority setting. It is known as the first nontrivial multi-authority
\mathsf{FE}
scheme beyond
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABE}
. It supports access policies that can be realized by
Linear Secret Sharing Schemes
(
\mathsf{LSSS}
) and is constructed using pairing-based techniques. Its security relies on variants of the subgroup decision assumptions over composite-order bilinear groups introduced in
[
19
]
.

Subsequently, Datta and Pal
[
28
]
proposed two
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
schemes that also support
\mathsf{LSSS}
-based access structures. These schemes are built in the more efficient prime-order bilinear group setting and rely on the well-studied
Decisional Bilinear Diffie-Hellman Assumption
(
\mathsf{DBDH}
) and its variants. Their constructions are proven secure in the Random Oracle Model. By moving to the prime-order group setting, their constructions achieve notable improvements in efficiency over the scheme of
[
9
]
. The construction supports computations over vectors of a priori unbounded length and an unbounded number of authorities. Furthermore, the schemes in
[
28
]
overcome the “one-use” restriction, allowing attributes to appear arbitrarily many times within access policies, thereby providing greater flexibility and versatility in policy design.

Evasive
\mathsf{LWE}
Assumption.

Evasive Learning with Errors
(Evasive
\mathsf{LWE}
)
[
42
]
is a non-standard variant of the
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption. Roughly, the assumption states that for any efficient sampling algorithm
\mathsf{Samp}
that outputs a matrix
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times t}
,

\displaystyle\text{if}\quad(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\top},\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top},\mathsf{aux})\approx(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{Q},\$_{1},\$_{2},\mathsf{aux}),

(1)

\displaystyle\text{then}\quad(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\top},\mathbf{A}^{-1}(\mathbf{Q}),\mathsf{aux})\approx(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{Q},\$_{1},\mathbf{A}^{-1}(\mathbf{Q}),\mathsf{aux}),

for uniformly random matrix
\mathbf{A}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
, uniformly random
\mathsf{LWE}
secret
\mathbf{s}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, Gaussian noise vectors
\mathbf{e}_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\mathbf{e}_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{t}
, and uniformly random vectors
\$_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\$_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{t}
. Here,
\mathbf{A}^{-1}(\mathbf{Q})
denotes a low-norm (typically Gaussian) preimage of
\mathbf{Q}
with respect to
\mathbf{A}
, i.e., a matrix with low-norm entries such that
\mathbf{A}\cdot(\mathbf{A}^{-1}(\mathbf{Q}))=\mathbf{Q}
. In
[
42
]
, the assumption is formulated for
public-coin
sampling algorithms
\mathsf{Samp}
, meaning that the auxiliary string
\mathsf{aux}
contains all the tossed coins (randomness) used by
\mathsf{Samp}
.

Intuitively, the evasive
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption essentially asserts that the only meaningful way to exploit the preimage
\mathbf{A}^{-1}(\mathbf{Q})
is to multiply it by the
\mathsf{LWE}
sample
\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\top}
, obtaining

(\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\top})\cdot(\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{Q})\approx\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{Q},

and then attempting to distinguish this value from uniform samples. However, the precondition (
1
) in the assumption guarantees that this advantage remains negligible, thereby preventing zeroizing attacks such as those discussed in
[
22
,
24
,
31
,
33
]
.

In follow-up work,
[
44
]
proposed a variant of public-coin evasive
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption involving multiple matrix pairs
(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{Q}_{i})_{i}
, along with their respective
\mathsf{LWE}
samples
(\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{1,i}^{\top},\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{2,i}^{\top})_{i}
. Roughly, the evasive
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption in
[
44
]
states that if

\displaystyle\text{if}\quad(\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i},\{\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{2,i}^{\top}\}_{i},\mathsf{aux})\approx(\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\$_{1,i}\}_{i},\{\$_{2,i}\}_{i},\mathsf{aux}),

\displaystyle\text{then}\quad(\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i},\{\mathbf{A}_{i}^{-1}(\mathbf{Q}_{i})\}_{i},\mathsf{aux})\approx(\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\$_{1,i}\}_{i},\{\mathbf{A}_{i}^{-1}(\mathbf{Q}_{i})\}_{i},\mathsf{aux}),

where
\mathbf{s}^{\top}=[\mathbf{s}_{1}^{\top}\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{s}_{\ell}^{\top}]\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell}
is an
\mathsf{LWE}
secret.

Evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
.

Evasive Inner-Product Functional Encryption
(Evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
), introduced by Yao-Ching Hsieh, Huijia Lin, and Ji Luo in
[
32
]
, extends the standard
\mathsf{IPFE}
framework
[
1
]
. Similar to the
\mathsf{NIPFE}
scheme
[
7
]
, evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
encrypts an
\ell
-dimensional vector
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{R}^{\ell}
over some ring
\mathcal{R}
into a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}_{\mathbf{u}}
, and allows the generation of secret keys
\{\mathsf{sk}_{\mathbf{v}_{j}}\}_{j}
for vectors
\{\mathbf{v}_{j}\}_{j}
. Decryption using
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathbf{v}_{j}}
yields noisy inner products of the form
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{j}\rangle+e_{j}
, where each
e_{j}
is a small-norm noise term, while revealing no additional information about
\mathbf{u}
. The key distinction lies in the underlying security model, which reflects a
generic-model view
analogous to that of the evasive
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption. Specifically,

\displaystyle\text{ if}\quad\{\mathbf{v}_{j},\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{j}\rangle+e_{j}\}_{j}\approx\{\mathbf{v}_{j},\$_{j}\}_{j},

\displaystyle\text{ then}\quad(\mathsf{ct}_{\mathbf{u}},\{\mathbf{v}_{j},\mathsf{sk}_{\mathbf{v}_{j}}\}_{j})\approx(\mathsf{ct}_{\$},\{\mathbf{v}_{j},\mathsf{sk}_{\mathbf{v}_{j}}\}_{j}),

where each
e_{j}
is small-norm noise term, and each
\$_{j}
and
\$
is a uniformly random elements over the appropriate range. In other words, if the noisy inner products with
\mathbf{u}
are pseudorandom, then the ciphertext encrypting
\mathbf{u}
is also pseudorandom, even given the key-vector pairs
\{\mathbf{v}_{j},\mathsf{sk}_{\mathbf{v}_{j}}\}_{j}
.

I-B

Our Results

In this work, we make the following conceptual and technical contributions:
We begin by proposing two new notions for attribute-based functional encryption in the multi-authority setting (Section
V
).

•

Multi-authority attribute-based (noisy) inner-product functional encryption (
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
)
: We propose the notion of
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
, a natural generalization of the
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
scheme
[
9
,
28
]
by allowing the
approximate
computation of inner products rather than
exact
ones when decrypting using authorized secret keys. The
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
primitive integrates two core features: (i) the approximate inner-product functionality of
\mathsf{NIPFE}
, and (ii) the decentralized access control mechanism of
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABE}
, where secret keys are issued by multiple authorities based on their attribute sets. This combination enables fine-grained, decentralized access control over approximate inner-product computations in a multi-authority setting.

We then formalize the security of the
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
scheme in the static setting, following the framework of
[
40
,
44
]
. In this model (also referred to as the
selective
model)
the adversary must commit to its challenge plaintexts, secret-key queries, and authority corruption choices immediately after the global parameters are initialized. Unlike the standard
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABE}
setting, where secret-key queries must be unauthorized to decrypt the challenge ciphertext, our static security model permits the adversary to request even
authorized
secret keys. However, such secret keys yield sufficiently
close
inner products when evaluated over the two challenge plaintexts. This relaxed notion is tailored to the noisy functional nature of our scheme. It ensures that no efficient adversary can distinguish between the encryptions of two plaintexts unless it can produce keys that yield meaningfully different decryption results.

•

Multi-authority attribute-based evasive inner-product functional encryption (
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
)
: We further introduce the notion of
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
, which serves as a relaxed variant of
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
in terms of its underlying security definition. While both primitives enable approximate inner-product computation in a multi-authority setting, the
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
scheme adopts a “generic-model view” inspired by recent works on evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
in
[
32
]
. Instead of requiring the adversary to distinguish between two challenge ciphertexts, the security model is formulated via a pseudorandomness-based game: the goal of the adversary is to distinguish the ciphertext of a structured plaintext generated by a public-coin sampler from that of a uniformly random plaintext.

The main difference between the adversarial capabilities in the
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
and
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
games lies in the secret-key queries. For unauthorized queries, both models handle them identically. However, for authorized queries, the adversary in
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
is more restricted. Specifically, it does not have full control over both the attribute set and the key vector
\mathbf{v}
that together define the secret-key queries. Instead, the adversary specifies only the attribute set, while the key vector
\mathbf{v}
is sampled by a public-coin sampler
\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}}
. This restriction mirrors the structure of evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
games. In addition, the challenge plaintext
\mathbf{u}
is sampled by another public sampling algorithm
\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{u}}
with private randomness (i.e., the adversary does not have access to
\mathbf{u}
). The security definition guarantees that if the sampler pair
(\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}},\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{u}})
produces noisy pseudorandom inner products, i.e.,

(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\rangle+e)\approx(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\$),

then no efficient adversary can distinguish the ciphertext of
\mathbf{u}
and that of a uniformly random plaintext with non-negligible advantage. Here
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}
denotes the randomness used by
\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}}
,
e
is a noise term, and
\$
is a uniformly random element.

To instantiate the above notions, we provide three concrete lattice-based constructions, supporting approximate inner-product computation under subset policies with decentralized access control, offering different trade-offs between noise management and security guarantees.

Construction of
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
.
We construct an
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
scheme for subset policies, following the framework of
[
44
]
. Our construction is proven to be statically secure in the random oracle model under the standard
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption and a new evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
assumption (
\mathsf{evIPFE}
) introduced in this work, which generalizes the evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
assumption proposed in
[
32
]
. The idea of evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
assumption is conceptually motivated by the evasive
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption and admits a reduction from the evasive
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption from
[
44
]
(cf. Appendix
A
). Full details of the construction, security proof, and parameter selection are provided in Section
VI
.

Theorem 1

(Informal)
.

Suppose that the
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption and the
\mathsf{evLWE}
assumption (cf. Section
IV
) hold with sub-exponential modulus-to-noise ratio, then there exists a statically secure
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
scheme for subset policies of arbitrary polynomial size in the random oracle model.

Construction of
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
.
We then construct an
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
scheme for subset policies. This construction follows the same syntax as the
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
scheme but achieves a stronger notion of security under different lattice-based assumptions. Specifically, it is proven to be statically secure in the random oracle model under the
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption and the new
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption (cf. Section
IV
) introduced in this work. The
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption is an indistinguishability-based variant of the
\mathsf{evIPFE}
discussed earlier. Full details of the construction, security analysis, and parameter selection are provided in Section
VII
.

Theorem 2

(Informal)
.

Suppose that the
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption and the
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption (cf. Section
IV
) hold with sub-exponential modulus-to-noise ratio, then there exists a statically secure
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
scheme for subset policies of arbitrary polynomial size in the random oracle model.

Construction of Noiseless
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
.
Finally, we modify our
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
scheme into a noiseless
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
scheme, which enables
exact
computation of the inner products upon successful decryption.
This construction aligns with the definition of
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
from
[
9
,
28
]
. The construction proposed in this work is the first such scheme based on lattice-related assumptions. Our modification relies on a standard modulus switching technique that removes the noise introduced during decryption. The resulting scheme retains a similar overall structure but achieves exact correctness. The scheme is proven to be statically secure in the random oracle model, under the
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption and the
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption. Full details of the construction, security analysis, and parameter selection are provided in Section
VIII
.

Theorem 3

(Informal)
.

Suppose that the
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption and the
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption (cf. Section
IV
) hold with sub-exponential modulus-to-noise ratio, then there exists a statically secure
noiseless

\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
scheme for subset policies of arbitrary polynomial size in the random oracle model.

I-C

Paper Organization

The paper is organized as follows. Section
II
provides a technical overview of our main constructions and ideas. Section
III
introduces necessary notations, basic concepts of lattices, the
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption, and its variants. Section
IV
, presents two variants of the Evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
assumptions, building on the framework of
[
32
]
. In Section
V
, we formalize monotone access structures and give the definitions of multi-authority attribute-based evasive inner-product functional encryption (
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
) and multi-authority attribute-based (noisy) inner-product functional encryption (
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
). Section
VI
presents our construction of an
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
scheme. Section
VII
introduces a construction of an
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
scheme. Section
VIII
describes a construction of a noiseless
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
scheme using the modulus-switching technique. Sections
VI
through
VIII
include detailed analyses of correctness, security, and parameter selection.

II
Technical Overviews

In this section, we demonstrate a high-level overview of our construction of multi-authority attribute-based (noisy/evasive) inner-product functional encryption with subset policies in the random oracle model. Our construction is inspired by the frameworks of
[
44
]
and
[
32
]
.

Preliminaries.

We introduce some notations used throughout this section. To simplify expressions involving noise, we adopt the wavy underline to indicate that a term is perturbed by some noise term. For example, we use the notation
\uwave{\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{A}}
to denote the term
\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{e}
for an unspecified noise vector
\mathbf{e}
. For a discrete set
S
, we denote
x\xleftarrow{\$}S
as
\mathbf{x}
is uniformly sampled from the set
S
. The term
\mathbf{A}^{-1}(\mathbf{y})
means sampling a short preimage
\mathbf{x}
such that
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y}
. For a positive integer
n\in\mathbb{N}
, denote
[n]
as the set
\{k\in\mathbb{N}:1\leq k\leq n\}
. Let
\$
denote a uniformly random vector of appropriate dimension. Let
\mathbf{G}_{n}=\mathbf{I}_{n}\otimes\mathbf{g}^{\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times n\lceil\log q\rceil}
denote the gadget matrix, where
\mathbf{g}^{\top}=(1,2,\ldots,2^{\lceil\log q\rceil-1})
. The inverse function
\mathbf{G}_{n}^{-1}
maps the vectors in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
to their binary expansions in
\{0,1\}^{n\lceil\log q\rceil}
. We will omit the subscript when the dimension
n
is clear. We use
\overset{c}{\approx}
as the abbreviation for computationally indistinguishable.

II-A

Our Schemes

Let
[L]
denote the index set of all authorities. In the subset policy setting, each ciphertext is associated with a subset
X\subseteq[L]
, while each secret key is associated with a subset
Y\subseteq[L]
and a key vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
. Decryption of a ciphertext encrypting
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
associated with
X
using the secret key corresponding to
Y
and
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
yields an approximate value of the Euclidean inner product
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\rangle=\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v}
whenever
X\subseteq Y
.

We next provide an informal description of our construction. The scheme consists of the following components:

•

The master public key for the authority indexed by
i
is given by
(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{B}_{i},\mathbf{P}_{i})\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
.

•

The master secret key for the authority indexed by
i
is the trapdoor
\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}_{i}}
for the matrix
\mathbf{A}_{i}
. This trapdoor allows efficient sampling of short preimages for
\mathbf{A}
.

•

Given a subset
Y\subseteq[L]
, a key vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, and a global identifier
\mathsf{gid}
, each authority
i\in Y
uses its trapdoor to generate the secret key component corresponding to
i
. The corresponding secret key is generated as

\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\{\mathbf{A}_{i}^{-1}(\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v})+\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}))\}_{i\in Y}.

Here
\mathsf{H}
is a hash function modeled as a random oracle that outputs vectors in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}}
with small norm.

•

To encrypt a message
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
under an attribute set
X\subseteq[L]
, the ciphertext is generated as

\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\left(\{\uwave{\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}}\}_{i\in X},\uwave{\sum_{i\in X}\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{i}},\uwave{\sum_{i\in X}\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i}}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\right),

where
\mathbf{s}_{i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
for each
i\in X
.

•

The decryption operation using
\mathsf{sk}
is performed as follows. It first computes
\mathbf{r}\leftarrow\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})
, and then computes

-\sum_{i\in X}\uwave{\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{i}^{-1}(\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v})+\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{r})+\uwave{\left(\sum_{i\in X}\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{i}\right)}\cdot\mathbf{r}+\left(\uwave{\sum_{i\in X}\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i}}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\right)\cdot\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v})\approx\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v}

whenever
Y\supseteq X
.

The randomization of
\mathsf{gid}
and
\mathbf{v}
.

To prevent collusion among users in the system who might attempt to combine their individual secret keys to decrypt ciphertexts, which contradicts the intended security requirements of our setting, we adopt the
global identifier model
(
\mathsf{gid}
), which is a standard technique widely used in multi-authority cryptographic schemes.

In the
\mathsf{gid}
model, each user is assigned a unique and efficiently verifiable global identifier during setup, which remains fixed once global parameters are established. Upon verifying a user’s global identifier, the authority validates his access rights and issues secret keys only for attributes authorized for that user. When instantiated in the random oracle model, the hash value of the global identifier
\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{gid})
provides consistent randomness across secret keys issued by different authorities, thereby preventing unauthorized key combination. In our
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
setting, we further extend this model by additionally incorporating the key
vector
\mathbf{v}
as part of the input to the hash function. This ensures that secret keys are randomized with respect to both the user’s identity and their functional target, further mitigating potential collusion attacks. We elaborate on this approach and propose our constructions in Sections
VI
\sim
VIII
.

II-B

Static Security for Our Scheme

In the following, we provide a high-level overview demonstrating that the proposed scheme is statically secure under certain lattice-based assumptions. The static security model of
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
follows a structure similar to that of
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABE}
. Specifically, the security game involves a set of corrupt authorities
\mathcal{C}\subseteq[L]
. The adversary is allowed to arbitrarily assign the public keys and master secret keys for these corrupt authorities. Let
X\subseteq[L]
denote the subset of authorities associated with the challenge ciphertexts. The adversary’s goal is to distinguish between the encryptions of two selected vectors
\mathbf{u}_{0}
and
\mathbf{u}_{1}
under the authority set
X
.

Within this setting, the adversary is also allowed to submit secret-key queries of the form
\{(\mathsf{gid},Y,\mathbf{v})\}
, where
Y
is a subset of non-corrupt (honest) authorities,
\mathsf{gid}
is a global user identifier, and
\mathbf{v}
is a key vector. Each secret-key query must satisfy at least one of the two following conditions:

(a)

(Y\cup\mathcal{C})\cap X\subsetneqq X
: This condition reflects the multi-authority aspect of the setting. The security requirement states that even if the adversary has access to secret keys with authorities that do not satisfy the policy (unauthorized keys), the ciphertexts remain indistinguishable, aligning with the static security notion in the
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABE}
scheme.

(b)

(\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\approx 0
: This condition enforces the inner-product functional encryption scheme requirement. It guarantees that secret keys satisfying the policy reveal only an approximate value of the inner-product
\mathbf{u}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{v}
(
i=0,1
), and no additional information beyond that.

Weakened Static Security:
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
Scheme.

Inspired by the evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
framework introduced in
[
32
]
, we begin our analysis with a weaker notion of static security, under which we define the
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
scheme. While sharing the same syntax as the
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
, this primitive imposes weaker adversarial
control. In particular, the queries mentioned above are no longer entirely determined by the adversary. More precisely, the secret-key queries satisfying condition (b) are jointly generated by both the adversary and the challenger, while the challenge plaintext
\mathbf{u}_{0}
is generated by a public-coin sampler and
\mathbf{u}_{1}
is uniformly sampled. This setting reflects a “generic-model view” of the scheme, analogous to evasive
\mathsf{LWE}

[
42
]
and evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}

[
32
]
. The challenge plaintext
\mathbf{u}_{0}
and the secret key queries
\{\mathsf{sk}_{\mathbf{v}_{j}}\}_{j}
(associated with key vector
\mathbf{v}_{j}
) satisfying condition (b) are generated such that

\{\mathbf{v}_{j},\{\uwave{\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{j}}\}_{j}\}\overset{c}{\approx}\{\mathbf{v}_{j},\{\$_{j}\}_{j}\}.

The security requirement in this model is that the adversary cannot distinguish between the ciphertext of
\mathbf{u}_{0}
and that of uniformly generated
\mathbf{u}_{1}
, even when it has access to the corresponding secret keys of its queries.

Our Assumption: Generalized Evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
.

We prove the static security of our construction as an
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
scheme under the standard
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption and the
\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption, which we formally introduce in Section
IV
. This assumption can be viewed as an extension of the evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
assumption proposed by
[
32
]
. We begin by informally recalling the assumption from
[
32
]
. Let
\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k}
be vectors generated by a public-coin sampler with public randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}
, and let
\mathbf{u}
be generated by a private-coin sampler. Let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{P}
be uniformly random matrices, and define

\mathbf{Q}:=\mathbf{P}[\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{1})\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{k})].

The assumption states that if the following
precondition
holds:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\uwave{\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{P},\uwave{\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{P}}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G},\uwave{\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}})\overset{c}{\approx}(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\$_{1},\mathbf{P},\$_{2}^{\prime},\$_{3}).

then the following
postcondition
also holds:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\uwave{\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{P},\uwave{\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{P}}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G},\mathbf{K})\overset{c}{\approx}(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\$_{1},\mathbf{P},\$_{2}^{\prime},\mathbf{K}),

where
\mathbf{K}\leftarrow\mathbf{A}^{-1}(\mathbf{Q})
and
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}
consists of all public randomness used by the samplers. The intuition behind this assumption is that the only way to use the matrix
\mathbf{K}
is via computing

\displaystyle\uwave{\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{K}\approx\uwave{\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}},

which is pseudorandom by the precondition, and hence rules out potential zeroizing attacks.

We now generalize this assumption in two stages:

1.

Introducing an auxiliary matrix
\mathbf{B}
.
Let
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k}
be the same as in the original assumption. Let
\mathbf{B}
be an additional uniformly random matrix, and let
\mathbf{r}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{r}_{k}
be generated by a public-coin algorithm. Let
\mathbf{Q}
be modified as

\mathbf{Q}=[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}]\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}\mathbf{r}_{1}&\cdots&\mathbf{r}_{k}\\
\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{1})&\cdots&\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{k})\end{array}\right].

The corresponding modified
precondition
becomes

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\uwave{\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{B},\uwave{\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{B}},\mathbf{P},\uwave{\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{P}}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G},\uwave{\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}})\overset{c}{\approx}(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\$_{1},\mathbf{B},\$_{2},\mathbf{P},\$_{2}’,\$_{3}).

and the
postcondition
is given as

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\uwave{\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{B},\uwave{\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{B}},\mathbf{P},\uwave{\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{P}}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G},\mathbf{K})\overset{c}{\approx}(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\$_{1},\mathbf{B},\$_{2},\mathbf{P},\$_{2}^{\prime},\mathbf{K}),

where
\mathbf{K}
is also sampled as
\mathbf{K}\leftarrow\mathbf{A}^{-1}(\mathbf{Q})
. The intuition behind this modified assumption remains similar to the original one.

2.

Generalized to the multi-instance setting.
In analogy to
[
44
]
, which modified the evasive
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption of
[
42
]
, we present a tailored variant of the evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
assumption
[
32
]
for our multi-authority setting. We can also extend the variant from Step 1) into our final
\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption. Specifically, for each index
i\in[\ell]
, let
\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{B}_{i},\mathbf{P}_{i}
be uniformly and independently random matrices. Let
\{\mathbf{v}_{i,j}\}_{i\in[\ell],j\in[k_{i}]},\{\mathbf{r}_{i,j}\}_{i\in[\ell],j\in[k_{i}]}
be vectors generated by a public-coin sampler with public randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}
, and let
\mathbf{u}
be generated by a private-coin sampler. Define the matrices
\mathbf{Q}_{i}
for each
i\in[\ell]
as:

\mathbf{Q}_{i}\leftarrow[\mathbf{B}_{i}\mid\mathbf{P}_{i}]\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}\mathbf{r}_{i,1}&\cdots&\mathbf{r}_{i,k_{i}}\\
\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{i,1})&\cdots&\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{i,k_{i}})\end{array}\right].

The
\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption states that if the following
precondition
holds:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\uwave{\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}}\},\{\mathbf{B}_{i}\},\uwave{\sum\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{i}},\{\mathbf{P}_{i}\},\uwave{\sum\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i}}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G},\{\uwave{\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}_{i}}\})\\
\overset{c}{\approx}\ &(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\$_{1,i}\},\hskip 8.00003pt\{\mathbf{B}_{i}\},\hskip 18.00005pt\$_{2},\hskip 11.00008pt\{\mathbf{P}_{i}\},\hskip 30.00005pt\$_{2}^{\prime},\hskip 30.00005pt\{\$_{3,i}\}),\end{split}

(2)

then the
postcondition

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\uwave{\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}}\},\{\mathbf{B}_{i}\},\uwave{\sum\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{i}},\{\mathbf{P}_{i}\},\uwave{\sum\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i}}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G},\{\mathbf{K}_{i}\})\\
\overset{c}{\approx}\ &(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\$_{1,i}\},\hskip 8.00003pt\{\mathbf{B}_{i}\},\hskip 18.00005pt\$_{2},\hskip 11.00008pt\{\mathbf{P}_{i}\},\hskip 30.00005pt\$_{2}^{\prime},\hskip 30.00005pt\{\mathbf{K}_{i}\})\end{split}

(3)

also holds, where
\mathbf{K}_{i}\leftarrow\mathbf{A}_{i}^{-1}(\mathbf{Q}_{i})
. Informally, the
\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption is no stronger than the evasive
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption of
[
44
]
, as it admits a reduction from the latter. Details of the reduction are provided in Appendix
A
.

Based on this
\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption, we can establish the (weakened) static security as an
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
scheme. The matrices
\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{B}_{i},\mathbf{P}_{i}
in the assumption correspond to the public keys for the authorities. Each column of
\mathbf{Q}_{i}
is of the form
\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{i,j})+\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{r}_{i,j}
. Here,
\mathbf{v}_{i,j}
corresponds to the key vector in a secret-key query in the game, and
\mathbf{r}_{i,j}
is interpreted as the hash output of some tuple
(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})
in the scheme. By carefully designing the matrix, the columns of
\mathbf{K}_{i}
in the postcondition instance serve as the secret-key responses to the secret-key queries. Noting that each secret-key query satisfies either condition (a) or condition (b), we can show the constructed matrices satisfy the precondition (
2
). By the
\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption, we obtain that postcondition (
3
) holds, implying that the resulting ciphertext is pseudorandom. A complete formal proof is provided in Section
VI
.

Desired Static Security:
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
Scheme.

In the static security model, the adversary is required to commit to its full set of secret-key queries and challenge plaintexts in advance. A statically secure
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
scheme requires that no efficient adversary can distinguish the ciphertexts encrypting the challenge plaintexts with non-negligible advantage, even in the presence of corrupted authorities and access to the secret keys corresponding to its queries.

Our Assumption: Indistinguishability-Based Evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
.

We base the security of our construction on the
Indistinguishability-based Evasive

\mathsf{IPFE}
(
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
) assumption, which is a variant of the
\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption discussed earlier.

The
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption follows a structure similar to the
\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption introduced in this work. Specifically, for each index
i\in[\ell]
, let
\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{B}_{i},\mathbf{P}_{i}
be independent and uniformly random matrices. Let
\{\mathbf{v}_{i,j}\}_{i\in[\ell],j\in[k_{i}]},\{\mathbf{r}_{i,j}\}_{i\in[\ell],j\in[k_{i}]}
and
\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{u}_{1}
be vectors generated by a public-coin sampler with public randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}
. Define the matrices
\mathbf{Q}_{i}
for each
i\in[\ell]
as:

\mathbf{Q}_{i}\leftarrow[\mathbf{B}_{i}\mid\mathbf{P}_{i}]\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}\mathbf{r}_{i,1}&\cdots&\mathbf{r}_{i,k_{i}}\\
\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{i,1})&\cdots&\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{i,k_{i}})\end{array}\right].

The
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption states that if the following
precondition
holds:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\uwave{\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}}\},\{\mathbf{B}_{i}\},\uwave{\sum\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{i}},\{\mathbf{P}_{i}\},\uwave{\sum\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i}}+\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{G},\{\uwave{\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}_{i}}\})\\
\overset{c}{\approx}\ &(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\uwave{\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}}\},\{\mathbf{B}_{i}\},\uwave{\sum\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{i}},\{\mathbf{P}_{i}\},\uwave{\sum\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i}}+\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{G},\{\uwave{\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}_{i}}\}),\end{split}

(4)

then the following
postcondition

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\uwave{\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}}\},\{\mathbf{B}_{i}\},\uwave{\sum\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{i}},\{\mathbf{P}_{i}\},\uwave{\sum\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i}}+\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{G},\{\mathbf{K}_{i}\})\\
\overset{c}{\approx}\ &(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\uwave{\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}}\},\{\mathbf{B}_{i}\},\uwave{\sum\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{i}},\{\mathbf{P}_{i}\},\uwave{\sum\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i}}+\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{G},\{\mathbf{K}_{i}\}),\end{split}

(5)

where
\mathbf{K}_{i}\leftarrow\mathbf{A}_{i}^{-1}(\mathbf{Q}_{i})
.

However, we need to make some additional assumptions about the sampler for vectors
\mathbf{r}_{i,j}
and
\mathbf{v}_{i,j}
. In this assumption, each pair
(\mathbf{r}_{i,j},\mathbf{v}_{i,j})
is required to be nonzero to avoid the obvious attack against the assumption. For the justification of this setting, we refer to Section
4
for details.

Following a strategy similar to the
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
case, we can prove that our construction satisfies the desired
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
static security notion. In this setting, all secret-key queries are fully determined by the adversary and the challenger plaintexts
\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{u}_{1}
are chosen by the adversary. Using the same method as for the
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
scheme, we construct each matrix
\mathbf{Q}_{i}
based on the submitted secret-key queries. By carefully designing
\mathbf{Q}_{i}
, the resulting columns of each
\mathbf{K}_{i}
serve as valid responses to the corresponding secret-key queries. The restriction on secret-key queries to satisfy either condition (a) or (b) ensures that the parameters
\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{B}_{i},\mathbf{P}_{i},\mathbf{Q}_{i}
meet the precondition (
4
) of the assumption. Then by the postcondition (
5
) of the
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption, we obtain that the ciphertexts of
\mathbf{u}_{0}
and
\mathbf{u}_{1}
are indistinguishable, thereby establishing the desired security.

Removing the Noise: Noiseless
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
.

Finally, we construct a noiseless
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
scheme enabling computation of
exact
inner products rather than approximate values, by slightly modifying the scheme proposed in Section
II-A
. Specifically, we eliminate the additive error term in the approximate inner products during decryption. Unlike the scheme in Section
II-A
that operates over plaintext vectors in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, this modified construction operates over input vectors in the smaller domain
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}
with
p\ll q
.

The global setup, authority setup, and key generation algorithms remain unchanged. To support computation over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}
within a
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
-based lattice setting, we adopt the
modulus-switching function

\lceil\cdot\rfloor_{p\rightarrow q}
that maps elements from
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
to
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
: For a vector
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}
, we define the encoding function:

\lceil\mathbf{u}\rfloor_{p\rightarrow q}:\mathbf{u}\mapsto\left\lceil\frac{q}{p}\cdot\mathbf{u}\right\rfloor\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}.

Similarly, we can define the decoding function
\lceil\cdot\rfloor_{q\rightarrow p}
as the inverse mapping of
\lceil\cdot\rfloor_{p\rightarrow q}
. With
\lceil\cdot\rfloor_{p\rightarrow q}
, we can embed
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}
into
\lceil\mathbf{u}\rfloor_{p\rightarrow q}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
. The encryption algorithm proceeds as

\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\left(\{\uwave{\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}}\}_{i\in X},\uwave{\sum_{i\in X}\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{i}},\uwave{\sum_{i\in X}\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i}}+\lceil\mathbf{u}^{\top}\rfloor_{p\rightarrow q}\mathbf{G}\right),

where
\mathbf{s}_{i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
for each
i\in X
. On the decryption side, we can use the same decryption process over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
as in Section
II-A
, followed by a final application of
\lceil\cdot\rfloor_{q\rightarrow p}
to recover the inner-product result over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

This modification to the encryption and decryption procedures removes the approximation gap in the decryption procedure while still enabling fine-grained multi-authority access control. The resulting scheme, formally given in Construction
2
, is particularly well-suited for applications requiring deterministic, noise-free computation of inner products, and its security is established under the standard
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption and the
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption tailored for noiseless settings. A detailed description of this noiseless scheme and its security analysis can be found in Section
VIII
.

III
Preliminaries

III-A

Notations

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
denote the security parameter used throughout this paper. For a positive integer
n\in\mathbb{N}
, denote
[n]
as the set
\{k\in\mathbb{N}:1\leq k\leq n\}
. For a positive integer
q\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
denote the ring of integers modulo
q
. Throughout this paper, vectors are assumed to be column vectors by default. We use bold lower-case letters (e.g.,
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}
) for vectors and bold upper-case letters (e.g.,
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
) for matrices. Let
\mathbf{v}[i]
denote the
i
-th entry of the vector
\mathbf{v}
, and let
\mathbf{U}[i,j]
denote the
(i,j)
-entry of the matrix
\mathbf{U}
.
We write
\mathbf{0}_{n}
for the all-zero vector of length
n
, and
\mathbf{0}_{n\times m}
for the all-zero matrix of dimension
n\times m
. The
infinity norm
of a vector
\mathbf{v}
and the corresponding operator norm of a matrix
\mathbf{U}
are defined as:

\|\mathbf{v}\|=\max_{i}|\mathbf{v}[i]|,\ \|\mathbf{U}\|=\max_{i,j}|\mathbf{U}[i,j]|.

For two matrices

\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
of dimensions
n_{1}\times m_{1}
and
n_{2}\times m_{2}
, respectively, their
Kronecker product
is an
n_{1}n_{2}\times m_{1}m_{2}
matrix given by

\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathbf{A}[1,1]\mathbf{B}&\cdots&\mathbf{A}[1,m_{1}]\mathbf{B}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\mathbf{A}[n_{1},1]\mathbf{B}&\cdots&\mathbf{A}[n_{1},m_{1}]\mathbf{B}\end{array}\right].

A function
f(\lambda)
is called
negligible
if
f(\lambda)=O(\lambda^{-c})
for every constant
c>0
. We denote a negligible function of
\lambda
by
\negl(\lambda)
. A function
f(\lambda)
is called
polynomial
if
f(\lambda)=O(\lambda^{c})
for some constant
c>0
. We denote a polynomial function of
\lambda
by
\poly(\lambda)
. A function
f(\lambda)
is called
super-polynomial
, if
f(\lambda)=\omega(\lambda^{c})
for every constant
c>0
. We denote a super-polynomial function of
\lambda
by
\mathrm{superpoly}(\lambda)
. We say an event occurs with
overwhelming probability
if its probability is
1-\negl(\lambda)
. An algorithm is called
efficient
if it runs in probabilistic polynomial time in its input length, typically parameterized by the security parameter
\lambda
in this work.

We denote the sampling of an element
x
from a distribution
D
by
x\leftarrow D
, and the uniform sampling of an element
x
from a set
S
by
x\xleftarrow{\$}S
. For a distribution
D
and a positive integer
m
, we write
\mathbf{x}\leftarrow D^{m}
to denote an
m
-dimensional vector whose entries are independently sampled from
D
. More generally, for integers
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, we write
\mathbf{X}\leftarrow D^{m\times n}
to denote an
m\times n
matrix with independently sampled entries from
D
. For two distributions
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
, We use the notation
D_{1}\equiv D_{2}
to denote that the two distributions are identical.

We also define the
modulus-switching function
. For an element
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
and some positive integer
p
, we define a function mapping from
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
to
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
as

\lceil\cdot\rfloor_{q\rightarrow p}:x\mapsto\left\lceil\frac{p}{q}\cdot x\right\rfloor\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}.

This map is applied coefficient-wise when extended to vectors or matrices. Such functions are commonly used in cryptographic schemes for modulus compression and message encoding.

Indistinguishability.

For two distributions
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
over a discrete domain
\Omega
, the
statistical distance
between them is defined as

\mathrm{SD}(D_{1},D_{2})=(1/2)\cdot\sum_{\omega\in\Omega}|D_{1}(\omega)-D_{2}(\omega)|.

Let
D_{1}=\{D_{1,\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}}
and
D_{2}=\{D_{2,\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}}
be two ensembles of distributions parameterized by the security parameter
\lambda
. We say
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
are
statistically indistinguishable
if there exists a negligible function
\negl(\cdot)
such that for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathrm{SD}(D_{1,\lambda},D_{2,\lambda})\leq\negl(\lambda).

We say
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
are
computationally indistinguishable
if for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, for all
efficient
algorithms
\mathcal{A}
, there exists a negligible function
\negl(\cdot)
such that

\Pr[\mathcal{A}(1^{\lambda},x_{\lambda})=1:x_{\lambda}\leftarrow D_{1,\lambda}]-\Pr[\mathcal{A}(1^{\lambda},y_{\lambda})=1:y_{\lambda}\leftarrow D_{2,\lambda}]\leq\negl(\lambda).

We use the notations
\overset{s}{\approx}
and
\overset{c}{\approx}
to denote statistical and computational indistinguishability, respectively.

III-B

Lattice Preliminaries

We recall some basic concepts related to lattices.

Discrete Gaussians.
Let
D_{\mathbb{Z},\sigma}
represent the centered
discrete Gaussian distribution
over
\mathbb{Z}
with standard deviation
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
(e.g.,
[
12
]
). For a matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
and a vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, let
\mathbf{A}_{\sigma}^{-1}(\mathbf{v})
denote the distribution of a random variable
\mathbf{u}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\sigma}^{m}
conditioned on
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{v}\mod q
.
When
\mathbf{A}_{\sigma}^{-1}
is applied to a matrix, it is understood as being applied independently to each column of the matrix.

The following lemma (e.g.,
[
38
]
) shows that for a given Gaussian width parameter
\sigma=\sigma(\lambda)
, the probability of a vector drawn from the discrete Gaussian distribution having norm greater than
\sqrt{\lambda}\sigma
is negligible.

Lemma 4
.

Let
\lambda
be a security parameter and
\sigma=\sigma(\lambda)
be a Gaussian width parameter. Then for all polynomials
n=n(\lambda)
, there exists a negligible function
\negl(\lambda)
such that for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,

\Pr\left[\|\mathbf{v}\|>\sqrt{\lambda}\sigma:\mathbf{v}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\sigma}^{n}\right]=\negl(\lambda).

Smudging Lemma.
In the following, we present the standard smudging lemma, which formalizes the intuition that sufficiently large standard deviation can “smudge out” small perturbations, making the resulting distributions statistically indistinguishable.

Lemma 5

(
[
14
]
)
.

Let
\lambda
be a security parameter, and let
e\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfy
|e|\leq B
. Suppose that
\sigma\geq B\cdot\lambda^{\omega(1)}
. Then, the following two distributions are statistically indistinguishable:

\{z:z\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\sigma}\}\,\text{ and }\,\{z+e:z\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\sigma}\}.

Lattice Trapdoors.
Lattices with trapdoors are structured lattices that are computationally indistinguishable from randomly chosen lattices (without auxiliary information). However, they possess specific “trapdoors”—short bases or auxiliary structures—that enable efficient solutions to otherwise hard lattice problems such as preimage sampling for the
Short Integer Solution
(
\mathsf{SIS}
) function. In this work, we adopt the trapdoor frameworks outlined in
[
30
,
37
]
, following the formalization in
[
20
]
.

Lemma 6

(
[
30
,
37
]
)
.

Let
n,m,q
be lattice parameters. Then there exist two efficient algorithms (
\mathsf{TrapGen}
,
\mathsf{SamplePre}
) with the following syntax:

•

\mathsf{TrapGen}(1^{n},1^{m},q)\rightarrow(\mathbf{A},\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}})
: On input the lattice dimension
n
, number of samples
m
, modulus
q
, this randomized algorithm outputs a matrix
\mathbf{A}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
together with a trapdoor
\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}}
.

•

\mathsf{SamplePre}(\mathbf{A},\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{y},\sigma)\rightarrow\mathbf{x}
: On input (
\mathbf{A},\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}}
) from
\mathsf{TrapGen}
, a target vector
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and a Gaussian width parameter
\sigma
, this randomized algorithm outputs a vector
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
.

Moreover, there exists a polynomial
m_{0}=m_{0}(n,q)=O(\sqrt{n\log q})
such that for all
m\geq m_{0}
, the above algorithms satisfy the following properties:

•

Trapdoor distribution:
The matrix
\mathbf{A}
output by
\mathsf{TrapGen}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
is statistically close to a uniformly random matrix. Specifically, if
(\mathbf{A},\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{TrapGen}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
and
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
, then the statistical distance between the distributions of
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
is at most
2^{-n}
.

•

Preimage sampling:
Suppose
\tau=O(\sqrt{n\log q})
. Then for all
\sigma\geq\tau\cdot\omega(\sqrt{\log n})
and all target vectors
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, the statistical distance between the following two distributions is at most
2^{-n}
:

\{\mathbf{x}\leftarrow\mathsf{SamplePre}(\mathbf{A},\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{y},\sigma)\}\,\text{ and }\,\{\mathbf{x}\leftarrow\mathbf{A}_{\sigma}^{-1}(\mathbf{y})\}.

The Gadget Matrix.

Here we recall the definition of the
gadget matrix
introduced in
[
37
]
. For positive integers
n,q\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbf{g}^{\top}=(1,2,\ldots,2^{\lceil\log q\rceil-1})
denote the
gadget vector
. Define the
gadget matrix

\mathbf{G}_{n}=\mathbf{I}_{n}\otimes\mathbf{g}^{\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
, where
m=n\lceil\log q\rceil
. The inverse function
\mathbf{G}_{n}^{-1}
maps vectors in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
to their binary expansions in
\{0,1\}^{m}
. More precisely, for a vector
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, the output
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{G}_{n}^{-1}(\mathbf{x})
is given by

\mathbf{y}=(y_{1,0},\ldots,y_{1,\lceil\log q\rceil-1},\ldots,y_{n,0},\ldots,y_{n,\lceil\log q\rceil-1}),

where the sequence
(y_{i,0},\ldots,y_{i,\lceil\log q\rceil})
corresponds to the
\lceil\log q\rceil
bits of the binary representation of
\mathbf{x}[i]
. The function
\mathbf{G}_{n}^{-1}(\cdot)
extends naturally to matrices by applying it column-wise. It is straightforward to verify that
\mathbf{G}_{n}\cdot\mathbf{G}_{n}^{-1}(\mathbf{A})=\mathbf{A}\mod q
for any matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times t}
. For simplicity, we will omit the subscript when the parameter
n
is clear from context.

Preimage Sampling.
The following lemma describes a useful property of discrete Gaussian distributions. Specifically, it basically states that, given a uniformly random matrix
\mathbf{A}
with sufficiently many columns, two methods of generating an input-output pair
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x})
produce statistically indistinguishable distributions.

Lemma 7

(Preimage Sampling
[
30
]
)
.

Let
n,m,q\in\mathbb{N}
, where
q>2
is a prime. Then there exists polynomials
m_{0}(n,q)=O(\sqrt{n\log q})
and
\chi_{0}(n,q)=\sqrt{n\log q}\cdot\omega(\sqrt{\log n})
such that for all
m\geq m_{0}(n,q)
and
\chi\geq\chi_{0}(n,q)
, the following two distributions are statistically indistinguishable:

\{(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}):\mathbf{A}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\mathbf{x}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m}\}\,\text{ and }\,\{(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):\mathbf{A}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\mathbf{y}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n},\mathbf{x}\leftarrow\mathbf{A}_{\chi}^{-1}(\mathbf{y})\}.

III-C

\mathsf{LWE}
Assumption and Its Variants

Learning with Errors Assumption.
We review the Learning With Errors (
\mathsf{LWE}
) assumption and its variants used throughout this work.

Assumption 1

(Learning with Errors (
\mathsf{LWE}
)
[
39
]
)
.

Let
\lambda
be a security parameter and let
n=n(\lambda),m=m(\lambda),q=q(\lambda),\sigma=\sigma(\lambda)
. The decisional learning with errors assumption, denoted
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,m,q,\sigma}
, states that for
\mathbf{A}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\mathbf{s}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n},\mathbf{e}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\sigma}^{m}
, and
\bm{\delta}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
, the following two distributions are computationally indistinguishable:

(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{e}^{\top})\,\text{ and }\,(\mathbf{A},\bm{\delta}).

Assumption 2

(Flipped
\mathsf{LWE}

[
16
]
)
.

Let
\lambda
be a security parameter and let
k=k(\lambda),m=m(\lambda),q=q(\lambda),\chi=\chi(\lambda),\sigma=\sigma(\lambda)
. The flipped
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption, denoted
\mathsf{FlipLWE}_{k,m,q,\chi,\sigma}
, states that for
\mathbf{A}\xleftarrow{\$}D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{k\times m},\mathbf{s}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{k},\mathbf{e}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\sigma}^{m}
, and
\bm{\delta}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
, the following two distributions are computationally indistinguishable:

(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{e}^{\top})\,\text{ and }\,(\mathbf{A},\bm{\delta}^{\top}).

The following theorem establishes the hardness of the flipped
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption based on the standard
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption.

Theorem 8

(
[
16
]
)
.

Let
q
be an integer, and suppose
k\geq 6n\log q
and
\sigma=\Omega(\sqrt{n\log q})
. Then, if the standard
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,m,q,\chi}
assumption holds, we have that the assumption
\mathsf{FlipLWE}_{k,m,q,\sigma,\chi}
holds.

IV
Evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
Assumptions

IV-A

The Evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
Assumption

We first present a variant of evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
assumption introduced by
[
32
]
, with slight modifications tailored to our setting.

Assumption 3

(Evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
(
\mathsf{evIPFE}
) Assumption, adapted,
[
44
,
32
]
)
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
be a security parameter, and let
n,q,m=n\lceil\log q\rceil,\chi,\chi^{\prime}
be lattice parameters specified by
\lambda
. Denote
\mathsf{gp}=(1^{\lambda},q,1^{n},1^{m},1^{m^{\prime}},1^{\chi},1^{\chi^{\prime}})
. Let
\mathcal{S}=(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{v}},\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{u}})
be a pair of sampling algorithms with the following syntax:

•

\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{v}}(\mathsf{gp};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}})
: Given the global parameter
\mathsf{gp}
and randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}\in\{0,1\}^{*}
, the algorithm outputs parameters

1^{\ell},\{1^{k_{i}}\}_{i\in[\ell]},

as well as
k_{1}+k_{2}+\cdots+k_{\ell}
vector tuples

(\mathbf{r}_{1,1},\mathbf{v}_{1,1}),(\mathbf{r}_{1,2},\mathbf{v}_{1,2}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{1,k_{1}},\mathbf{v}_{1,k_{1}});\ldots;(\mathbf{r}_{\ell,1},\mathbf{v}_{\ell,1}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\ell,k_{\ell}},\mathbf{v}_{\ell,k_{\ell}}),

where
\mathbf{r}_{i,j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}},\mathbf{v}_{i,j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
for all
i\in[\ell],j\in[k_{i}]
.

•

\mathbf{u}\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathsf{gp},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pri}})
: Given the global parameter
\mathsf{gp}
, public randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}
used in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{v}}
, and private randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pri}}\in\{0,1\}^{*}
. It outputs a vector
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
.

For two adversaries
\mathcal{A}_{0}
and
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, we define their advantage functions as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A}_{0}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\left|\mathrm{Pr}\left[\mathcal{A}_{0}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}],\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top},\{\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]})=1\right]\right.

\displaystyle-\left.\mathrm{Pr}\left[\mathcal{A}_{0}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\bm{\delta}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}],\bm{\delta}_{2}^{\top},\{\bm{\delta}_{3,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]})=1\right]\right|;

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{\mathsf{Post}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\left|\mathrm{Pr}\left[\mathcal{A}_{1}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}],\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top},\{\mathbf{K}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell]})=1\right]\right.

\displaystyle-\left.\mathrm{Pr}\left[\mathcal{A}_{1}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\bm{\delta}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}],\bm{\delta}_{2}^{\top},\{\mathbf{K}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell]})=1\right]\right|;

where the parameters are sampled as follows:

•

\left(\begin{array}[]{l}1^{\ell},\{1^{k_{i}}\}_{i\in[\ell]};\\
(\mathbf{r}_{1,1},\mathbf{v}_{1,1}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{1,k_{1}},\mathbf{v}_{1,k_{1}});\\
\cdots\\
(\mathbf{r}_{\ell,1},\mathbf{v}_{\ell,1}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\ell,k_{\ell}},\mathbf{v}_{\ell,k_{\ell}})\end{array}\right)\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{v}}(\mathsf{gp};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}),\quad\mathbf{u}\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathsf{gp},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pri}}),

•

\mathbf{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{B}_{\ell}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m^{\prime}},\mathbf{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{P}_{\ell}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\mathbf{B}^{\top}\leftarrow[\mathbf{B}_{1}^{\top}\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{B}_{\ell}^{\top}],\mathbf{P}^{\top}\leftarrow[\mathbf{P}_{1}^{\top}\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{P}_{\ell}^{\top}],

•

\mathbf{Q}_{i}\leftarrow[\mathbf{B}_{i}\mid\mathbf{P}_{i}]\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}\mathbf{r}_{i,1}&\cdots&\mathbf{r}_{i,k_{i}}\\
\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{i,1})&\cdots&\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{i,k_{i}})\end{array}\right]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times k_{i}},

•

(\mathbf{A}_{1},\mathsf{td}_{1}),\ldots,(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathsf{td}_{\ell})\leftarrow\mathsf{TrapGen}(1^{n},1^{m},q),

•

\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{\ell}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n},\mathbf{s}^{\top}\leftarrow[\mathbf{s}_{1}^{\top}\mid\ldots\mid\mathbf{s}_{\ell}^{\top}]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell},

•

\mathbf{e}_{1,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m},\mathbf{e}_{3,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{k_{i}}\text{ for each }i\in[\ell],\mathbf{e}_{2}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m^{\prime}+m},

•

\bm{\delta}_{1,i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\bm{\delta}_{3,i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{k_{i}}\text{ for each }i\in[\ell],\bm{\delta}_{2}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}+m},

•

\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{1,i}^{\top},\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{\top}\text{ for each }i\in[\ell],\quad\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}^{\top}[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}+[\mathbf{0}\mid\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G}]
,

•

\mathbf{K}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{SamplePre}(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathbf{Q}_{i},\chi^{\prime})\text{ for each }i\in[\ell].

We say that the
\mathsf{evIPFE}_{n,m,m^{\prime},q,\chi,\chi^{\prime}}
assumption holds, if for all efficient samplers
\mathcal{S}
, the following implication holds: If there exists an efficient adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}
with a non-negligible advantage function
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{\mathsf{Post}}(\lambda)
, then there exists another efficient adversary
\mathcal{A}_{0}
with a non-negligible advantage function
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A}_{0}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}(\lambda)
.

Remark 9

(Relation to the Evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
Assumption in
[
32
]
)
.

Assumption
3
defined above originates from the evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
assumption introduced by
[
32
]
. Following the approach of
[
44
]
, which defines a variant of public-coin evasive
\mathsf{LWE}
based on the formulation in
[
42
]
by introducing multiple (i.e.,
\ell\geq 1
) independently and uniformly sampled matrices
\mathbf{A}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
, we apply an analogous modification to the original evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
assumption proposed in
[
32
]
.

In the following, we focus on the connection between Assumption
3
and the evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
assumption from
[
32
]
. Informally, the latter basically states if

\displaystyle(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\mathbf{z}_{1}^{\top},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G},\mathbf{z}_{3}^{\top})\overset{c}{\approx}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\bm{\delta}_{1}^{\top},\mathbf{P},\bm{\delta}_{2}^{\top},\bm{\delta}_{3}^{\top}),

(6)

where
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{d}
are generated by some sampler
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{v}},\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{u}})
, and the other parameters are sampled as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\mathbf{P}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times D},\mathbf{Q}\leftarrow\mathbf{P}[\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{1})\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{k})],

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n},\mathbf{e}_{1}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m},\mathbf{e}_{2}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{D},\mathbf{e}_{3}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{k},

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{1}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\top},\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{P}+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top},\mathbf{z}_{3}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top},

\displaystyle\bm{\delta}_{1}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\bm{\delta}_{2}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{D},\bm{\delta}_{3}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{k},

then the following postcondition holds

(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\mathbf{z}_{1}^{\top},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G},\mathbf{K})\overset{c}{\approx}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\bm{\delta}_{1}^{\top},\mathbf{P},\bm{\delta}_{2}^{\top},\mathbf{K}),

where
\mathbf{K}\leftarrow\mathbf{A}_{\chi^{\prime}}^{-1}(\mathbf{Q})
.

We now compare Assumption
3
with the version in
[
32
]
. On the one hand, when
\ell=1
, Assumption
3
generalizes original setting in
[
32
]
by incorporating an auxiliary matrix
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m^{\prime}}
and additional vectors
\mathbf{r}_{1,j}
. Specifically, if we set
m^{\prime}=0,d=n
and
D=m
, then Assumption
3
collapses to the formulation used in
[
32
]
.

On the other hand, when
\ell=1
, Assumption
3
can also be roughly viewed as a special case of
[
32
]
, instantiated with a restricted sampler
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
. Concretely, suppose
D=m^{\prime}+m
and we write the matrix
\mathbf{P}
as
[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}^{\prime}]
with
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m^{\prime}},\mathbf{P}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
. If the sampler
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{u}}
ensures that
\mathbf{u}
is always of the form
[\mathbf{0}_{m^{\prime}}^{\top}\mid\mathbf{u}^{\prime}]
for some
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
, then

\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{s}^{\top}[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}+[\mathbf{0}_{m^{\prime}}^{\top}\mid\mathbf{u}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}],

which aligns with the corresponding component in Assumption
3
. Finally, we explain the role of the
\mathbf{r}_{1,j}
components (note
\ell=1
) in Assumption
3
.
Roughly, we consider the term

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{3}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{P}[\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{1})\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{k})]+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{P}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G})[\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{1})\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{k})]-\mathbf{u}^{\top}[\mathbf{v}_{1}\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{v}_{k}]+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}

\displaystyle\overset{c}{\approx}\bm{\delta}_{2}^{\top}[\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{1})\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{k})]-\mathbf{u}^{\top}[\mathbf{v}_{1}\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{v}_{k}]+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}.

Since
\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k}
are public, and
\mathbf{z}_{3}^{\top}
includes the term
\mathbf{u}^{\top}[\mathbf{v}_{1}\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{v}_{k}]
masked with the noise
\mathbf{e}_{3}
, its distribution—conditioned on all other public values—is effectively determined by the inner products
\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{j}
and the added noise. In particular, if
\mathbf{u}
is constrained to have zero entries in its first
m^{\prime}
coordinates, then the first
m^{\prime}
entries of
\mathbf{v}_{j}
(corresponding to the
\mathbf{r}_{1,j}
components in Assumption
3
) have no influence on the distribution of
\mathbf{z}_{3}
. This explains why such components can be included in the assumption without affecting its security implications.

Remark 10

(Public-coin and private-coin sampler)
.

As justified by
[
32
]
, it is necessary for the sampling procedure of
\mathbf{u}
to be
private-coin
, in order to prevent trivial attacks where the adversary directly computes the inner-products
\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{j}
. In our setting, all other components—including the global parameters and the tuples
(\mathbf{r}_{i,j},\mathbf{v}_{i,j})
—are sampled using a
public-coin
process, thereby avoiding the obfuscation-based counterexamples (cf.
[
21
]
). Moreover, the public-coin nature of
(\mathbf{r}_{i,j},\mathbf{v}_{i,j})
ensures that these vectors can be reused across multiple experiments.

Remark 11

(Reduction from Evasive
\mathsf{LWE}
in
[
44
]
)
.

In Appendix
A
, we provide a reduction from the evasive
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption in
[
44
]
to our
\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption. Consequently, the security of our
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
construction (cf. Section
VI
) can be based on the standard
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption and the evasive
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption from
[
44
]
.

IV-B

The Indistinguishability-Based Evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
Assumption

In this section, we present an indistinguishability-based variant of the
\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption introduced in the previous subsection. This version refines the original assumption by replacing distributional indistinguishability with a two-challenge indistinguishability game, where an adversary is challenged to distinguish between two inner-product encodings corresponding to
\mathbf{u}_{0}
and
\mathbf{u}_{1}
.

Assumption 4

(Indistinguishability-Based Evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
(
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
) Assumption)
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
be a security parameter, and let
q,n,m=n\lceil\log q\rceil,m^{\prime},\chi,\chi^{\prime}
be lattice parameters specified by
\lambda
. Denote
\mathsf{gp}=(1^{\lambda},q,1^{n},1^{m},1^{m^{\prime}},1^{\chi},1^{\chi^{\prime}})
. Let
\mathcal{S}
be an algorithm defined as follows:

•

\mathcal{S}(\mathsf{gp};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}})
: Given the security parameter
\lambda
and public randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}\in\{0,1\}^{*}
, the algorithm outputs parameters

1^{\ell},\{1^{k_{i}}\}_{i\in[\ell]},

followed by
k_{1}+\cdots+k_{\ell}
vector tuples

(\mathbf{r}_{1,1},\mathbf{v}_{1,1}),(\mathbf{r}_{1,2},\mathbf{v}_{1,2}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{1,k_{1}},\mathbf{v}_{1,k_{1}});\ldots;(\mathbf{r}_{\ell,1},\mathbf{v}_{\ell,1}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\ell,k_{\ell}},\mathbf{v}_{\ell,k_{\ell}}),

where
\mathbf{r}_{i,j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}},\mathbf{v}_{i,j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and
(\mathbf{r}_{i,j},\mathbf{v}_{i,j})\neq(\mathbf{0}^{m^{\prime}},\mathbf{0}^{n})
for all
i\in[\ell],j\in[k_{i}]
. The output also includes two additional vectors
\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{u}_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
.

For two adversaries
\mathcal{A}_{0}
and
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, we define their respective advantage functions as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\left|\mathrm{Pr}\left[\mathcal{A}_{0}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}],\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}+[\mathbf{0}\mid\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{G}],\mathbf{z}_{3}^{\top})=1\right]\right.

\displaystyle-\left.\mathrm{Pr}\left[\mathcal{A}_{0}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}],\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}+[\mathbf{0}\mid\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{G}],\mathbf{z}_{3}^{\top})=1\right]\right|;

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{\mathsf{Post}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\left|\mathrm{Pr}\left[\mathcal{A}_{1}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}],\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}+[\mathbf{0}\mid\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{G}],\{\mathbf{K}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell]})=1\right]\right.

\displaystyle-\left.\mathrm{Pr}\left[\mathcal{A}_{1}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}],\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}+[\mathbf{0}\mid\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{G}],\{\mathbf{K}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell]})=1\right]\right|;

where the parameters are sampled as follows:

•

\left(\begin{array}[]{l}1^{\ell},\{1^{k_{i}}\}_{i\in[\ell]};\\
(\mathbf{r}_{1,1},\mathbf{v}_{1,1}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{1,k_{1}},\mathbf{v}_{1,k_{1}});\\
\cdots\\
(\mathbf{r}_{\ell,1},\mathbf{v}_{\ell,1}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\ell,k_{\ell}},\mathbf{v}_{\ell,k_{\ell}});\\
\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{u}_{1}\end{array}\right)\leftarrow\mathcal{S}(\mathsf{gp};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}),

•
