\mathbf{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{B}_{\ell}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m^{\prime}},\mathbf{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{P}_{\ell}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\mathbf{B}^{\top}\leftarrow[\mathbf{B}_{1}^{\top}\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{B}_{\ell}^{\top}],\mathbf{P}^{\top}\leftarrow[\mathbf{P}_{1}^{\top}\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{P}_{\ell}^{\top}],

•

\mathbf{Q}_{i}\leftarrow[\mathbf{B}_{i}\mid\mathbf{P}_{i}]\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}\mathbf{r}_{i,1}&\cdots&\mathbf{r}_{i,k_{i}}\\
\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{i,1})&\cdots&\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{i,k_{i}})\end{array}\right]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times k_{i}},

•

(\mathbf{A}_{1},\mathsf{td}_{1}),\ldots,(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathsf{td}_{\ell})\leftarrow\mathsf{TrapGen}(1^{n},1^{m},q),

•

\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{\ell}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n},\mathbf{s}^{\top}\leftarrow[\mathbf{s}_{1}^{\top}\mid\ldots\mid\mathbf{s}_{\ell}^{\top}]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell},

•

\mathbf{e}_{1,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m},\mathbf{e}_{3,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{k_{i}}\text{ for each }i\in[\ell],\mathbf{e}_{2}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m^{\prime}+m},

•

\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{1,i}^{\top},\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{\top}\text{ for each }i\in[\ell],\quad\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}^{\top}[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}
,

•

\mathbf{K}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{SamplePre}(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathbf{Q}_{i},\chi^{\prime})\text{ for each }i\in[\ell].

We say that the
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}_{n,m,m^{\prime},q,\chi,\chi^{\prime}}
assumption holds if, for every efficient sampler
\mathcal{S}
, the following implication holds: If there exists an efficient adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}
with non-negligible advantage function
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{\mathsf{Post}}(\lambda)
, then there exists another efficient adversary
\mathcal{A}_{0}
with non-negligible advantage function
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A}_{0}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}(\lambda)
.

Remark 12

(Public-coin Sampler)
.

In contrast to
Assumption
3
, Assumption
4
adopts a public-coin sampler. This design is justified by the fact that, in the indistinguishability-based setting, there is no need to keep the challenge vectors
\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{u}_{1}
private, since computing
\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{j}
and
\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{j}
does not help the adversary distinguish between the two challenge instances. Consequently, the two separate algorithms
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{u}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{v}}
in Assumption
3
can be unified into a single public-coin algorithm in this context. The adoption of the public-coin setting also helps to exclude obfuscation-based counterexamples, as discussed in
[
21
]
.

Remark 13
.

Assumption
4
can be viewed as an indistinguishability-based variant of Assumption
3
. However, it is important to emphasize that the evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
assumption introduced in
[
32
]
cannot be directly reformulated as an indistinguishability-style assumption. Specifically, even if the precondition

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\mathbf{z}_{1}^{\top},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{G},\mathbf{z}_{3}^{\top})\overset{c}{\approx}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\mathbf{z}_{1}^{\top},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{G},\mathbf{z}_{3}^{\top}),\end{split}

(7)

holds, where
\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{u}_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{d},\mathbf{V}=[\mathbf{v}_{1}\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{v}_{k}]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{d\times k}
are sampled by some algorithm
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{v}},\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{u}})
, and the remaining parameters are sampled as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\mathbf{P}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times D},\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{P}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{V})=\mathbf{P}[\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{1})\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{k})],

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n},\mathbf{e}_{1}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m},\mathbf{e}_{2}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{D},\mathbf{e}_{3}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{k},

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{1}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\top},\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{P}+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top},\mathbf{z}_{3}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top},

we cannot in general conclude the corresponding postcondition as in the pseudorandomness-version assumption, namely,

\displaystyle\begin{split}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\mathbf{z}_{1}^{\top},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{G},\mathbf{K})\overset{c}{\approx}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\mathbf{z}_{1}^{\top},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{G},\mathbf{K}),\end{split}

(8)

where
\mathbf{K}\leftarrow\mathbf{A}_{\chi^{\prime}}^{-1}(\mathbf{Q})
. To illustrate this, we consider a sampler
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
that always outputs the all-zero matrix
\mathbf{V}=\mathbf{0}_{d\times k}
. Informally, we then observe the following chain of distributions:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\mathbf{z}_{1}^{\top},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{G},\mathbf{z}_{3}^{\top})\\
\equiv\ &(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\mathbf{z}_{1}^{\top},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{G},(\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{G})\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{V})+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}-\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{V})-\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{V})\\
\overset{c}{\approx}\ &(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\bm{\delta}_{1}^{\top},\mathbf{P},\bm{\delta}_{2}^{\top},\bm{\delta}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{V})+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}-\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{V})-\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{V})\\
\approx\ &(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\bm{\delta}_{1}^{\top},\mathbf{P},\bm{\delta}_{2}^{\top},\bm{\delta}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{V})+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}-\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{V})-\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{V})\\
\overset{c}{\approx}\ &(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\mathbf{z}_{1}^{\top},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{G},(\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{G})\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{V})+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}-\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{V})-\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{V})\\
\equiv\ &(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{A},\mathbf{z}_{1}^{\top},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{G},\mathbf{z}_{3}^{\top}),\end{split}

which implies the precondition (
7
). However, in the postcondition, the presence of the low-norm matrix
\mathbf{K}
satisfying
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{P}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{V})
yields a short basis of the
q
-ary lattice
\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}:\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0}\bmod{q}\}
with high probability—a trapdoor for the matrix
\mathbf{A}
. This trapdoor enables the adversary to distinguish between
\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{G}
and
\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{G}
, in the postcondition, thereby breaking indistinguishability.

In contrast, Assumption
4
avoids this issue by defining the matrix
\mathbf{Q}_{i}
as

\mathbf{Q}_{i}\leftarrow[\mathbf{B}_{i}\mid\mathbf{P}_{i}]\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}\mathbf{r}_{i,1}&\cdots&\mathbf{r}_{i,k_{i}}\\
\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{i,1})&\cdots&\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{i,k_{i}})\end{array}\right]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times k_{i}},

Each column vector
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\mathbf{r}_{i,j}\\
\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{i,j})\end{smallmatrix}\right]
is selected to be a nonzero vector and independent of the uniformly random matrix
[\mathbf{B}_{i}\mid\mathbf{P}_{i}]
. As a result, the marginal distribution of each column of
\mathbf{Q}_{i}
is uniform. Thus, the zeroizing attack, for example, by computing

(\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{1,i}^{\top})\mathbf{K}_{i}\approx\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}_{i}

does not yield a valid equation over the integers. This design aligns with the idea of
[
42
]
when introducing the evasive
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption.

V
Multi-Authority Attribute-Based Inner Product Functional Encryption

In this section, we present and formalize the core definitions underlying our work. We begin by introducing the notion of multi-authority attribute-based (noisy) inner-product functional encryption (
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
), which generalizes previous
\mathsf{NIPFE}

[
7
,
11
]
frameworks to support noise-tolerant decryption and multi-authority access control. We then introduce a relaxed “evasive” variant, called multi-authority attribute-based evasive inner-product functional encryption (
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
), inspired by the evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
model of
[
32
]
, which captures a weaker security notion more suitable for certain lattice-based instantiations. Both schemes are formalized in the static security model, with a focus on subset policies for simplicity and clarity.

V-A

Multi-authority Attribute-based (Noisy) Inner-Product Functional Encryption (
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
)

We first introduce the concept of
monotone access structures

[
15
]
, which play a fundamental role in access control mechanisms and secret-sharing schemes. The monotonicity property captures the idea that adding participants to an authorized subset preserves its authorization status. In this context, subset policies can be regarded as a specific instance of monotone access structures.

Definition 14

(Access Structure,
[
15
]
)
.

Let
S
be a set and
2^{S}
be the power set of
S
, i.e., the collection of all subsets of
S
. An
access structure
on
S
is a set
\mathbb{A}\subseteq 2^{S}\setminus\{\varnothing\}
, consisting of some non-empty subsets of
S
. A subset
A\in 2^{S}
is called
authorized
if
A\in\mathbb{A}
, and
unauthorized
otherwise.

An access structure is called
monotone
if it satisfies the following condition: for all subsets
B,C\in 2^{S}
, if
B\in\mathbb{A}
and
B\subseteq C
, then
C\in\mathbb{A}
. In other words, adding more elements to an authorized subset does not invalidate its authorization.

In the following, we begin by presenting the definition of multi-authority attribute-based (noisy) inner-product functional encryption (
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
). For simplicity, we adopt the
small universe
setting, as described in
[
28
]
, where each authority controls a single attribute, following the convention of
[
40
,
26
]
. In this setting, an authority and its corresponding attribute can be considered equivalent to some extent. This definition naturally extends to a more general setting in which each authority can manage an arbitrary polynomial number of attributes (with respect to the security parameter), as outlined in
[
40
]
. Our definition extends the standard
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
model (e.g.,
[
28
]
) by allowing the decryption to yield a noisy approximation of the inner product. This reflects more realistic applications where exact reconstruction of the inner product is not required and allows for a broader range of lattice-based constructions. Formally, we define the following.

Definition 15

(
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
,
[
9
,
28
]
)
.

Let
\lambda
be the security parameter, and
n=n(\lambda),q=q(\lambda)
be lattice parameters. Let
\mathcal{AU}
denote the universe of authority identifiers, and
\mathcal{GID}
be the universe of global identifiers for users. Let
B_{0}=B_{0}(\lambda),B_{1}=B_{1}(\lambda)
be bounding values. A
(B_{0},B_{1})
-
multi-authority attribute-based (noisy) inner-product functional encryption scheme
for a class of policies
\mathcal{P}
(described by a monotone access structure on a subset of
\mathcal{AU}
) over the vector space
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
is defined as a tuple of efficient algorithms
\Pi_{\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}}=(\mathsf{GlobalSetup},\mathsf{AuthSetup},\mathsf{Keygen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
. These algorithms proceed as follows:

•

\mathsf{GlobalSetup}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow\mathsf{gp}
: The global setup algorithm takes as input the security parameter
\lambda
, and outputs the global parameters
\mathsf{gp}
. We assume that
\mathsf{gp}
specifies the description of
\mathcal{AU}
,
\mathcal{GID}
,
n
, and
q
.

•

\mathsf{AuthSetup}(\mathsf{gp},\mathsf{aid})\rightarrow(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}})
: The authority (local) setup algorithm takes as input the global parameters
\mathsf{gp}
and an authority identifier
\mathsf{aid}\in\mathcal{AU}
. It outputs a public key
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}
and a master secret key
\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}}
.

•

\mathsf{Keygen}(\mathsf{gp},\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})\rightarrow\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}
: The key generation algorithm takes as input the global parameters
\mathsf{gp}
, the public key
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}
, the authority’s master secret key
\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}}
, a user identifier
\mathsf{gid}\in\mathcal{GID}
, and a key vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
. It outputs a secret key
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}
associated with attribute
\mathsf{aid}
, user identifier
\mathsf{gid}
, and key vector
\mathbf{v}
.

•

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{gp},\mathbb{A},\{\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A},\mathbf{u})\rightarrow\mathsf{ct}
: The encryption algorithm takes as input the global parameters
\mathsf{gp}
, an access structure
\mathbb{A}\in\mathcal{P}
on a set of authorities
A\subseteq\mathcal{AU}
, the set of public keys
\{\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}\}
associated with authorities
\mathsf{aid}\in A
, and a plaintext vector
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
. It outputs a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
.

•

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{gp},\{\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v},\mathsf{ct})\rightarrow\Gamma
: The decryption algorithm takes as input the global parameters
\mathsf{gp}
, a collection of secret keys
\{\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\}
associated with authorities
\mathsf{aid}\in A
, a user identifier
\mathsf{gid}\in\mathcal{GID}
, and a key vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, and a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
. It outputs a value
\Gamma\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, corresponding to the (approximate) inner product of
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{v}
or a symbol
\perp
to indicate the failure of decryption.

Approximate Correctness.
A
(B_{0}(\lambda),B_{1}(\lambda))
-
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
scheme is said to be
correct
if, for every security parameter
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, every plaintext vector
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, every key vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, every global identifier
\mathsf{gid}\in\mathcal{GID}
, every set of authorities
A\subseteq\mathcal{AU}
, every access structure
\mathbb{A}\in\mathcal{P}
defined over
A
, and every subset of authorized authorities
A^{\prime}\in\mathbb{A}
, it holds that

\mathrm{Pr}\left[\Gamma=\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v}+e_{0}\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\mathsf{gp}\leftarrow\mathsf{GlobalSetup}(1^{\lambda});\\
\forall\mathsf{aid}\in A,(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}})\leftarrow\mathsf{AuthSetup}(\mathsf{gp},\mathsf{aid});\\
\forall\mathsf{aid}\in A^{\prime},\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Keygen}(\mathsf{gp},\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v});\\
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{gp},\mathbb{A},\{\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A},\mathbf{u});\\
\Gamma\leftarrow\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{gp},\{\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{\prime}},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v},\mathsf{ct});\\
e_{0}\in[-B_{0}(\lambda),B_{0}(\lambda)]\end{array}\right.\right]\geq 1-\negl(\lambda).

Intuitively, we adopt an approximate correctness guarantee: if the decryption is authorized, the decryption result
\Gamma
should recover the inner product
\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v}
up to a small additive noise term
e_{0}
, bounded in absolute value by
B_{0}(\lambda)
.

Static Security.
In this paper, we extend the static security model of
[
9
,
28
]
by incorporating a
noisy
setting. This model is adapted from the security model for
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABE}
in
[
40
]
and for noisy
\mathsf{IPFE}
in
[
11
]
. This model is formalized as a game between a challenger and an adversary. The term
static
refers to the restriction that the adversary must specify all of its queries at the beginning of the game.

For a security parameter
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the static security game between a challenger and an adversary
\mathcal{A}
for an
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
scheme as follows:

Global Setup:
The challenger runs
\mathsf{gp}\leftarrow\mathsf{GlobalSetup}(1^{\lambda})
to generate the global parameters and sends
\mathsf{gp}
to the adversary
\mathcal{A}
.

Adversary’s Queries:
The adversary specifies the following queries:

•

A set of corrupt authorities
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{AU}
, along with their respective public keys
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}
for each corrupt authority
\mathsf{aid}\in\mathcal{C}
.

•

A set of non-corrupt authorities
\mathcal{N}\subseteq\mathcal{AU}
, where
\mathcal{N}\cap\mathcal{C}=\varnothing
.

•

A set of secret-key queries
\mathcal{Q}=\{(\mathsf{gid},A,\mathbf{v})\}
where each query specifies a global identifier
\mathsf{gid}\in\mathcal{GID}
, a subset of non-corrupt authorities
A\subseteq\mathcal{N}
, and a key vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
. We assume without loss of generality that each pair

(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})
appears at most once in the query set
\mathcal{Q}
, even if associated with different authority subsets
A
. This avoids the adversary combining keys from multiple key responses to enable decryption that would otherwise be unauthorized.

•

A pair of plaintext vectors
\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{u}_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, a set of authorities
A^{*}\subseteq\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{N}
, and an access structure
\mathbb{A}\in\mathcal{P}
defined over
A^{*}
.

Challenger’s Replies:
The challenger first flips a fair coin
\beta\xleftarrow{\$}\{0,1\}
and generates key pairs
(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}})\leftarrow\mathsf{AuthSetup}(\mathsf{gp},\mathsf{aid})
for each
\mathsf{aid}\in\mathcal{N}
. It then responds to adversary
\mathcal{A}
as follows:

•

The public keys
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}
for each non-corrupt authority
\mathsf{aid}\in\mathcal{N}
.

•

The secret keys
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Keygen}(\mathsf{gp},\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})
for each secret-key query
(\mathsf{gid},A,\mathbf{v})
and each
\mathsf{aid}\in A
.

•

The challenge ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}_{\beta}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{gp},\mathbb{A},\{\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}},\mathbf{u}_{\beta})
.

Guess Phase:
The adversary outputs a guess
\beta^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
for the value of
\beta
.

The
advantage
of the adversary
\mathcal{A}
in this game is defined by

\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}(\lambda):=|\mathrm{Pr}[\beta=\beta^{\prime}]-1/2|.

Unlike the static security model of
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABE}
(e.g.,
[
26
,
44
]
), where the adversary is restricted to making only
unauthorized
secret-key queries, the static security model for
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
schemes—as considered in
[
9
,
28
]
—allows the adversary to ask secret-key queries that may potentially decrypt the challenge ciphertext and derive an approximate inner product. To account for this broader adversarial capability, we impose the following limitations on the adversary in the game of a
(B_{0}(\lambda),B_{1}(\lambda))
-
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
scheme:

Definition 16

(Admissible Adversary)
.

An adversary
\mathcal{A}
is said to be admissible for the
(B_{0},B_{1})
-
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
security game defined above if, the set
A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C}
is not authorized under the access structure
\mathbb{A}
, i.e.,
A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C}\notin\mathbb{A}
, and for every secret-key query
(\mathsf{gid},A,\mathbf{v})\in\mathcal{Q}
, at least one of the following conditions holds, ensuring either unauthorized access or functional indistinguishability:

•

Unauthorized access
:
(A\cup\mathcal{C})\cap A^{*}\notin\mathbb{A}
.

•

Functional indistinguishability
: The vector
\mathbf{v}
satisfies
\|(\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\|\leq B_{1}
. (This ensures that the adversary cannot gain useful distinguishing information from the inner product of the challenge plaintexts.)

Definition 17

(Static Security for an
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
scheme)
.

An
(B_{0},B_{1})
-
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
scheme is said to be
statically secure
if, for every efficient admissible adversary
\mathcal{A}
, there exists a negligible function
\negl(\cdot)
such that, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, the advantage of
\mathcal{A}
in the above security game is at most
\negl(\lambda)
.

Remark 18

(Noiseless
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
)
.

For a correct and statically secure
(B_{0},B_{1})
-
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
, it is natural to require that
B_{0}\geq B_{1}
holds. Otherwise, an admissible adversary could distinguish between the two challenge ciphertexts by querying an authorized secret key associated with a key vector
\mathbf{v}
, for which
(\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1})^{\top}\mathbf{v}=B_{1}>B_{0}
. In this scenario, the difference between the two inner products would not be sufficiently obscured by the lower noise level guaranteed by the decryption process, thereby compromising security.

In particular, when
B_{0}=B_{1}=0
, the
(B_{0},B_{1})
-
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
degenerates into a
noiseless

\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
scheme, or simply an
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
scheme for brevity. Specifically, in this case, the decryption process using authorized secret keys is expected to return the
exact
inner product (except with negligible probability). Meanwhile, in the security game, an
admissible
adversary is restricted to submitting secret-key queries that are either unauthorized or guaranteed to yield the
same
inner product when decrypting both challenge ciphertexts. In this case, this definition coincides with the standard definition of
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
as formulated in
[
9
,
28
]
.

Remark 19

(Static Security in the Random Oracle Model)
.

In this work, we analyze the static security of our
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
construction in the
random oracle model

[
17
]
, following the approach of
[
40
,
26
,
27
,
44
,
28
]
. In this setting, the hash function
\mathsf{H}
(modeled as a random oracle programmed by the challenger) is specified as part of the global parameters, and all parties in the scheme have access to this function.

During the static security game in the random oracle model, the adversary is allowed to submit the random oracle queries as part of its initial queries. It is further allowed to make additional random oracle queries during the phase in which the challenger provides its responses.
The challenger must answer all such queries consistently throughout the interaction.

\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
for subset policies.

In this paper, we primarily focus on constructing an
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
scheme for the class of subset policies. In this setting, the ciphertext is encrypted under a set of authorities (attributes)
A\subseteq\mathcal{AU}
, which uniquely determines the access structure under the subset policy setting. Consequently, the access structure

\mathbb{A}
does not need to be explicitly specified in the encryption algorithm, as the policy is implicitly defined by the set
A
. The decryption algorithm succeeds only if a user possesses secret keys associated with a set of authorities
B
such that
A\subseteq B
.

The flexibility of the subset policy model provides an efficient framework for expressing access control rules, making it well-suited for a wide range of applications that require dynamic access restrictions based on user roles or permissions. By combining the definition of the
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABE}
scheme for subset policies, as introduced in
[
44
]
, with the definition of
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
scheme in
[
9
,
28
]
, we formalize the following definition.

Definition 20

(
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
for subset policies)
.

Let
\lambda
be a security parameter and
n=n(\lambda),q=q(\lambda)
be lattice parameters. Let
\mathcal{AU}
be the universe of authority identifiers. Define the class of subset policies
\mathcal{P}
as

\mathcal{P}=\{\mathbb{A}:\mathbb{A}=\{B:B\supseteq A\}\text{ where }A\subseteq\mathcal{AU}\}.

An access structure
\mathbb{A}
for a subset policy is determined by the set
A\subseteq\mathcal{AU}
. For an
(B_{0}(\lambda),B_{1}(\lambda))
-
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
scheme
\Pi_{\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}}=\{\mathsf{GlobalSetup},\mathsf{AuthSetup},\mathsf{Keygen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec}\}
for subset policies over the vector space
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, we can omit the explicit specification of the access structure
\mathbb{A}
in the encryption algorithm, since it is implicitly defined by the set of public keys
\{\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A}
. Precisely, the encryption algorithm
\mathsf{Enc}
in Definition
15
can be simplified as follows (with other algorithms unchanged):

•

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{gp},\{\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A},\mathbf{u})\rightarrow\mathsf{ct}
: The encryption algorithm takes as input the global parameters
\mathsf{gp}
, the set of public keys
\{\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}\}
corresponding to authorities
\mathsf{aid}\in A
, and a plaintext vector
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
. It outputs a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
.

In the subset policy setting, admissibility (Definition
16
) of an adversary imposes specific structural constraints on secret-key queries. We now make this more explicit by classifying such queries into two types. Let
\mathcal{Q}=\{(\mathsf{gid},A,\mathbf{v})\}
denote the set of secret-key queries and let
A^{*}
denote the set of attributes related to the challenging ciphertext. According to Definition
16
, an admissible adversary is additionally required to satisfy
A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C}\subsetneqq A^{*}
, and each query
(\mathsf{gid},A,\mathbf{v})\in\mathcal{Q}
must satisfy at least one of the following conditions:

•

Unauthorized access
:
(A\cup\mathcal{C})\cap A^{*}\subsetneqq A^{*}
.

•

Functional indishtinguishability
:
\|(\mathbf{u}_{1}-\mathbf{u}_{0})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\|\leq B_{1}
.

To facilitate the analysis in our security proof, we classify secret-key queries into the following types:

•

Type I secret-key query
: A query satisfying
(A\cup\mathcal{C})\cap A^{*}\subsetneqq A^{*}
.

•

Type II secret-key query
: A query satisfying
(A\cup\mathcal{C})\cap A^{*}=A^{*}
and
\|(\mathbf{u}_{1}-\mathbf{u}_{0})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\|\leq B_{1}
.

It follows directly that each secret-key query must belong to one of these two types.

Remark 21
.

As mentioned in
[
44
]
, the
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABE}
scheme for subset policies implies an
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABE}
scheme for access structure supporting access structures defined by a polynomial-size conjunction or a
\mathsf{DNF}
formula. Similarly, our construction of the
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
scheme for subset policies can also be extended to an
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
scheme for access structure decided by conjunction or a
\mathsf{DNF}
formula by following an analogous argument.

V-B

Multi-authority Attribute-based Evasive Inner-product Functional Encryption (
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
)

Inspired by the evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
scheme introduced in
[
32
]
, we define the notion of a
multi-authority attribute-based evasive inner-product functional encryption
(
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
) scheme by relaxing the security requirement of the
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
scheme described above. In
[
32
]
, the evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
captures a “generic-model view” analogous to that of evasive
\mathsf{LWE}
. The authors consider the
static
(also known as
very selective

[
32
]
) security model with respect to samplers producing pseudorandom noisy inner-product outputs.

Following this approach, we consider a weakened static security game where the adversary is no longer required to fully specify the entire Type II secret-key queries or the challenge plaintexts. Instead, these components are jointly determined by a predefined sampler and the adversary. We formalize the definition of an
(B_{0},\chi_{s})
-
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
scheme accordingly, adopting most of the notations following the convention of
[
32
]
. The syntax of the scheme and its approximate correctness guarantee follow the definition of the
(B_{0},B_{1})
-
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
scheme given in Definition
15
, where the parameter
B_{0}
retains the same role of bounding the decryption noise. We omit restating this part in the
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
definition below for clarity. We formalize the static security model for
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
as follows.

Static security.
Let
\mathsf{Samp}=(\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}},\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{u}})
be two algorithms with the following syntax.

•

\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}}(1^{\lambda};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}})
: The algorithm takes as input the security parameter
\lambda
and public randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}
, and outputs a sequence of vectors
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{2}^{\prime},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
.

•

\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{u}}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pri}})
: The algorithm takes as input the security parameter
\lambda
, public randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}
, and private randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pri}}
, and outputs a single vector
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
.

For a security parameter
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the static security game between a challenger and an (admissible) adversary
\mathcal{A}
for an
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
scheme, parameterized by the sampler
\mathsf{Samp}=(\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}},\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{u}})
, as follows:

Global Setup:
The challenger runs
\mathsf{gp}\leftarrow\mathsf{GlobalSetup}(1^{\lambda})
to generate the global parameters and sends
\mathsf{gp}
to the adversary
\mathcal{A}
.

Adversary’s Queries:
The adversary specifies the following queries:

•

A set
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{AU}
of corrupt authorities, along with their respective public keys
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}
for each corrupt authority
\mathsf{aid}\in\mathcal{C}
.

•

A set
\mathcal{N}\subseteq\mathcal{AU}
of non-corrupt authorities, where
\mathcal{N}\cap\mathcal{C}=\varnothing
.

•

A set of authorities
A^{*}\subseteq\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{N}
such that
A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C}\subsetneqq A^{*}
.

•

A set of Type I secret-key queries
\mathcal{Q}=\{(\mathsf{gid},A,\mathbf{v})\}
, where
A\subseteq\mathcal{N}
and
(A\cup\mathcal{C})\cap A^{*}\subsetneqq A^{*}
.

•

A partial set of Type II secret-key queries
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{par}}^{\prime}=\{(\mathsf{gid}^{\prime},A^{\prime})\}
, where
A^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{N}
and
(A^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{C})\cap A^{*}=A^{*}
.

Challenger’s Replies:

Then the challenger proceeds as follows:

1.

The challenger first flips a fair coin
\beta\xleftarrow{\$}\{0,1\}
and generates key pairs
(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}})\leftarrow\mathsf{AuthSetup}(\mathsf{gp},\mathsf{aid})
for each
\mathsf{aid}\in\mathcal{N}
.

2.

The challenger samples
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}\xleftarrow{\$}\{0,1\}^{\kappa_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pri}}\xleftarrow{\$}\{0,1\}^{\kappa_{2}}
, where
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}
denotes the upper bounds of the random bits used by
\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}}
and
\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{u}}
, respectively. Then it computes

\displaystyle(\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{2}^{\prime},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}}(1^{\lambda};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{0}\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{u}}(1^{\lambda};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pri}}),\mathbf{u}_{1}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}.

Next, the challenger builds the full set of Type II queries

\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}=\{(\mathsf{gid}_{1}^{\prime},A_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathsf{gid}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},A_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime})\}.

Before detailing the responses, we make the following assumptions without loss of generality:

•

Each pair
(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})
appears at most once in
\mathcal{Q}
, for the same reason as in
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{AB(N)IPFE}
. Likewise, each pair
(\mathsf{gid}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime})
appears at most once in
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
, since two queries
(\mathsf{gid}^{\prime},A_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime})
and
(\mathsf{gid}^{\prime},A_{2}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime})
can, without loss of generality, be replaced by a single query
(\mathsf{gid}^{\prime},A_{1}^{\prime}\cup A_{2}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime})
.

•

Each pair
(\mathsf{gid}_{j}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{par}}^{\prime}
does not appear in the Type I secret-key queries set
\mathcal{Q}
. If a pair
(\mathsf{gid}_{j}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})
appears in both query sets, it can be safely removed from
\mathcal{Q}
. This is because the challenger will already return the secret keys associated with all authorities in
A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}
—these cover all the authorities in any Type I query involving
(\mathsf{gid}_{j}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})
. As such, the Type I secret-key would be redundant.

•

The partial set of Type II queries
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{par}}^{\prime}
is assumed to contain exactly
Q^{\prime}
elements, matching the number of vectors output by the sampler
\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}}
. If necessary, the set
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{par}}^{\prime}
can be padded or truncated to ensure that its size matches
Q^{\prime}
, without affecting the adversary’s view or the underlying distribution.

3.

It then responds to the adversary
\mathcal{A}
as follows:

•

The public keys
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}
for each non-corrupt authorities
\mathsf{aid}\in\mathcal{N}
.

•

The secret keys
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Keygen}(\mathsf{gp},\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})
for each Type I secret-key query
(\mathsf{gid},A,\mathbf{v})\in\mathcal{Q}
and each
\mathsf{aid}\in A
.

•

The secret keys
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Keygen}(\mathsf{gp},\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{gid}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime})
for each Type II secret-key query
(\mathsf{gid}^{\prime},A^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
and each
\mathsf{aid}\in A^{\prime}
.

•

The challenge ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}_{\beta}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{gp},\{\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}},\mathbf{u}_{\beta})
.

•

The public randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}
.

Guess phase:
The adversary outputs a guess
\beta^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
for the value of
\beta
.

The
advantage
of the adversary
\mathcal{A}
in distinguishing
\beta
is defined as

\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{Samp}}(\lambda):=|\mathrm{Pr}[\beta=\beta^{\prime}]-1/2|.

Definition 22

(Static Security for an
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
scheme)
.

We say that the sampling algorithms
\mathsf{Samp}=(\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}},\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{u}})
in the game above produces
pseudorandom noisy inner products
with noise parameter
\sigma
, if

(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\prime}+e_{i}\}_{i\in[Q^{\prime}]})\overset{c}{\approx}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\delta_{i}\}_{i\in[Q^{\prime}]}),

where
e_{i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\sigma},\delta_{i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
for each
i\in[Q^{\prime}]
. An
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
scheme is said to be
\mathsf{Samp}
-
secure
if for every efficient admissible adversary
\mathcal{A}
, the advantage of
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{Samp}}(\lambda)
is negligible in
\lambda
. Furthermore, an
(B_{0},\chi_{s})
-
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
scheme is said to be
statically secure
if it is
\mathsf{Samp}
-secure for all efficient
\mathsf{Samp}
that produce pseudorandom noisy inner products with noise parameter
\sigma\leq\chi_{s}
.

Remark 23
.

As justified in
[
32
]
, to ensure indistinguishability in the static security game, it is necessary to make the sampling process of
\mathbf{u}_{0}
private-coin, meaning the distinguisher cannot trivially use randomness to obtain
\mathbf{u}_{0}
. Otherwise, the distinguisher may obtain
\mathbf{u}_{0}
and
\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\prime}
for
i\in[Q^{\prime}]
, and compute the corresponding inner products
\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\prime}
. This would allow distinguishing the challenge ciphertext and thus break the security of the scheme.

VI
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
Scheme from
\mathsf{evIPFE}
Assumption (in the Random Oracle Model)

In this section, we present our construction of the
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
scheme for subset policies in the random oracle model. The construction follows a structure similar to that in
[
26
,
44
,
32
]
. Later, we prove the static security of this scheme based on the
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption and
\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption, analogous to the approach introduced in
[
44
,
32
]
.

Construction 1

(
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
for subset policies in the random oracle model)
.

Let
\lambda
be the security parameter. Let
n
and
q
be lattice parameters, and define
m=n\lceil\log q\rceil
. Let
m^{\prime}
,
\chi
, and
\chi^{\prime}
be additional lattice parameters. Let
\mathcal{AU}=\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
denote the universe of authority identifiers, and let
\mathcal{GID}=\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
denote the universe of global identifiers for users. Let
\mathsf{H}:\mathcal{GID}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}}
be a hash function, modeled as a random oracle, similarly to Construction 5 in
[
44
]
. More precisely,

•

The outputs of the random oracle
\mathsf{H}
are modeled as following a discrete Gaussian distribution with parameter
\chi^{\prime}
. Specifically, on each input query
(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})\in\mathcal{GID}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, the output
\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})
is distributed according to discrete Gaussian
D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}
.

•

As noted in
[
44
]
, such a hash function
\mathsf{H}
can be instantiated using a standard random oracle
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}:\mathcal{GID}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda m^{\prime}}
, where the outputs of
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})
are uniformly distributed over
\{0,1\}^{\lambda m^{\prime}}
. According to Lemma 5.7 and Remark 5.9 of
[
44
]
, the outputs of
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}
can then be efficiently transformed into the desired discrete Gaussian distribution above using inversion sampling techniques.

A
(B_{0},\chi_{s})
-
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
scheme over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
for subset policies consists of a tuple of efficient algorithms
\Pi_{\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}}=(\mathsf{GlobalSetup},\mathsf{AuthSetup},\mathsf{Keygen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
. These algorithms proceed as follows:

•

\mathsf{GlobalSetup}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow\mathsf{gp}
: The global setup algorithm takes as input the security parameter
\lambda
, and outputs the global parameters
\mathsf{gp}=(\lambda,n,m,m^{\prime},q,\chi,\chi^{\prime},\mathsf{H})
.

•

\mathsf{AuthSetup}(\mathsf{gp},\mathsf{aid})\rightarrow(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}})
: The authority setup algorithm takes as input the global parameters
\mathsf{gp}
and an authority identifier
\mathsf{aid}\in\mathcal{AU}
. It samples
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{td}_{\mathsf{aid}})\leftarrow\mathsf{TrapGen}(1^{n},1^{m},q),\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m^{\prime}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
.
It outputs a public key
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}=(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}})
and a master secret key
\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}}=\mathsf{td}_{\mathsf{aid}}
.

•

\mathsf{Keygen}(\mathsf{gp},\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})\rightarrow\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}
: The key generation algorithm takes as input the global parameters
\mathsf{gp}
, the public key
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}=(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}})
, the authority’s master secret key
\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}}=\mathsf{td}_{\mathsf{aid}}
, the user identifier
\mathsf{gid}\in\mathcal{GID}
, the key vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, the key generation algorithm computes
\mathbf{r}\leftarrow\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})
and uses the trapdoor
\mathsf{td}_{\mathsf{aid}}
for
\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}}
to sample
\mathbf{k}\leftarrow\mathsf{SamplePre}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{td}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v})+\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{r},\chi)
. It outputs a secret key
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}=\mathbf{k}
.

•

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{gp},\{\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A},\mathbf{u})\rightarrow\mathsf{ct}
: The encryption algorithm takes as input the global parameters
\mathsf{gp}
, a set of public keys
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}=(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}})
associated with authorities
A\subseteq\mathcal{AU}
, a plaintext vector
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
. The encryption algorithm samples
\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n},\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m}
for each
\mathsf{aid}\in A
,
\mathbf{e}_{2}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m^{\prime}}
, and
\mathbf{e}_{3}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m}
. It outputs a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
, where

\mathsf{ct}=\left(\left\{\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\right\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A},\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A}\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top},\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A}\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\right).

•

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{gp},\{\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v},\mathsf{ct})\rightarrow\Gamma
: The decryption algorithm takes as input the global parameters
\mathsf{gp}
, a collection of secret keys
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}=\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}
associated with
authorities
\mathsf{aid}\in A
, a user identifier
\mathsf{gid}
, a key vector
\mathbf{v}
, and a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}=(\{\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A},\mathbf{c}_{2}^{\top},\mathbf{c}_{3}^{\top})
. The decryption algorithm first computes
\mathbf{r}\leftarrow\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})
and outputs

\Gamma=\mathbf{c}_{3}^{\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v})+\mathbf{c}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{r}-\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A}\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}.

VI-A

Correctness

Theorem 24

(Correctness)
.

Let
L
be an upper bound on the number of attributes associated with a ciphertext. Let
\chi_{0}=\sqrt{n\log q}\cdot\omega(\sqrt{\log n})
be a polynomial such that Lemma
7
holds. Suppose that the lattice parameters
n,q,\chi
are such that
\chi\geq\chi_{0}(n,q)
. Then the scheme
\Pi_{\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}}
in Construction
1
is
correct
as a
(B_{0},\chi_{s})
-
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
scheme, where the parameter
B_{0}=\sqrt{\lambda}\chi m+\lambda\chi\chi^{\prime}m^{\prime}+\lambda\chi^{2}mL.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
be any plaintext,
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
a key vector,
\mathsf{gid}\in\mathcal{GID}
a user identifier, and
A
a set of authorities. First, sample the global parameters
\mathsf{gp}\leftarrow\mathsf{GlobalSetup}(1^{\lambda})
, the authority key pairs
(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}})\leftarrow\mathsf{AuthSetup}(\mathsf{gp},\mathsf{aid})
for each
\mathsf{aid}\in A
, the secret keys
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Keygen}(\mathsf{gp},\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})
for each
\mathsf{aid}\in A
, and the ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{gp},\{\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A},\mathbf{u})
.

In the following, we verify the correctness by expanding the computation of the decryption process
\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{gp},\{\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v},\mathsf{ct})
in detail:

•

The global parameters
\mathsf{gp}=(\lambda,n,m,m^{\prime},q,\chi,\chi^{\prime},\mathsf{H})
consist of the security parameter, the lattice parameters, and the description of a hash function
\mathsf{H}:\mathcal{GID}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}}
.

•

For the plaintext vector
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, the ciphertext associated with authorities
\{\mathsf{aid}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A}
is constructed as
\left(\left\{\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\right\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A},\mathbf{c}_{2}^{\top},\mathbf{c}_{3}^{\top}\right)
, where

\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}=\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top},\mathbf{c}_{2}^{\top}=\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A}\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top},\mathbf{c}_{3}^{\top}=\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A}\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G},

where
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}})
is the public key associated with the authority
\mathsf{aid}\in A
.

•

Each secret key is generated as
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}=\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\leftarrow\mathsf{SamplePre}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{td}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v})+\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{r},\chi)
, where
\mathbf{r}\leftarrow\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})
. By Lemma
6
,
\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}
is distributed according to
D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m}
conditioned on

\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}=\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v})+\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{r}.

By Lemma
4
, it holds with overwhelming probability that
\|\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\|\leq\sqrt{\lambda}\chi
and
\|\mathbf{r}\|\leq\sqrt{\lambda}\chi^{\prime}
.

•

Using the secret key
\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}
, the decryption algorithm computes

\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}=\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}+\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}=\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v})+\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}

for each
\mathsf{aid}\in A
. Substituting the above expression into the decryption formula, we obtain:

\mathbf{c}_{3}^{\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v})+\mathbf{c}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{r}-\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A}\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}=\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v})+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{r}-\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A}\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}},

where the error term is

\tilde{e}:=\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v})+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{r}-\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A}\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}.

We conclude that the total error term
\tilde{e}
satisfies
|\tilde{e}|\leq B_{0}
, as required.

•

To bound
|\tilde{e}|
, we analyze each of its components: By Lemma
4
, the following bounds hold with overwhelming probability:

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}\|\leq\sqrt{\lambda}\chi,\quad\text{for each }\mathsf{aid}\in A,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{e}_{2}\|\leq\sqrt{\lambda}\chi,\quad\|\mathbf{e}_{3}\|\leq\sqrt{\lambda}\chi.

Using these bounds, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v})\|

\displaystyle\leq m\sqrt{\lambda}\chi,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{r}\|

\displaystyle\leq m^{\prime}\lambda\chi\chi^{\prime},

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\|

\displaystyle\leq m\lambda\chi^{2}.

Combining these results, we obtain the bound for the error term
\tilde{e}
:

\|\tilde{e}\|\leq\sqrt{\lambda}\chi m+\lambda\chi\chi^{\prime}m^{\prime}+\lambda\chi^{2}m\ell.

Thus, the scheme satisfies correctness, as claimed.

∎

VI-B

Security

Theorem 25
.

Let
\chi_{0}(n,q)=\sqrt{n\log q}\cdot\omega(\sqrt{\log n})
be a polynomial such that Lemma
7
holds. Let
Q_{0}
be the upper bound on the total number of secret-key queries (including those in the partial set) submitted by the adversary. Suppose that the following parameter conditions hold:

•

\chi^{\prime}=\Omega(\sqrt{n\log q})
.

•

Let
\chi
be an error distribution parameter such that
\chi>\chi_{0}
and
\chi\geq\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot(\sqrt{\lambda}(m+\ell)\chi_{s}+\lambda m\chi^{\prime}\chi_{s})
for all
\ell=\poly(\lambda)
, where
\chi_{s}
is a noise parameter such that
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,m_{1},q,\chi_{s}}
assumption holds for some
m_{1}=\poly(m,m^{\prime},Q_{0})
.

•

The assumption
\mathsf{evIPFE}_{n,m,m^{\prime},q,\chi,\chi}
holds.

Then Construction
1
is
statically secure
as a
(B_{0},\chi_{s})
-
\mathsf{evIPFE}
scheme.

Proof.

We prove the
static security
of Construction
1
by defining a sequence of hybrid experiments. We begin by fixing a public sampling algorithm tuple
\mathsf{Samp}=(\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}},\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{u}})
producing pseudorandom noisy inner products with noise parameter
\chi_{s}
.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{0}
.
This experiment corresponds to the real static security game, in which the challenger encrypts the plaintext
\mathbf{u}
sampled from
\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{u}}
, exactly as specified in Construction
1
. The experiment proceeds as follows.

At the beginning of the experiment, the adversary specifies the following queries:

•

A set of corrupt authorities
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{AU}
, along with their public keys:
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}=(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}})
for each
\mathsf{aid}\in\mathcal{C}
.

•

A set of non-corrupt authorities
\mathcal{N}\subseteq\mathcal{AU}
, satisfying
\mathcal{N}\cap\mathcal{C}=\varnothing
.

•

A set of authorities
A^{*}\subseteq\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{N}
, satisfying
(A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C})\subsetneqq A^{*}
.

•

A set of Type I secret-key queries
\mathcal{Q}=\{(\mathsf{gid},A,\mathbf{v})\}
, where
A\subseteq\mathcal{N}
and
(A\cup\mathcal{C})\cap A^{*}\subsetneqq A^{*}
.

•

A partial set of Type II secret-key queries
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{par}}^{\prime}=\{(\mathsf{gid}^{\prime},A^{\prime})\}
, where
A^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{N}
and
(A^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{C})\cap A^{*}=A^{*}
.

To simulate the random oracle, the challenger initializes an empty table
\mathsf{T}:\mathcal{GID}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}}
, which will be used to consistently store and respond to all random oracle queries throughout the experiment. Then the challenger with respect to samplers
\mathsf{Samp}=(\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}},\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{u}})
processes the adversary’s queries as follows.

•

Public keys for non-corrupt authorities:
For each non-corrupt authority
\mathsf{aid}\in\mathcal{N}
, the challenger samples
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{td}_{\mathsf{aid}})\leftarrow\mathsf{TrapGen}(1^{n},1^{m},q),\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m^{\prime}}
, and
\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
. The public key associated with
\mathsf{aid}
is then set as
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}\leftarrow(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}})
.

•

Secret-key queries:

The challenger handles secret-key queries in two types as follows:

–

Type I
: For each Type I secret-key query
(\mathsf{gid},A,\mathbf{v})\in\mathcal{Q}
, the challenger first computes
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\leftarrow\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})
, and samples

\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\leftarrow\mathsf{SamplePre}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{td}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v})+\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}},\chi)

for each
\mathsf{aid}\in A
. Then it sets the secret key as
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\leftarrow\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}
.

–

Type II
: The Type II secret-key queries are determined jointly by the partial set
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{par}}^{\prime}
and by the vectors
\{\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime}\}
output by the sampler
\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}}
. The challenger samples public randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}\xleftarrow{\$}\{0,1\}^{\kappa_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pri}}\xleftarrow{\$}\{0,1\}^{\kappa_{2}}
, where
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}
are the upper bounds of the random bits used by
\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}}
and
\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{u}}
, respectively. Then it samples

\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}}(1^{\lambda};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}})\text{ and }\mathbf{u}\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{u}}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pri}}).

The full set of Type II secret-key queries is then given by

\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}=\{(\mathsf{gid}_{1}^{\prime},A_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathsf{gid}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},A_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime})\}.

For each
(\mathsf{gid}^{\prime},A^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
, the challenger computes
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}\leftarrow\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{gid}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime})
, and samples

\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}\leftarrow\mathsf{SamplePre}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{td}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}^{\prime})+\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}},\chi)

for each
\mathsf{aid}\in A^{\prime}
. It then sets the secret key as
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}\leftarrow\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}
.

–

The challenger sends the public randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}
and all the secret keys generated above to the adversary.

•

Challenge ciphertext:
For each
\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}
, the challenger samples
\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and
\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m}
. Then, it samples
\mathbf{e}_{2}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m^{\prime}},\mathbf{e}_{3}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m}
. Finally, the challenge ciphertext is constructed as
\mathsf{ct}=\left(\{\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}},\mathbf{c}_{2}^{\top},\mathbf{c}_{3}^{\top}\right)
, where

\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}=\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top},\mathbf{c}_{2}^{\top}=\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}}\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top},\mathbf{c}_{3}^{\top}=\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}}\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G}.

•

Random oracle queries:
Upon receiving a query
(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})\in\mathcal{GID}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, the challenger checks whether the input
(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})
has been queried before—either during the adversary’s direct random oracle queries or implicitly through processing the secret-key queries. If so, the challenger retrieves and responds with the stored value
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}
from the table
\mathsf{T}
. If the input is new, the challenger samples
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m^{\prime}}
, records the mapping
(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})\mapsto\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}
in the table
\mathsf{T}
, and then replies with
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}
.

At the end of the experiment, the adversary outputs a bit
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
, which is taken as the output of the experiment.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{1}
.
This experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{0}
, except for how the challenger generates the challenge ciphertext.

•

For each
\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C}
, the challenger samples
\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and
\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m}
, and computes
\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}
. Then for each
\mathsf{aid}^{*}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}
, the challenger samples
\boxed{\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}^{*}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}}
. It also samples
\boxed{\mathbf{c}_{2}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}},\mathbf{c}_{3}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}}
. Finally, it outputs the challenge ciphertext

(\{\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}},\mathbf{c}_{2}^{\top},\mathbf{c}_{3}^{\top}).

Game
\mathsf{H}_{2}
.
This experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{1}
, except for how the challenger generates the third component of the challenge ciphertext. Specifically,

•

The challenger samples
\mathbf{u}_{\delta}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, and computes the challenge ciphertext:

(\{\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}},\mathbf{c}_{2}^{\top},\boxed{\mathbf{c}_{3}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}_{\delta}^{\top}\mathbf{G}}).

Game
\mathsf{H}_{3}
.
This experiment corresponds to the real static security game, in which the challenger encrypts the plaintext
\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{u}_{\delta}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, where
\mathbf{u}
is sampled from
\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{u}}
as in
\mathsf{H}_{1}
and
\mathbf{u}_{\delta}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
. Specifically, this experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{0}
, except that the embedded plaintext is shifted by
\mathbf{u}_{\delta}
.
In particular, the third component of the challenge ciphertext is computed as

\mathbf{c}_{3}^{\top}=\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}}\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}+(\mathbf{u}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}_{\delta}^{\top})\mathbf{G}.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{4}
.
This experiment corresponds to the real static security game, in which the challenger encrypts the uniformly random plaintext
\mathbf{u}_{\delta}
. Specifically, this experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{0}
, except that the embedded plaintext is
\mathbf{u}_{\delta}
.
In particular,

\mathbf{c}_{3}^{\top}=\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}}\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}+\boxed{\mathbf{u}_{\delta}^{\top}\mathbf{G}}.

Lemma 26
.

Let
Q_{0}
be the upper bound on the total number of secret-key queries (including those in the partial set
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{par}}^{\prime}
) submitted by the adversary. Suppose that the following parameter conditions hold:

•

m^{\prime}>6n\log q
.

•

\chi^{\prime}=\Omega(\sqrt{n\log q})
.

•

Let
\chi
be an error distribution parameter such that
\chi\geq\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot(\sqrt{\lambda}(m+\ell)\chi_{s}+\lambda m\chi^{\prime}\chi_{s})
, where
\chi_{s}
is a noise parameter such that
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,m_{1},q,\chi_{s}}
assumption holds for some
m_{1}=\poly(m,m^{\prime},Q_{0})
.

•

The assumption
\mathsf{evIPFE}_{n,m,m^{\prime},q,\chi,\chi}
holds.

Then we have that
\mathsf{H}_{0}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{1}
.

Proof.

Suppose that there exists an
efficient
adversary
\mathcal{A}
that distinguishes
\mathsf{H}_{0}
from
\mathsf{H}_{1}
with non-negligible advantage. Based on adversary
\mathcal{A}
, we define a pair of sampling algorithms
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}=(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{u}},\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{v}})
for global parameters
\mathsf{gp}=(1^{\lambda},q,1^{n},1^{m},1^{m^{\prime}},1^{\chi},1^{\chi})
, with respect to the
\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption, as follows.

•

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{v}}(\mathsf{gp};(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}}))
: This algorithm takes as inputs the global parameter
\mathsf{gp}
and public randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}}\in\{0,1\}^{*}
, and proceeds as follows.

–

Run adversary
\mathcal{A}(1^{\lambda};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}})
with randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}}
, and extract from its output: a set of Type I secret-key queries
\mathcal{Q}=\{(\mathsf{gid},A,\mathbf{v})\}
, a partial set of Type II secret-key queries
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{par}}^{\prime}=\{(\mathsf{gid}_{1}^{\prime},A_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathsf{gid}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},A_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime})\}
, and a set of ciphertext authorities
A^{*}=\{\mathsf{aid}_{1}^{*},\ldots,\mathsf{aid}_{\ell}^{*}\}
.

–

Denote
|\mathcal{Q}|=Q
, and index the queries as
(\mathsf{gid}_{1},A_{1},\mathbf{v}_{1}),\ldots,(\mathsf{gid}_{Q},A_{Q},\mathbf{v}_{Q})
. Then for each
i\in[\ell]
, let
N_{i}\in[Q]
denote the number of Type I secret-key queries in which the challenge authority
\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}
appears. Suppose that authority
\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}
is contained in the set
A_{j_{1}},\ldots,A_{j_{N_{i}}}
for some indices
j_{1},\ldots,j_{N_{i}}\in[Q]
, listed in increasing order. Define the mapping
\rho_{i}:[N_{i}]\rightarrow[Q]
by setting
\rho_{i}(k)=j_{k}
. That is,
\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}
appears exactly in the set
A_{\rho_{i}(1)},\ldots,A_{\rho_{i}(N_{i})}
with the indices ordered increasingly.

–

Run the sampler
\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}}(1^{\lambda};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}})
with randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}}
, which outputs the vectors
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}
.

–

For each
i\in[Q]
, sample
\mathbf{r}_{i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}
, and for each
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
, sample
\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}
.

–

Finally,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},{\mathbf{v}}}
outputs

\displaystyle 1^{\ell},\{1^{N_{i}+Q^{\prime}}\}_{i\in[\ell]};

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(N_{1})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(N_{1})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime});

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{2}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{2}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{2}(N_{2})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{2}(N_{2})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime});

\displaystyle\cdots;

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{\ell}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{\ell}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{\ell}(N_{\ell})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{\ell}(N_{\ell})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}).

The output is structured as shown above, where each row (excluding the first) corresponds to a challenge authority.

•

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{u}}(\mathsf{gp},(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}});\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pri}})
: takes as input the global parameter
\mathsf{gp}
, public randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}}
and private randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pri}}
. It computes
\mathbf{u}\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{u}}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pri}})
and outputs
\mathbf{u}
.

With respect to the sampling algorithm
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}
, We invoke Claim
27
below, and defer its proof to the end of this section.

Claim 27
.

Let
Q_{0}
be the upper bound on the total number of secret-key queries (including those in the partial set
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{par}}^{\prime}
) submitted by the adversary. Suppose that the following parameter conditions hold:

•

m^{\prime}>6n\log q
.

•

\chi^{\prime}=\Omega(\sqrt{n\log q})
.

•

Let
\chi
be an error distribution parameter such that
\chi\geq\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot(\sqrt{\lambda}(m+\ell)\chi_{s}+\lambda m\chi^{\prime}\chi_{s})
, where
\chi_{s}
is a noise parameter such that
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,m_{1},q,\chi_{s}}
assumption holds for some
m_{1}=\poly(m,m^{\prime},Q_{0})
.

•

The sampling algorithm
\mathsf{Samp}=(\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}},\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{u}})
produces pseudorandom noisy inner products with noise parameter
\chi_{s}
.

Then, for every efficient distinguisher
\mathcal{D}
, there exists a negligible function
\negl(\cdot)
such that for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we have
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}(\lambda)=\negl(\lambda)
, where
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
denotes the advantage of the distinguisher
\mathcal{D}
(as defined in Assumption
IV
) in the
\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption with respect to the sampling algorithm
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}=(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{v}},\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{u}})
.

Proof of Lemma
26
(Continued)
.
To complete the proof, we prove that if there exists an
efficient
adversary
\mathcal{A}
that can distinguish between
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1}
with non-negligible advantage, then we can construct an
efficient
algorithm
\mathcal{B}
such that
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{B}}^{\mathsf{Post}}
is non-negligible in the evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}
assumption with respect to the sampling algorithm
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}=(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{u}},\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{v}})
. The algorithm
\mathcal{B}
proceeds as follows:

1.

Algorithm
\mathcal{B}
begins by receiving an
\mathsf{evIPFE}
challenge

(1^{\lambda},(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}}),\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}],\mathbf{y}_{2}^{\top},\{\mathbf{K}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell]}),

where
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}}\in\{0,1\}^{*},\mathbf{A}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\mathbf{y}_{1,i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\mathbf{K}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m\times(N_{i}+Q^{\prime})}
for each
i\in[\ell]
,
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell\times m^{\prime}},\mathbf{P}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell\times m},\mathbf{y}_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}+m}
.

2.

For each
i\in[\ell]
, algorithm
\mathcal{B}
parses the matrix
\mathbf{K}_{i}
as

\mathbf{K}_{i}=[\mathbf{K}_{i}^{(1)}\mid\mathbf{K}_{i}^{(2)}],

where
\mathbf{K}_{i}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m\times N_{i}}
and
\mathbf{K}_{i}^{(2)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m\times Q^{\prime}}
. Let
\mathbf{k}_{i,j},\mathbf{k}_{i,j}^{\prime}
denote the
j
-th column vectors of
\mathbf{K}_{i}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{K}_{i}^{(2)}
, respectively.

3.

Algorithm
\mathcal{B}
runs algorithm
\mathcal{A}(1^{\lambda};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}})
, feeding in the randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}}
. The adversary
\mathcal{A}
outputs the following queries:

•

A set of corrupt authorities
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{AU}
, along with their public keys
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}=(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}})
for all
\mathsf{aid}\in\mathcal{C}
.

•

A set of non-corrupt authorities
\mathcal{N}\subseteq\mathcal{AU}
, satisfying
\mathcal{N}\cap\mathcal{C}=\varnothing
.

•

A ciphertext authority set
A^{*}\subseteq\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{N}
, satisfying
(A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C})\subsetneqq A^{*}
.

•

A set of Type I secret-key queries
\mathcal{Q}=\{(\mathsf{gid},A,\mathbf{v})\}
, where
A\subseteq\mathcal{N}
and
(A\cup\mathcal{C})\cap A^{*}\subsetneqq A^{*}
.

•

A partial set of Type II secret-key queries
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{par}}^{\prime}=\{(\mathsf{gid}^{\prime},A^{\prime})\}
, where
A^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{N}
and
(A^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{C})\cap A^{*}=A^{*}
.

4.

Algorithm
\mathcal{B}
runs sampling algorithm
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{v}}(\mathsf{gp};(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}}))
, which outputs

\displaystyle 1^{\ell},\{1^{N_{i}+Q^{\prime}}\}_{i\in[\ell]};

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(N_{1})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(N_{1})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime});

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{2}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{2}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{2}(N_{2})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{2}(N_{2})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime});

\displaystyle\cdots;

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{\ell}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{\ell}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{\ell}(N_{\ell})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{\ell}(N_{\ell})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}).

The full set of Type II secret-key queries is then given by
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}=\{(\mathsf{gid}_{1}^{\prime},A_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathsf{gid}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},A_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime})\}.

Since the randomness used to simulate
\mathcal{A}
in Step 3) comes from
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}}
, the values produced here—namely, the vectors
\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{Q}
—align exactly with those generated by
\mathcal{A}
’s simulation under the public randomness in Step 3). This ensures that algorithm
\mathcal{B}
’s internal simulation of
\mathcal{A}
is consistent with the actual input instance of the
\mathsf{evIPFE}
challenge.

5.

From the construction of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}
, we have that
\ell=|A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}|
, i.e., the number of non-corrupt authorities appearing in the challenge ciphertext. Let
A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}=\{\mathsf{aid}_{1}^{*},\ldots,\mathsf{aid}_{\ell}^{*}\}
. Algorithm
\mathcal{B}
first sets
\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}\leftarrow\mathbf{A}_{i}
for each
i\in[\ell]
, and parses
[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}]
as

[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}_{1}^{*}}&\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}_{1}^{*}}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}_{\ell}^{*}}&\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}_{\ell}^{*}}\end{array}\right]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell\times(m^{\prime}+m)},

where
\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m^{\prime}}
and
\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
for each
\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}
.

6.

Let
|\mathcal{Q}|=Q
and
|\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}|=Q^{\prime}
. For each
i\in[Q]
, algorithm
\mathcal{B}
partitions
A_{i}=A_{i,\mathsf{chal}}\cup\bar{A}_{i,\mathsf{chal}}\subseteq\mathcal{N}
, where
A_{i,\mathsf{chal}}
consists of the authorities in
A_{i}
that appear in the ciphertext, i.e.,
A_{i,\mathsf{chal}}=A_{i}\cap A^{*}
.

7.

Algorithm
\mathcal{B}
initializes an empty table
\mathsf{T}:\mathcal{GID}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}}
. This table will be used to store and consistently respond to all queries made to the random oracle during the experiment.

8.

The algorithm
\mathcal{B}
responds to the queries as follows:

•

Public keys for non-corrupt authorities
:

–

For each
\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}
, set
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}\leftarrow(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}},\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}})
.

–

For authorities
\mathsf{aid}\in\mathcal{N}\setminus A^{*}
, algorithm
\mathcal{B}
samples
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{td}_{\mathsf{aid}})\leftarrow\mathsf{TrapGen}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
,
\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m^{\prime}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
and sets the public key
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}\leftarrow(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}})
.

•

Secret keys
: The algorithm
\mathcal{B}
responds to each secret-key query depending on its type:

–

Type I
: For a Type I secret-key query
(\mathsf{gid}_{k},A_{k},\mathbf{v}_{k})
, recall that
A_{k}
is partitioned as
A_{k}=A_{k,\mathsf{chal}}\cup\bar{A}_{k,\mathsf{chal}}
, where
A_{k,\mathsf{chal}}=A_{k}\cap A^{*}
.

*

For each
\mathsf{aid}_{i^{*}}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}
, recall that the number of secret-key queries involving
\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}
is the parameter
N_{i}
given in the
\mathsf{evIPFE}
challenge. Let
\rho(\cdot)
be the index mapping previously defined in the proof of Lemma
26
. For each
j\in[N_{i}]
, set
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*},\mathsf{gid}_{\rho_{i}(j)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)}}\leftarrow\mathbf{k}_{i,j}
. Then the algorithm
\mathcal{B}
checks if the table
\mathsf{T}
has ever recorded the image of
(\mathsf{gid}_{\rho_{i}(j)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)})
, if not, store the mapping
(\mathsf{gid}_{\rho_{i}(j)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)})\mapsto\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i}(j)}
to the table.

*

For each
k\in[Q]
, if
A_{k,\mathsf{chal}}=\varnothing
, then sample
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid}_{k},\mathbf{v}_{k}}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}
, and add the mapping
(\mathsf{gid}_{k},\mathbf{v}_{k})\mapsto\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid}_{k},\mathbf{v}_{k}}
to the table. At this point, the table contains the image of all pairs
(\mathsf{gid}_{k},\mathbf{v}_{k})
for each
k\in[Q]
.

*

For each
k\in[Q]
, for each
\mathsf{aid}\in\bar{A}_{k,\mathsf{chal}}
, compute

\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid}_{k},\mathbf{v}_{k}}\leftarrow\mathsf{SamplePre}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{td}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{k})+\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{gid}_{k},\mathbf{v}_{k})),

where the value
\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{gid}_{k},\mathbf{v}_{k})
is retrieved from the table
\mathsf{T}
.

–

Type II
: For a Type II query
(\mathsf{gid}_{j}^{\prime},A_{j}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})
, recall that
A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}=\{\mathsf{aid}_{1}^{*},\ldots,\mathsf{aid}_{\ell}^{*}\}\subseteq A_{j}^{\prime}
.

*

For each
i\in[\ell],j\in[Q^{\prime}]
, set
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*},\mathsf{gid}_{j}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime}}\leftarrow\mathbf{k}_{i,j}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
. Next, algorithm
\mathcal{B}
adds the mapping
(\mathsf{gid}_{j}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})\mapsto\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}
to the table
\mathsf{T}
.

*

For each
\mathsf{aid}\in A_{j}^{\prime}\setminus A^{*}
, algorithm
\mathcal{B}
computes

\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid}_{j}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime}}\leftarrow\mathsf{SamplePre}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{td}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime},\chi)

efficiently
.

•

Challenge ciphertext
: Algorithm
\mathcal{B}
parses
\mathbf{y}_{2}
as
\mathbf{y}_{2}^{\top}=[\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{2}^{\top}\mid\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{3}^{\top}]
where
\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}},\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
. It constructs the ciphertext as follows:
For each
\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C}
, sample
\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and
\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m}
, then set
\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
. For
\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}
, set
\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}\leftarrow\mathbf{y}_{1,i}
. Finally, algorithm
\mathcal{B}
outputs the ciphertext

\mathsf{ct}=\left(\{\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}},\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C}}\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{2}^{\top},\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C}}\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{3}^{\top}\right).

•

Random oracle queries:
Upon receiving a query
(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})\in\mathcal{GID}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, algorithm
\mathcal{B}
checks whether the input
(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})
has been queried before—either during the adversary’s direct random oracle queries or implicitly through processing the secret-key queries. If so, algorithm
\mathcal{B}
retrieves and responds with the stored value
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}
from the table
\mathsf{T}
. If the input is new, the challenger samples
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m^{\prime}}
, records the mapping
(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})\mapsto\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}
in the table
\mathsf{T}
, and then replies with
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}
.

9.

Algorithm
\mathcal{B}
outputs whatever algorithm
\mathcal{A}
outputs.

The distributions of the public keys for non-corrupt authorities are exactly the same as those in
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1}
, as they are uniformly generated. We now analyze the responses to the secret-key queries:

•

For each
\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{k}_{i,j}

\displaystyle\leftarrow(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}})_{\chi}^{-1}(\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)})+\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i}(j)}),\quad\text{for all }j\in[N_{i}],

\displaystyle\mathbf{k}_{i,j}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leftarrow(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}})_{\chi}^{-1}(\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}),\quad\text{for all }j\in[Q^{\prime}].

This exactly matches the distribution of
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*},\mathsf{gid}_{\rho_{i}(j)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)}}
and
