\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*},\mathsf{gid}_{j}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime}}
in the actual game, respectively.

•

For each
\mathsf{aid}\in A_{j}\setminus A^{*}
for some
j\in[Q]
, the secret key
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid}_{j},\mathbf{v}_{j}}
is generated using
\mathsf{SamplePre}
, identical to the procedure in
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1}
. The same argument also applies to
\mathsf{aid}\in A_{j}^{\prime}\setminus A^{*}
for
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
.

Finally, we analyze the distribution of the challenge ciphertext. We consider the following two cases:

•

If for each
i\in[\ell]
,
\mathbf{y}_{1,i}^{\top}=\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{1,i}^{\top}
and
\mathbf{y}_{2}^{\top}=\mathbf{s}^{\top}[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}+[\mathbf{0}_{m^{\prime}}^{\top}\mid\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G}]
for some
\mathbf{s}^{\top}=[\mathbf{s}_{1}^{\top}\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{s}_{\ell}^{\top}]\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell},\mathbf{e}_{1,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m},\mathbf{e}_{2}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m^{\prime}+m}
. We parse
\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}
into two components as
[\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\top}\mid\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{3}^{\top}]
where
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}},\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}^{\top}=\mathbf{y}_{1,i}^{\top}=\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}+\mathbf{e}_{1,i}^{\top},\quad\text{ for each }\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N},

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{2}^{\top}=\sum_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}}\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}+\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\top},

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{3}^{\top}=\sum_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}}\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}+\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{3}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G}.

Then the ciphertext is constructed as

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}^{\top}=\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}+\mathbf{e}_{1,i}^{\top},\quad\text{ for each }\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N},

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}=\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top},\quad\text{ for each }\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C},

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{2}^{\top}=\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C}}\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\sum_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}}\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}+\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\top},

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{3}^{\top}=\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C}}\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\sum_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}}\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}+\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{3}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G},

where
\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and
\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m}
for each
\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C}
.
Since all randomness is sampled exactly as in
\mathsf{H}_{0}
, the resulting ciphertext generated by
\mathcal{B}
is identically distributed to that in the experiment
\mathsf{H}_{0}
.

•

If for each
i\in[\ell]
,
\mathbf{y}_{1,i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
, and
\mathbf{y}_{2}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}+m}
. Then from the construction, the ciphertext is constructed as

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}^{\top}=\mathbf{y}_{1,i}^{\top},\quad\text{ for each }\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N},

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}=\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top},\quad\text{ for each }\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C},

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{2}^{\top}=\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C}}\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{2}^{\top},

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{3}^{\top}=\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C}}\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{3}^{\top},

where
\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and
\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m}
for each
\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C}
. Since
\{\mathbf{y}_{1,i}\}_{i\in[\ell]},\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{2},\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{3}
are all uniform and independent of each other, the overall distribution of
\{\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}},\mathbf{c}_{2},\mathbf{c}_{3}
is also uniform, which matches the distribution in experiment
\mathsf{H}_{1}
.

In either case, the algorithm
\mathcal{B}
constructed perfectly simulates experiments
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1}
. The advantage
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{B}}^{\mathsf{Post}}
in the
\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption with respect to the sampling algorithm
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}
is the same as the non-negligible advantage of
\mathcal{A}
distinguishing between
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1}
. By the definition of the
\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption, the existence of such an efficient post-challenge adversary
\mathcal{B}
with non-negligible advantage would imply the existence of another efficient adversary
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
breaking the pre-challenge security, i.e.,
\mathsf{Adv}^{\mathsf{Pre}}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}
is non-negligible, contradicting Claim
27
. Hence, under the
\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption, no efficient adversary can distinguish between
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1}
with non-negligible advantage. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 28
.

We have that
\mathsf{H}_{1}\equiv\mathsf{H}_{2}
.

Proof.

The proof follows directly from the fact that in
\mathsf{H}_{2}
, the third component of the ciphertext is generated as
\mathbf{c}_{3}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}_{\delta}^{\top}\mathbf{G}
, where
\mathbf{u}_{\delta}
is sampled uniformly from
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and is independent of all other components. The resulting component remains uniform and thus identical to that in
\mathsf{H}_{1}
.
∎

Lemma 29
.

Under the same assumptions as in Lemma
26
, we have that
\mathsf{H}_{2}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{3}
.

Proof.

The proof follows essentially the same argument as in Lemma
26
.
∎

Lemma 30
.

We have that
\mathsf{H}_{3}\equiv\mathsf{H}_{4}
.

Proof.

The result follows directly from the fact that both
\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{u}_{\delta}
in
\mathsf{H}_{3}
and
\mathbf{u}_{\delta}
in
\mathsf{H}_{4}
are uniformly distributed over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, as
\mathbf{u}_{\delta}
is sampled uniformly and independently of
\mathbf{u}
. Hence,
\mathbf{c}_{3}^{\top}
is identically distributed in both experiments.
∎

Proof of Theorem
25
(Continued).
By Lemmas
26
,
28
,
29
, and
30
, and a standard hybrid argument, we conclude that under the given parameter constraints,
\mathsf{H}_{0}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{4}
. This implies that ciphertexts encrypting the vector
\mathbf{u}
generated by
\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}}
are computationally indistinguishable from those encrypting a uniformly random vector
\mathbf{u}_{\delta}
. Therefore, the theorem follows.
∎

To complete the proof of Theorem
25
, it remains to establish the validity of Claim
27
.

Proof of Claim
27
.

We prove the claim by defining a sequence of hybrid experiments.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

On input the security parameter
\lambda
, the challenger proceeds as follows:

1.

Let
\kappa=\kappa(\lambda)
be an upper bound on the number of random bits used by the adversary
\mathcal{A}
, and the sampling algorithms
\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{u}}
,
\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}}
. The challenger samples
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pri}}\xleftarrow{\$}\{0,1\}^{\kappa}
and run the sampling algorithms
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}=(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{v}},\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{u}})
as follows.

•

Run
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{v}}(\mathsf{gp};(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}}))
, which outputs

\displaystyle 1^{\ell},\{1^{N_{i}+Q^{\prime}}\}_{i\in[\ell]};

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(N_{1})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(N_{1})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime});

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{2}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{2}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{2}(N_{2})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{2}(N_{2})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime});

\displaystyle\cdots;

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{\ell}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{\ell}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{\ell}(N_{\ell})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{\ell}(N_{\ell})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}).

•

Run
\mathbf{u}\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{u}}(\mathsf{gp},(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}});\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pri}})
.

2.

Sample
\mathbf{B}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell\times m^{\prime}},\mathbf{P}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell\times m}
, and parse the matrices

\mathbf{B}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{B}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{B}_{\ell}\end{array}\right]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell\times m^{\prime}},\mathbf{P}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{P}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{P}_{\ell}\end{array}\right]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell\times m},

with
\mathbf{B}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m^{\prime}},\mathbf{P}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

3.

For each
i\in[\ell]
, define the matrix:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{(1)}\leftarrow\left[\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(1)})+\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i}(1)}\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(N_{i})})+\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i}(N_{i})}\right]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times N_{i}},

and similarly define:

\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{(2)}=\left[\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime})+\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime}\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime})+\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}\right]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times Q^{\prime}}.

Finally, set
\mathbf{Q}_{i}=[\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{(1)}\mid\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{(2)}]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times(N_{i}+Q^{\prime})}
. The construction of each matrix
\mathbf{Q}_{i}
follows exactly the format specified in Assumption
3
, ensuring consistency with the
\mathsf{evIPFE}
input distribution.

4.

Then the challenger samples
\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{\ell}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and sets
\mathbf{s}^{\top}=\left[\mathbf{s}_{1}^{\top}\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{s}_{\ell}^{\top}\right]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell}
. For each
i\in[\ell]
, it samples
\mathbf{e}_{1,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m},\mathbf{e}_{3,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{N_{i}+Q^{\prime}}
. Then it samples
\mathbf{e}_{2}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m^{\prime}+m}
.

5.

The challenger samples
(\mathbf{A}_{1},\mathsf{td}_{1}),\ldots,(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathsf{td}_{\ell})\leftarrow\mathsf{TrapGen}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
. For each
i\in[\ell]
, it computes the following values :

•

\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{1,i}^{\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
for each
i\in[\ell]
.

•

\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}=\mathbf{s}^{\top}[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}+[\mathbf{0}_{m^{\prime}}^{\top}\mid\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G}]=\left[\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{i}\,\Bigg|\,\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}+m}.

•

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{N_{i}+Q^{\prime}}
for each
i\in[\ell]
.

The component
\mathbf{z}_{3,i}
and
\mathbf{e}_{3,i}
are parsed as

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{\top}=[\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}\mid\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{N_{i}+Q^{\prime}},\quad\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{\top}=[\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}\mid\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{N_{i}+Q^{\prime}},

where
\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)},\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{N_{i}}
and
\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)},\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(2)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{Q^{\prime}}
. For clarity, define
\mathbf{t}_{i,j}=\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)})+\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i}(j)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and
y_{i,j}=\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{t}_{i,j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
for each
i\in[\ell]
and
j\in[N_{i}]
. Similarly define
\mathbf{t}_{i,j}^{\prime}=\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and
y_{i,j}^{\prime}=\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{t}_{i,j}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
for each
i\in[\ell]
and
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
.

In summary, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{(1)}+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}=\left[\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{t}_{i,1}\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{t}_{i,N_{i}}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}=\left[y_{i,1}\mid\cdots\mid y_{i,N_{i}}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{N_{i}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{(2)}+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}=\left[\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{t}_{i,1}^{\prime}\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{t}_{i,Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}=\left[y_{i,1}^{\prime}\mid\cdots\mid y_{i,Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{Q^{\prime}}.

6.

The challenger outputs the tuple
(1^{\lambda},(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}}),\{(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top})\}_{i\in[\ell]},[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}],\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top},\{\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]})
and sends it to the distinguisher
\mathcal{D}
.

7.

The distinguisher
\mathcal{D}
outputs a bit
\hat{b}\in\{0,1\}
, which is taken as the output of the experiment.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
The experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, except for the procedure how the challenger samples
\mathbf{z}_{1,i},\mathbf{z}_{2},\mathbf{z}_{3,i}
, which are now smudged with additional noise.

•

\mathbf{z}_{1,i}
: For each
i\in[\ell]
, sample
\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{1,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}^{m}
, and set
\boxed{\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{1,i}^{\top}+\mathbf{e}_{1,i}^{\top}}
.

•

\mathbf{z}_{2}
: For each
i\in[\ell]
, sample
\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}^{m^{\prime}}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\prime}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}^{m}
, and compute
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\top}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}
. Then set

\boxed{\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}\leftarrow\left[\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\,\Bigg|\,\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}=\left[\sum_{i\in[\ell]}(\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\top})\,\Bigg|\,\sum_{i\in[\ell]}(\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top})+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}}.

•

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)}
: For each
i\in[\ell]
and
j\in[N_{i}]
, compute

\displaystyle y_{i,j}

\displaystyle\leftarrow(\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top})\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)})+(\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\top})\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i}(j)}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i}(j)},

and set

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}\leftarrow\left[y_{i,1}\mid\cdots\mid y_{i,N_{i}}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{N_{i}}.

•

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)}
: For each
i\in[\ell]
and
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
, compute

\displaystyle y_{i,j}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leftarrow(\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top})\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+(\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\top})\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime},

and set

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}\leftarrow\left[y_{i,1}^{\prime}\mid\cdots\mid y_{i,Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{Q^{\prime}}.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
The experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, except for the procedure how the values
\mathbf{z}_{1,i},\mathbf{z}_{2},\mathbf{z}_{3,i}
are sampled.

•

\mathbf{z}_{1,i}
: For each
i\in[\ell]
, sample
\boxed{\mathbf{z}_{1,i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}}
.

•

\mathbf{z}_{2}
: For each
i\in[\ell]
, sample
\boxed{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}}}
and
\boxed{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}}
. Then set

\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}\leftarrow\left[\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\,\Bigg|\,\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}.

•

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)}
: For each
i\in[\ell]
and
j\in[N_{i}]
, compute

y_{i,j}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i}(j)},

then set

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}\leftarrow\left[y_{i,1}\mid\cdots\mid y_{i,N_{i}}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{N_{i}}.

•

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)}
: For each
i\in[\ell]
and
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
, compute

y_{i,j}^{\prime}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime},

then set

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}\leftarrow\left[y_{i,1}^{\prime}\mid\cdots\mid y_{i,Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{Q^{\prime}}.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
The experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, except it samples
\boxed{\mathbf{z}_{3,1}^{(1)}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{N_{1}}}
.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{4}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
The experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, except for how the components
\mathbf{z}_{2}
and
\mathbf{z}_{3,1}^{(2)}
are sampled.

•

\mathbf{z}_{2}
: For each
i\in[\ell]
, sample
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}},\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
. Then set

\boxed{\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}\leftarrow\left[\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\,\Bigg|\,\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}},

which omits the additive term
[\mathbf{0}_{m^{\prime}}\mid\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G}]
compared to the construction in
\mathsf{H}_{3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

•

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)}
: For
i=1
, sample
\boxed{\mathbf{z}_{3,1}^{(2)}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{Q^{\prime}}}
. For
i\neq 1
, sample
\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)}
in the same manner as in
\mathsf{H}_{3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{5}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
The experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{4}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, except it samples
\mathbf{z}_{2}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}+m}
,
\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{N_{i}},\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{Q^{\prime}}
for each
i\in[\ell]
.

Lemma 31
.

Suppose that
\chi\geq\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot(\sqrt{\lambda}(m+\ell)\chi_{s}+\lambda m^{\prime}\chi^{\prime}\chi_{s})
. Then we have
\mathsf{H}_{0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{s}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Proof.

The only difference between
\mathsf{H}_{0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
lies in the way the error terms in
\mathbf{z}_{1,i},\mathbf{z}_{2},\mathbf{z}_{3,i}
are generated. We analyze each of these components individually below:

•

Error term in
\mathbf{z}_{1,i}
: In experiment
\mathsf{H}_{1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, the error term in
\mathbf{z}_{1,i}
is given by
\mathbf{e}_{1,i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{1,i}
, where
\mathbf{e}_{1,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{1,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}^{m}
. By Lemma
4
, we have
\|\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{1,i}\|\leq\sqrt{\lambda}\chi_{s}
with overwhelming probability. Given that
\chi>\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot\sqrt{\lambda}\chi_{s}
, Lemma
5
implies that
\mathbf{e}_{1,i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{1,i}\overset{s}{\approx}\mathbf{e}_{1,i}
.

•

Error term in
\mathbf{z}_{2}
: In
\mathsf{H}_{1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, the error term in
\mathbf{z}_{2}
is given by

\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\top}:=\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}+\left[\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\top}\,\Bigg|\,\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\right],

where
\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}^{m^{\prime}}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\prime}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}^{m}
. Lemma
4
implies that
\|\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\|\leq\ell\cdot\sqrt{\lambda}\chi_{s}
with overwhelming probability. Given that
\chi>\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot\ell\sqrt{\lambda}\chi_{s}
, Lemma
5
ensures that
\mathbf{e}_{2}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\overset{s}{\approx}\mathbf{e}_{2}
. Hence, the distributions of
\mathbf{z}_{2}
in
\mathsf{H}_{0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
are statistically indistinguishable.

•

Error term in
\mathbf{z}_{3,i}
: In experiment
\mathsf{H}_{1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, the error term of
y_{i,j}
is given by

\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)})+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i}(j)}.

By Lemma
4
, the infinity norm of this term is at most
\sqrt{\lambda}m\chi_{s}+\lambda m^{\prime}\chi^{\prime}\chi_{s}
with overwhelming probability. Since
\chi>\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot(\sqrt{\lambda}m\chi_{s}+\lambda m^{\prime}\chi^{\prime}\chi_{s})
, Lemma
5
implies that the distributions of
\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)}
in the two experiments are statistically indistinguishable. The same argument applies to the component
\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)}
as well.

In conclusion, under the constraints of the stated parameters, all error terms in
\mathbf{z}_{1,i},\mathbf{z}_{2},\mathbf{z}_{3,i}

are statistically close in the two experiments. Hence, we have

\mathsf{H}_{0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{s}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
,
as claimed.
∎

Lemma 32
.

Suppose that the assumption
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,m_{1},q,\chi_{s}}
holds for
m_{1}=\poly(m,m^{\prime})
. Then we have
\mathsf{H}_{1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Proof.

We prove this lemma by defining a sequence of intermediate hybrid experiments
\mathsf{H}_{1,d}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
for each
0\leq d\leq\ell
.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{1,d}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
This experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, except for how the value
\mathbf{z}_{1,i},\mathbf{z}_{2},\mathbf{z}_{3,i}
are generated:

•

\mathbf{z}_{1,i}
:

–

For each
i\leq d
, sample
\mathbf{z}_{1,i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
.

–

For each
i>d
, sample
\mathbf{e}_{1,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m},\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{1,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}^{m}
, and set
\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{1,i}^{\top}+\mathbf{e}_{1,i}^{\top}
.

•

\mathbf{z}_{2}
:

–

For each
i\leq d
, sample
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
.

–

For each
i>d
, sample
\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}^{m^{\prime}},\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\prime}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}^{m}
, and set
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\top}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}
.

–

Then sample
\mathbf{e}_{2}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m^{\prime}+m}
, and set

\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}\leftarrow\left[\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\,\Bigg|\,\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}.

•

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)}
: For each
i\in[\ell]
, sample
\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(1)}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{N_{i}}
. Then for each
j\in[N_{i}]
, compute

y_{i,j}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i}(j)},

and let

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}\leftarrow\left[y_{i,1}\mid\cdots\mid y_{i,N_{i}}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{N_{i}}.

•

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)}
: Similarly, for each
i\in[\ell]
, sample
\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(2)}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{Q^{\prime}}
. Then for
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
, compute

y_{i,j}^{\prime}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime},

and let

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}\leftarrow\left[y_{i,1}^{\prime}\mid\cdots\mid y_{i,Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{Q^{\prime}}.

It follows naturally by the construction that
\mathsf{H}_{1,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\equiv\mathsf{H}_{1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, and
\mathsf{H}_{1,\ell}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\equiv\mathsf{H}_{2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
. We now demonstrate that, for each
d\in[\ell]
, the hybrids
\mathsf{H}_{1,d-1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1,d}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
are computationally indistinguishable under the
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,m_{1},q,\chi_{s}}
assumption for
m_{1}=\poly(m,m^{\prime})
.

Suppose, for contradiction, that there exists an efficient distinguisher
\mathcal{D}
that can distinguish between
\mathsf{H}_{1,d-1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1,d}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
with non-negligible advantage. We use
\mathcal{D}
to construct an adversary
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
that breaks the
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption. The adversary
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
proceeds as follows:

1.

Algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
begins by receiving an
\mathsf{LWE}
challenge
(\mathbf{D},\mathbf{y})
, where
\mathbf{D}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times(2m+m^{\prime})},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{2m+m^{\prime}}
. Then it parses
\mathbf{D}
and
\mathbf{y}
as

\mathbf{D}=[\mathbf{A}_{d}\mid\mathbf{B}_{d}\mid\mathbf{P}_{d}],\quad\mathbf{y}^{\top}=[\mathbf{y}_{1}^{\top}\mid\mathbf{y}_{2}^{\top}\mid\mathbf{y}_{2}^{\prime\top}],

where
\mathbf{A}_{d},\mathbf{P}_{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\mathbf{B}_{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m^{\prime}}
, and
\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\mathbf{y}_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}}
.

2.

Algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
simulates
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}=(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{v}},\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{u}})
as follows:

•

It samples
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pri}}\xleftarrow{\$}\{0,1\}^{\kappa}
as the randomness for algorithm
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

•

It computes

\left(\begin{array}[]{l}1^{\ell},1^{N_{i}+Q^{\prime}};\\
(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(N_{1})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(N_{1})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime});\\
(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{2}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{2}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{2}(N_{2})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{2}(N_{2})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime});\\
\cdots;\\
(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{\ell}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{\ell}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{\ell}(N_{\ell})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{\ell}(N_{\ell})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime})\end{array}\right)\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{v}}(\mathsf{gp};(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}})).

•

It computes
\mathbf{u}\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{u}}(\mathsf{gp},(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}});\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pri}})
.

3.

Algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
samples
\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{P}_{i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
and
\mathbf{B}_{i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m^{\prime}}
for each
i\neq d
. It also sets
\mathbf{A}_{d}
,
\mathbf{B}_{d}
, and
\mathbf{P}_{d}
to be the respective components provided in the
\mathsf{LWE}
challenge instance. Note that the distribution of the matrix
\{\mathbf{A}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell]},[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}]
constructed here exactly matches the setup process in the
\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption.

4.

Algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
constructs the remaining components
\mathbf{Q}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
as described in the
\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption.

5.

For each
i\in[\ell]
such that
i>d
, algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
samples
\mathbf{s}_{i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
. It then proceeds as follows:

•

\mathbf{z}_{1,i}
:

–

For each
i<d
, sample
\mathbf{z}_{1,i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
.

–

For
i=d
, sample
\mathbf{e}_{1,d}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m}
and compute
\mathbf{z}_{1,d}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{y}_{1}^{\top}+\mathbf{e}_{1,d}^{\top}
.

–

For each
i>d
, sample
\mathbf{e}_{1,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m},\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{1,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}^{m}
, and compute
\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{1,i}^{\top}+\mathbf{e}_{1,i}^{\top}
.

•

\mathbf{z}_{2}
:

–

For each
i<d
, sample
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}},\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
.

–

For
i=d
, set
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,d}\leftarrow\mathbf{y}_{2}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,d}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathbf{y}_{2}^{\prime}
.

–

For each
i>d
, sample
\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}^{m^{\prime}}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\prime}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}^{m}
, and compute
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\top}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}
. Then sample
\mathbf{e}_{2}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m^{\prime}+m}
and set

\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}\leftarrow\left[\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\,\Bigg|\,\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}.

•

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)},\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)}
: Sample
\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)},\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)}
via the same procedure as in
\mathsf{H}_{1,d}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

6.

Finally, algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
sends the challenge
\{1^{\lambda},(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}}),\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}],\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top},\{\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]}\}
to distinguisher
\mathcal{D}
and outputs whatever
\mathcal{D}
outputs.

We now analyze the distribution of the challenge given to
\mathcal{D}
. Note that the distributions of matrices
\mathbf{A}_{d},\mathbf{B}_{d},\mathbf{P}_{d}
match exactly those in the original game. Therefore, it suffices to analyze the distribution of vectors
\mathbf{z}_{1,i},\mathbf{z}_{2},\mathbf{z}_{3,i}
. Observe that for all
i\neq d
, the components
\mathbf{z}_{1,i},\mathbf{z}_{2},\mathbf{z}_{3,i}
are constructed identically in both
\mathsf{H}_{1,d-1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1,d}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
. Hence, we only need to analyze the case of
i=d
. We now distinguish between the two possible forms of the
\mathsf{LWE}
challenge, depending on whether the challenge is structured or uniformly random.

•

If
\mathbf{y}_{1}=\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{d}+\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\top},\mathbf{y}_{2}=\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{d}+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top},\mathbf{y}_{2}^{\prime}=\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{d}+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\prime\top}
for some
\mathbf{s}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n},\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\prime}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}^{m},\mathbf{e}_{2}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}^{m^{\prime}}
. In this case, the values
\mathbf{z}_{1,d},\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,d},\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,d}^{\prime}
are distributed identically to those in
\mathsf{H}_{1,d-1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

•

If
\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{2}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\mathbf{y}_{2}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}}
, then
\mathbf{z}_{1,d},\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,d},\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,d}^{\prime}
are sampled according to the same distributions as in
\mathsf{H}_{1,d}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Consequently,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
provides a perfect simulation of the distribution of the corresponding hybrid. As a result, algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
can distinguish the
\mathsf{LWE}
instance from the uniform instance with the same advantage as
\mathcal{D}
distinguishes between
\mathsf{H}_{1,d-1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1,d}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
. This leads to a contradiction with the
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption. We can conclude
\mathsf{H}_{1,d-1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{1,d}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
for each
d\in[\ell]
. By a standard hybrid argument, it follows that
\mathsf{H}_{1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, as desired.
∎

Lemma 33
.

Suppose
m^{\prime}>6n\log q
and
\chi^{\prime}=\Omega(\sqrt{n\log q})
. Let
\chi_{s}
be an error parameter such that
\chi\geq\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot\sqrt{\lambda}\chi_{s}
and the
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,m_{1},q,\chi_{s}}
assumption holds for
m_{1}=\poly(Q_{0})
, where
Q_{0}
is the upper bound on the total number of secret-key queries (including those in the partial set
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{par}}^{\prime}
) submitted by the adversary. Then we have that
\mathsf{H}_{2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Proof.

We prove this claim by defining a sequence of intermediate hybrid games between
\mathsf{H}_{2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
. For each
d\in[N_{1}]
, we define the following games:

Game
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
This experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, except for how the components
y_{1,j}
are sampled.

•

For each
j<d
, the challenger samples
\boxed{y_{1,j}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}}
.

•

For
j\geq d
, the challenger computes
y_{1,j}
exactly as in
\mathsf{H}_{2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, i.e.,
y_{1,j}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(j)})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(j)}
.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
This experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, except for how it samples the values
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}
. Most of the notation follows the convention used in
[
44
]
.

•

Denote
\gamma=\rho_{1}(d)
. Precisely,
\gamma
is the index of the
d
-th Type I secret-key query involving authority
\mathsf{aid}_{1}^{*}
.

•

We consider the
\gamma
-th Type I secret-key query
(\mathsf{gid}_{\gamma},A_{\gamma},\mathbf{v}_{\gamma})
submitted by the adversary
\mathcal{A}
. Since the restriction of the Type I secret-key query guarantees that
A_{\gamma}\subsetneqq A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}
, there exists some index
1<i^{*}\leq\ell
such that
\mathsf{aid}_{i^{*}}^{*}\notin A_{\gamma}
. Let
i^{*}
be the smallest such index. In particular,
\mathsf{aid}_{i^{*}}^{*}\notin A_{\gamma}
.

•

The challenger samples
\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}},\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,i^{*}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}}
. Then sample
\mathbf{s}_{0}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}}
, and compute
\boxed{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}\leftarrow\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}+\mathbf{s}_{0}}
,
\boxed{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}\leftarrow\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,i^{*}}-\mathbf{s}_{0}}
. For
i\notin\{1,i^{*}\}
, the challenger samples
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}}
, as in
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
. The components are then constructed as follows:

–

\mathbf{z}_{2}
: Set

\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}\leftarrow\left[\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\,\Bigg|\,\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}.

–

y_{i,j}
:
For
i=1
and
j<d
, sample
y_{1,j}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
. Otherwise (i.e., for
i\neq 1
or
j\geq d
), compute
y_{i,j}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i}(j)}
.

–

y_{i,j}^{\prime}
: For each
i\in[\ell]
and
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
, compute
y_{i,j}^{\prime}
as in
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, i.e.,
y_{i,j}^{\prime}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}
.

•

In particular, we have the affected components in the following:

–

For
i=1
and
d\leq j\leq N_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle y_{1,j}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(j)})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(j)}=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(j)})+\boxed{\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(j)}+\mathbf{s}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(j)}}.

–

For
i=1
and
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
, we have

y_{1,j}^{\prime}=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\boxed{\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}+\mathbf{s}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}}.

–

For
i=i^{*}
and
j\in[N_{i^{*}}]
, we have

y_{i^{*},j}=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)}=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)})+\boxed{\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)}-\mathbf{s}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)}}.

–

For
i=i^{*}
and
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
, we have that

y_{i^{*},j}^{\prime}=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\boxed{\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}-\mathbf{s}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}}.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
The experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, except for how
y_{i,j},y_{i,j}^{\prime}
are sampled. Specifically,

•

The challenger samples
\tilde{e}_{i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}
for each
i\in[Q]
, and
\tilde{e}_{j}^{\prime}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}
for each
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
.

•

For
i=1
and
j\geq d
, set

y_{1,j}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(j)})+\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(j)}+(\mathbf{s}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(j)}+\boxed{\tilde{e}_{\rho_{1}(j)}}).

•

For
i=1
and each
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
, set

y_{1,j}^{\prime}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}+(\mathbf{s}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}+\boxed{\tilde{e}_{j}^{\prime}}).

•

For
i=i^{*}
, set

\displaystyle y_{i^{*},j}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)})+\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)}-(\mathbf{s}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)}+\boxed{\tilde{e}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)}}),\quad\text{for all }j\in[N_{i^{*}}]

\displaystyle y_{i^{*},j}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}-(\mathbf{s}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}+\boxed{\tilde{e}_{j}^{\prime}}),\quad\text{for all }j\in[Q^{\prime}].

•

For all other pairs
(i,j)
, the components
y_{i,j},y_{i,j}^{\prime}
are computed in the same way as in
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
The experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, except for how
y_{i,j},y_{i,j}^{\prime}
are sampled. Specifically

•

The challenger samples
\boxed{\delta_{i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}}
for each
i\in[Q]
, and
\boxed{\delta_{j}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}}
for each
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
.

•

For
i=1
and
j\geq d
, set

y_{1,j}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(j)})+\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(j)}+\boxed{\delta_{\rho_{1}(j)}}.

•

For
i=1
and each
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
, set

y_{1,j}^{\prime}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}+\boxed{\delta_{j}^{\prime}}.

•

For
i=i^{*}
, set

\displaystyle y_{i^{*},j}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)})+\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)}+\boxed{\delta_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)}},\quad\text{for all }j\in[N_{i^{*}}],

\displaystyle y_{i^{*},j}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}+\boxed{\delta_{j}^{\prime}},\quad\text{for all }j\in[Q^{\prime}].

•

For all other pairs
(i,j)
, the components
y_{i,j},y_{i,j}^{\prime}
are computed in the same way as in
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,4}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
The experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
except it samples
\boxed{y_{1,d}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}}
.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,5}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
The experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,4}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, except for how the values
y_{i,j},y_{i,j}^{\prime}
are sampled. Specifically,

•

The challenger samples
\tilde{e}_{i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}
for each
i\in[Q]
, and
\tilde{e}_{j}^{\prime}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}
for each
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
.

•

For
i=1
and
j=d
, the challenger samples
y_{1,d}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
.

•

For
i=1
and
j>d
, set

y_{1,j}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(j)})+\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(j)}+\boxed{(\mathbf{s}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(j)}+\tilde{e}_{\rho_{1}(j)})}.

•

For
i=1
and each
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
, set

y_{1,j}^{\prime}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}+\boxed{(\mathbf{s}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}+\tilde{e}_{j}^{\prime})}.

•

For
i=i^{*}
, set

\displaystyle y_{i^{*},j}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)})+\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)}-\boxed{(\mathbf{s}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)}+\tilde{e}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)})},\quad\text{for all }j\in[N_{i^{*}}],

\displaystyle y_{i^{*},j}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}-\boxed{(\mathbf{s}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}+\tilde{e}_{j}^{\prime})},\quad\text{for all }j\in[Q^{\prime}].

•

For all other pairs
(i,j)
, the components
y_{i,j},y_{i,j}^{\prime}
are computed in the same way as in
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,4}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,6}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
This experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,5}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, except for how the values
y_{i,j},y_{i,j}^{\prime}
are sampled.

•

For
i=1
and
j>d
, set

\displaystyle y_{1,j}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(j)})+\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(j)}+\boxed{\mathbf{s}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(j)}}.

•

For
i=1
and each
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
, set

y_{1,j}^{\prime}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}+\boxed{\mathbf{s}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}}.

•

For
i=i^{*}
, set

\displaystyle y_{i^{*},j}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)})+\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)}+\boxed{\mathbf{s}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)}},\quad\text{for all }j\in[N_{i^{*}}],

\displaystyle y_{i^{*},j}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}+\boxed{\mathbf{s}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}},\quad\text{for all }j\in[Q^{\prime}].

•

For all other pairs
(i,j)
, the components
y_{i,j},y_{i,j}^{\prime}
are computed in the same way as in
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,5}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

In the following, we present the proof that each adjacent pair of these intermediate hybrid games defined above is indistinguishable for all
d\in[N_{1}]
. By the hybrid argument, this will imply that
\mathsf{H}_{2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Claim 34
.

We have
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\equiv\mathsf{H}_{2,d,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, for all
d\in[N_{1}]
.

Proof.

The only difference between the two experiments lies in the way the vectors
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}
are sampled. In
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, they are sampled independently and uniformly from
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}}
. In
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, they are constructed as

\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\top}\leftarrow\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}+\mathbf{s}_{0},\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\top}\leftarrow\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,i^{*}}-\mathbf{s}_{0},

where
\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1},\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,i^{*}},\mathbf{s}_{0}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}}
. It follows that the joint distribution of the pair
(\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1},\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}})
in experiment
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
is distributed identically to the pair sampled in
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Claim 35
.

Suppose that
\chi\geq\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot\sqrt{\lambda}\chi_{s}
. We have that
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{s}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{2,d,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
for all
d\in[N_{1}]
.

Proof.

The only difference between
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
lies in the modification of the error terms added to certain components
y_{i,j}
and
y_{i,j}^{\prime}
. We focus first on the error terms appearing in
\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)}
, as the reasoning for
\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)}
is analogous. Let
z_{3,i,j}^{(1)}
and
e_{3,i,j}^{(1)}
denote the
j
-th entries of
\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(1)}
, respectively. Then

•

In
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, for the affected components, we have

\displaystyle z_{3,1,j}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(j)})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(j)}+e_{3,1,j}^{(1)},\quad\text{for }j>d

\displaystyle z_{3,i^{*},j}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)}+e_{3,i^{*},j}^{(1)},\quad\text{for all }j\in[N_{i^{*}}].

•

In
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, the corresponding components are computed as:

\displaystyle z_{3,1,j}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(j)})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(j)}+e_{3,1,j}^{(1)}+\tilde{e}_{\rho_{1}(j)},\quad\text{for }j>d

\displaystyle z_{3,i^{*},j}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)}+e_{3,i^{*},j}^{(1)}-\tilde{e}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)}\quad\text{for all }j\in[N_{i^{*}}].

Since
\chi\geq\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot\sqrt{\lambda}\chi_{s}
, Lemma
4
ensures that
e_{3,1,j}^{(1)}+\tilde{e}_{\rho_{1}(j)}\overset{s}{\approx}e_{3,1,j}^{(1)}
, and similarly for
e_{3,i^{*},j}^{(1)}-\tilde{e}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)}\overset{s}{\approx}e_{3,i^{*},j}^{(1)}
.

For all other
(i,j)
, the distribution of
z_{3,i,j}^{(1)}
remains unchanged between the two experiments. Hence we can conclude that the distribution of
\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)}
is identical in both experiments for all
i\in[\ell]
. The same argument also applies to
\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)}
, as the additional error terms are smudged in an analogous manner. This completes the proof.
∎

Claim 36
.

Suppose that
m^{\prime}>6n\log q
and
\chi^{\prime}=\Omega(\sqrt{n\log q})
. Suppose the
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,m_{1},q,\chi_{s}}
assumption holds for some
m_{1}=\poly(Q_{0})
. Then we have that
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{2,d,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
for all
d\in[N_{1}]
.

Proof.

Assume for contradiction that there exists an efficient distinguisher
\mathcal{D}
that can distinguish between
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
with non-negligible advantage. We construct an algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
for the
\mathsf{FlipLWE}_{m^{\prime},m_{1},q,\chi^{\prime},\chi_{s}}
assumption. Algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
proceeds as follows:

1.

Algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
begins by receiving a
\mathsf{FlipLWE}
challenge
(\mathbf{D},\bm{\delta})
, where
\mathbf{D}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}\times(Q+Q^{\prime})}
and
\bm{\delta}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{Q+Q^{\prime}}
. Then algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
parses
\mathbf{D}
and
\bm{\delta}
as:

\mathbf{D}=[\mathbf{D}_{1}\mid\mathbf{D}_{1}^{\prime}],\quad\bm{\delta}^{\top}=[\bm{\delta}_{1}^{\top}\mid\bm{\delta}_{1}^{\prime\top}],

where
\mathbf{D}_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}\times Q},\mathbf{D}_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}\times Q^{\prime}}
, and
\bm{\delta}_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{Q},\bm{\delta}_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{Q^{\prime}}
. Let
\mathbf{d}_{j},\mathbf{d}_{j}^{\prime}
denote the
j
-th column vectors of
\mathbf{D}_{1},\mathbf{D}_{1}^{\prime}
, respectively. Let
\delta_{j},\delta_{j}^{\prime}
denote the
j
-th entries of
\bm{\delta}_{1},\bm{\delta}_{1}^{\prime}
, respectively.

2.

Algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
simulates
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{v}}
as follows:

•

It samples
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}}\xleftarrow{\$}\{0,1\}^{\kappa}
as the randomness for algorithm
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{v}}
.

•

Run algorithm
\mathcal{A}(1^{\lambda};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}})
with randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}}
, and extract from its output: a set of Type I secret-key queries
\mathcal{Q}=\{(\mathsf{gid},A,\mathbf{v})\}
and a partial set of Type II secret-key queries
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{par}}^{\prime}=\{(\mathsf{gid}^{\prime},A^{\prime})\}
.

•

Run
\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}}(1^{\lambda};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}})
with randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}}
. It outputs
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}
.

•

Finally, outputs

\displaystyle 1^{\ell},\{1^{N_{i}+Q^{\prime}}\}_{i\in[\ell]};

\displaystyle(\mathbf{d}_{\rho_{1}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{d}_{\rho_{1}(N_{1})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(N_{1})}),(\mathbf{d}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{d}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime});

\displaystyle(\mathbf{d}_{\rho_{2}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{2}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{d}_{\rho_{2}(N_{2})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{2}(N_{2})}),(\mathbf{d}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{d}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime});

\displaystyle\cdots;

\displaystyle(\mathbf{d}_{\rho_{\ell}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{\ell}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{d}_{\rho_{\ell}(N_{\ell})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{\ell}(N_{\ell})}),(\mathbf{d}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{d}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}).

The only difference between the simulation and the original specification of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{v}}
lies in how the vectors
\mathbf{r}_{i}
and
\mathbf{r}_{i}^{\prime}
are generated. In the simulation, the challenger replaces these vectors with
\mathbf{d}_{i},\mathbf{d}_{i}^{\prime}
which are the columns of the
\mathsf{FlipLWE}
matrix
\mathbf{D}
. However, since both
\mathbf{d}_{i},\mathbf{d}_{i}^{\prime}
are sampled from the same distribution
D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}
as the original
\mathbf{r}_{i},\mathbf{r}_{i}^{\prime}
, this substitution does not affect the validity of the simulation.

3.

Algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
samples
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pri}}\xleftarrow{\$}\{0,1\}^{\kappa}
and computes

\mathbf{u}\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{u}}(1^{\lambda},(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}});\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pri}})
.

4.

For each
i\in[\ell]
, it samples
\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{P}_{i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\mathbf{B}_{i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m^{\prime}}
. It then sets

[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}]\leftarrow\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}\mathbf{B}_{1}&\mathbf{P}_{1}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
\mathbf{B}_{\ell}&\mathbf{P}_{\ell}\end{array}\right]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell\times(m^{\prime}+m)}.

5.

Algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
samples
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}},\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
for each
i\in[\ell]
, and constructs the following components:

•

For
i=1,j<d
, sample
y_{1,j},y_{1,j}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
.

•

For
i=1,j\geq d
, compute

y_{1,j}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(j)})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{d}_{\rho_{1}(j)}+\delta_{\rho_{1}(j)}.

•

For
i=1,j\in[Q^{\prime}]
, compute

y_{1,j}^{\prime}=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{d}_{j}^{\prime}+\delta_{j}^{\prime}.

•

For
i=i^{*}
, compute

\displaystyle y_{i^{*},j}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\top}\mathbf{d}_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)}+\delta_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)},\quad\text{ for all }j\in[N_{i^{*}}],

\displaystyle y_{i^{*},j}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i^{*}}^{\top}\mathbf{d}_{j}^{\prime}+\delta_{j}^{\prime},\quad\text{ for all }j\in[Q^{\prime}].

•

For
i\notin\{1,i^{*}\}
, compute
y_{i,j}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\mathbf{d}_{\rho_{i}(j)}
and
y_{i,j}^{\prime}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\mathbf{d}_{j}^{\prime}
.

6.

Algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
constructs the components
\mathbf{z}_{1,i},\mathbf{z}_{2},\mathbf{z}_{3,i}
as follows:

•

\mathbf{z}_{1,i}
: For each
i\in[\ell]
, sample
\mathbf{z}_{1,i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
.

•

\mathbf{z}_{2}
: Sample
\mathbf{e}_{2}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m^{\prime}+m}
, and set

\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}\leftarrow\left[\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\,\Bigg|\,\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}.

•

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{\top}=[\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}\mid\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}]
: For each
i\in[\ell]
, sample
\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(1)}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{N_{i}},\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(2)}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{Q^{\prime}}
, and set

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}

\displaystyle\leftarrow[y_{i,1}\mid\cdots\mid y_{i,N_{i}}]+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(1)\top},

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}

\displaystyle\leftarrow[y^{\prime}_{i,1}\mid\cdots\mid y^{\prime}_{i,Q^{\prime}}]+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}.

7.

Finally, algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
sends the challenge
\{1^{\lambda},(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}}),(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top})_{i\in[\ell]},[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}],\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top},\{\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]}\}
to distinguisher
\mathcal{D}
and outputs whatever
\mathcal{D}
outputs.

By inspecting the construction of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, we can observe that the components

\{1^{\lambda},(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}}),(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top})_{i\in[\ell]},[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}]\}

are identically distributed in both experiments
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
. Therefore, it suffices to analyze the distributions of the components
\mathbf{z}_{2}
and
\mathbf{z}_{3,i}
in two experiments. We distinguish between the following two cases depending on the form of the flipped
\mathsf{LWE}
challenge:

•

If
\bm{\delta}^{\top}=\mathbf{s}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{D}+\mathbf{e}^{\top}
for some
\mathbf{s}_{0}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}},\mathbf{e}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}^{Q+Q^{\prime}}
, then by the construction procedure of
\mathbf{z}_{2}
and
\mathbf{z}_{3,i}
, the simulated distribution is identical to that in experiment
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

•

If
\bm{\delta}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{Q+Q^{\prime}}
, then the resulting distributions of
\mathbf{z}_{2},\mathbf{z}_{3,i}
are exactly the same as in experiment
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
. Hence the simulation is exactly as in experiment
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

In conclusion, distinguisher
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
constructed as above can distinguish flipped
\mathsf{LWE}
sample from uniform samples with the same advantage as
\mathcal{D}
distinguishes between
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
. However, by corollary
8
, under the assumption
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,m_{1},q,\chi_{s}}
and given parameter constraints, the assumption
\mathsf{FlipLWE}_{m^{\prime},m_{1},q,\chi^{\prime},\chi_{s}}
must hold. This leads to a contradiction, implying that
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{2,d,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, which completes the proof.
∎

Claim 37
.

We have that
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\equiv\mathsf{H}_{2,d,4}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
for all
d\in[N_{1}]
.

Proof.

The only difference between experiments
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,4}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
lies in the distribution of
y_{1,d}
. First, we argue that
\rho_{1}(d)\neq\rho_{i^{*}}(j)
for all
j\in[N_{i^{*}}]
. This follows from the construction of
i^{*}
. Recall
i^{*}
was defined as the smallest index such that
\mathsf{aid}_{i^{*}}\notin A_{\rho_{1}(d)}
. Since
\rho_{i^{*}}(j)
indexes secret-key queries that contain
\mathsf{aid}_{i^{*}}
, it follows that
A_{\rho_{i^{*}}(j)}\neq A_{\rho_{1}(d)}
for all
j\in[N_{i^{*}}]
. In particular,
\rho_{i^{*}}(j)\neq\rho_{1}(d)
for all
j\in[N_{i^{*}}]
. This implies that the only component that involves
\delta_{\rho_{1}(d)}
in experiment
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
is
y_{1,d}
. Since
\delta_{\rho_{1}(d)}
is sampled from the uniform distribution and is independent of other elements, the replacement of
y_{1,d}
with a uniformly random value in
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,4}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
does not affect the overall distribution of the experiment, completing the proof.
∎

Claim 38
.

Suppose that
m^{\prime}>6n\log q
and
\chi^{\prime}=\Omega(\sqrt{n\log q})
. Suppose the
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,m_{1},q,\chi_{s}}
assumption holds for some
m_{1}=\poly(Q_{0})
. Then we have that
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,4}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\equiv\mathsf{H}_{2,d,5}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
for all
d\in[N_{1}]
.

Proof.

The proof follows essentially the same argument as that of Claim
36
.
∎

Claim 39
.

Suppose that
\chi\geq\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot\sqrt{\lambda}\chi_{s}
. We have that
\mathsf{H}_{2,d,5}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{s}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{2,d,6}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
for all
d\in[N_{1}]
.

Proof.

The proof follows essentially the same argument as that Claim
35
.
∎

Claim 40
.

For all
d\in[N_{1}]
, we have
\mathsf{H}_{2,d-1,6}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\equiv\mathsf{H}_{2,d,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Proof.

The proof follows essentially the same argument as that of Claim
34
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
33
(Continued)
.
By Claims
34
through
40
, we obtain that for all
d\in[N_{1}]
,
\mathsf{H}_{2,d-1,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{2,d,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
for all
d\in[N_{1}]
. Applying a standard hybrid argument over the sequence of hybrids,
\mathsf{H}_{2,1,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{2,N_{1},0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
By definition of these hybrid experiments above,
\mathsf{H}_{2,1,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\equiv\mathsf{H}_{2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{2,N_{1},0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\equiv\mathsf{H}_{3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, which together yields
\mathsf{H}_{2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, as claimed.
∎

Lemma 41
.

Suppose that
\chi\geq\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot\sqrt{\lambda}\chi_{s}
and
\mathsf{Samp}=(\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}},\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{u}})
produces pseudorandom noisy inner product with noise parameter
\chi_{s}
. We have that
\mathsf{H}_{3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{4}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma by defining a sequence of hybrid experiments between
\mathsf{H}_{3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{4}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{3,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
: The experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, except for how it samples
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime}
. Specifically, the challenger first samples
\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
, and sets
\boxed{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime}\leftarrow\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\prime}-\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G}}
. This modification affects the computation of the following components:

•

\mathbf{z}_{2}
: We have

\boxed{\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}\leftarrow\left[\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\,\Bigg|\,\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\right]=\left[\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\,\Bigg|\,\ \tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}+\sum_{2\leq i\leq\ell}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\right]}.

•

y_{1,j}^{\prime}
: We have

y_{1,j}^{\prime}=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}=\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}-\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime}

for each
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{3,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
The experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{3,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, except for the procedure how it samples
y_{1,j}^{\prime}
. Specifically, the term
\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{j}
is perturbed by an additional noise term.

•

For each
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
, the challenger first samples
e_{j}\xleftarrow{\$}D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}
, and sets

y_{1,j}^{\prime}\leftarrow\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}-(\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime}+\boxed{e_{j}}).

Game
\mathsf{H}_{3,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
The experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{3,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
except for the procedure how it samples
y_{1,j}^{\prime}
.

•

For each
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
, the challenger first samples
\omega_{j}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, and sets

y_{1,j}^{\prime}\leftarrow\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}+\boxed{\omega_{j}}.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{3,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
The experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{3,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
except it samples
\boxed{y_{1,j}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}}
for each
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
.

From the constructions above, we first observe that
\mathsf{H}_{3,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\equiv\mathsf{H}_{3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
. The only difference between these two experiments lies in the way
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime}
is sampled, which is set as
\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\prime}-\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G}
in
\mathsf{H}_{3,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
. However, since
\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\prime}
is sampled uniformly from
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
, it follows that
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime}
remains uniformly distributed over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
and independent of all other components. Therefore, the joint distribution of all variables in
\mathsf{H}_{3,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
remains identical to that in
\mathsf{H}_{3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Similarly, we have that
\mathsf{H}_{3,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\equiv\mathsf{H}_{4}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
. This equivalence follows directly from the fact that the only symbolic change between the two experiments is the replacement of
\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\prime}
with
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime}
. Since both variables are sampled uniformly from
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
, this substitution does not affect the distribution of any component in the experiment. Since all values
y_{1,j}^{\prime}
are sampled uniformly at random in experiments
\mathsf{H}_{3,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{4}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, the overall distributions of the two experiments are identical.

In the following, we present the proof that each adjacent pair of these intermediate hybrids
\mathsf{H}_{3,i}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{3,i+1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
are indistinguishable for
i=0,1,2
. By applying a standard hybrid argument, these results yield that
\mathsf{H}_{3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{4}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, as desired.

Claim 42
.

Suppose that
\chi\geq\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot\chi_{s}
. Then we have that
\mathsf{H}_{3,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{s}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{3,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Proof.

The proof follows essentially the same noise smudging argument as that of Claim
35
.
∎

Claim 43
.

Suppose that the sampler
\mathsf{Samp}=(\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}},\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{u}})
produces pseudorandom noisy inner products with noise parameter
\chi_{s}
. Then we have
\mathsf{H}_{3,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{3,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Proof.

Assume for contradiction that there exists an efficient distinguisher
\mathcal{D}
that can distinguish
\mathsf{H}_{3,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{3,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
with non-negligible advantage. We use
\mathcal{D}
to construct an algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
that breaks the pseudorandomness of noisy inner products produced by the sampler
\mathsf{Samp}
.
Algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
proceeds as follows:

1.

Algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
begins by receiving a challenge instance
(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\beta_{j}\}_{j\in[Q^{\prime}]})
.

2.

Algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
simulates the sampler
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{v}}
as follows:

•

It samples
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}}\xleftarrow{\$}\{0,1\}^{\kappa}
as part of the randomness for algorithm
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{v}}
.

•

It runs algorithm
\mathcal{A}(1^{\lambda};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}})
with randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}}
, and extracts from its output: a set of Type I secret-key queries
\mathcal{Q}=\{(\mathsf{gid},A,\mathbf{v})\}
and a partial set of Type II secret-key queries
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{par}}^{\prime}=\{(\mathsf{gid}^{\prime},A^{\prime})\}
.

•

For each
i\in[Q]
, sample
\mathbf{r}_{i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}
, and for each
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
, sample
\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}
.

•

Run
\mathsf{Samp}_{\mathbf{v}}(1^{\lambda};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}})
with randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}
. It outputs
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}
.

•

Finally, outputs

\displaystyle 1^{\ell},\{1^{N_{i}+Q^{\prime}}\}_{i\in[\ell]};

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(N_{1})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(N_{1})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime});

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{2}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{2}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{2}(N_{2})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{2}(N_{2})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime});

\displaystyle\cdots;

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{\ell}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{\ell}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{\ell}(N_{\ell})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{\ell}(N_{\ell})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}).

The only difference between this simulation and the original specification of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{v}}
lies in how the randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}
is generated. In the simulation, algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
uses the challenge-provided
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}
instead of a freshly sampled
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{2}}
. However, since
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}
is uniformly distributed, this substitution does not alter the distribution of any component. Thus, the overall output remains consistent with that of the original specification of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathbf{v}}
.

3.

Algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
samples
\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{P}_{i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\mathbf{B}_{i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m^{\prime}}
for all
i\in[\ell]
. It then sets

[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}]\leftarrow\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}\mathbf{B}_{1}&\mathbf{P}_{1}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
\mathbf{B}_{\ell}&\mathbf{P}_{\ell}\end{array}\right]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell\times(m^{\prime}+m)}.

4.

Then algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
constructs the components as follows:

•

\mathbf{z}_{1,i}
: For each
i\in[\ell]
, sample
\mathbf{z}_{1,i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
.

•

\mathbf{z}_{2}
: For each
i\in[\ell]
, sample
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}}
. Then for each
2\leq i\leq\ell
, sample
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
, and sample
\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
. Set

\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}\leftarrow\left[\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\,\Bigg|\,\ \tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}+\sum_{2\leq i\leq\ell}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\right].

•

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)}
: It is computed in the same way as in experiment
\mathsf{H}_{3,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

•

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)}
:

–

For
i=1
, compute

y_{1,j}^{\prime}\leftarrow\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}+\beta_{j}

for each
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
.

–

For
i>1
, the sampling procedure for
y_{i,j}^{\prime}
is the same as in experiment
\mathsf{H}_{3,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{3,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

–

Finally, it samples
\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(2)}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{Q^{\prime}}
for each
i\in[\ell]
, and sets

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}\leftarrow[y_{i,1}^{\prime}\mid\cdots\mid y_{i,Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}]+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}.

5.

The algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
sends the challenge
\{1^{\lambda},(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}),(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top})_{i\in[\ell]},[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}],\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top},\{\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]}\}
to the distinguisher
\mathcal{D}
and outputs whatever
\mathcal{D}
outputs.

From the definition of the algorithm
\mathcal{D}
, it is clear that the components

\{1^{\lambda},(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}),(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top})_{i\in[\ell]},[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}],\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top},\{\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},\{\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)}\}_{2\leq i\leq\ell}\}

are exactly distributed as in
\mathsf{H}_{3,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{3,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
. It remains to consider the distribution of
\mathbf{z}_{3,1}^{(2)}
in two experiments.

•

If
\beta_{j}=\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime}+e_{j}
for some
\mathbf{u}\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{u}}(1^{\lambda},(\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}_{1}},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}});\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pri}}),e_{j}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}
, then by the construction procedure, each
y_{1,j}^{\prime}
is computed exactly as in
\mathsf{H}_{3,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, and thus the resulting distribution of
\mathbf{z}_{3,1}^{(2)}
matches that in
\mathsf{H}_{3,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

•

If
\beta_{j}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, then the resulting distribution of
\mathbf{z}_{3,1}^{(2)}
is identical to that in experiment
\mathsf{H}_{3,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Therefore, algorithm
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
breaks the pseudorandomness of the noisy inner product produced by
\mathsf{Samp}
with the same advantage as
\mathcal{D}
distinguishes between
\mathsf{H}_{3,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{3,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
. This leads to a contradiction with the assumption that
\mathsf{Samp}
produces pseudorandom noisy inner products. Hence
\mathsf{H}_{3,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{3,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, as claimed.
∎

Claim 44
.

We have that
\mathsf{H}_{3,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\equiv\mathsf{H}_{3,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Proof.

The claim follows directly from the observation that in experiment
\mathsf{H}_{3,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, the only component depending on
\omega_{j}
is
y_{1,j}^{\prime}
. Since
\omega_{j}
is sampled uniformly and independent of all other components, it follows that
y_{1,j}^{\prime}
is also uniform and independent of all other components. This matches precisely the distribution of
y_{1,j}^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{H}_{3,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
41
(Continued)
.
By applying a sequence of hybrid arguments from Claim
42
to Claim
44
, we obtain that
\mathsf{H}_{3,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{3,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
. Since we have already established that
\mathsf{H}_{3,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\equiv\mathsf{H}_{3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{3,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\equiv\mathsf{H}_{4}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, which completes the argument that
\mathsf{H}_{3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{4}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
∎

Lemma 45
.

Suppose
m^{\prime}>6n\log q
and
\chi^{\prime}=\Omega(\sqrt{n\log q})
. Let
\chi_{s}
be an error parameter such that
\chi\geq\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot\sqrt{\lambda}\chi_{s}
and suppose that the
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,m_{1},q,\chi_{s}}
assumption holds for
m_{1}=\poly(Q_{0})
, where
Q_{0}
is the upper bound on the total number of secret-key queries (including those in the partial set
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{par}}^{\prime}
) submitted by the adversary. Then we have that
\mathsf{H}_{4}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{5}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Proof.

We prove this lemma by defining a sequence of intermediate hybrid experiments between
\mathsf{H}_{4}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{5}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
. For each
1<d\leq\ell+1
, we introduce the following sequence of games:

Game
\mathsf{H}_{4,d,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
The experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{4}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, except for how it samples
\mathbf{z}_{2}
and
y_{i,j},y_{i,j}^{\prime}
. Specifically, the challenger first samples
\boxed{\mathbf{z}_{2}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}+m}}
, and then sets
y_{i,j}
and
y_{i,j}^{\prime}
as follows:

•

For each
i<d
, sample
\boxed{y_{i,j}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}}
for each
j\in[N_{i}]
and
\boxed{y_{i,j}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}}
for each
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
.

•

For each
i\geq d
, set

\displaystyle y_{i,j}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i}(j)},\quad\text{for all }j\in[N_{i}],

\displaystyle y_{i,j}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime},\quad\text{for all }j\in[Q^{\prime}].

Game
\mathsf{H}_{4,d,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
The experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{4,d,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, except for how it samples
y_{d,j},y_{d,j}^{\prime}
. The challenger first samples
e_{i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}
for each
i\in[Q]
and
e_{j}^{\prime}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}
for each
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
, then sets
y_{i,j}
and
y_{i,j}^{\prime}
as follows:

•

For each
i<d
, sample
y_{i,j}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
for each
j\in[N_{i}]
and
y_{i,j}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
for each
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
as in
\mathsf{H}_{4,d,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

•

For
i=d
, set

\displaystyle y_{d,j}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,d}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{d}(j)})+(\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,d}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{d}(j)}+\boxed{e_{\rho_{d}(j)}}),\quad\text{for all }j\in[N_{d}],

\displaystyle y_{d,j}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,d}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+(\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,d}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}+\boxed{e_{j}^{\prime}}),\quad\text{for all }j\in[Q^{\prime}].

•

For each
i>d
, compute
y_{i,j},y_{i,j}^{\prime}
in the same way as in
\mathsf{H}_{4,d,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{4,d,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
The experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{4,d,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
except that it replaces the error terms in
y_{d,j},y_{d,j}^{\prime}
with uniformly random values. The challenger first samples
\delta_{i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
for each
i\in[Q]
, and
\delta_{j}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
for each
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
. Then it sets
y_{d,j}
and
y_{d,j}^{\prime}
as follows:

\displaystyle y_{d,j}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,d}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{d}(j)})+\boxed{\delta_{\rho_{d}(j)}},\quad\text{for all }j\in[N_{d}],

\displaystyle y_{d,j}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,d}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\boxed{\delta_{j}^{\prime}},\quad\text{for all }j\in[Q^{\prime}].

Game
\mathsf{H}_{4,d,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
The experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{4,d,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
except for the values
y_{d,j},y_{d,j}^{\prime}
are sampled uniformly at random. Specifically,

•

The challenger samples
\boxed{y_{d,j}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}}
for each
j\in[N_{d}]
and
\boxed{y_{d,j}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}}
for each
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
.

From the construction above, it follows that
\mathsf{H}_{4,2,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\equiv\mathsf{H}_{4}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
. Since the only component dependent on
