\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime}
is
\mathbf{z}_{2}
, directly sampling
\mathbf{z}_{2}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}+m}
does not affect the overall distribution. Moreover, it is straightforward to verify that
\mathsf{H}_{4,\ell+1,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\equiv\mathsf{H}_{5}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
. In the following, we prove that each adjacent pair of hybrids is computationally indistinguishable for all
2\leq d\leq\ell
.

Claim 46
.

Suppose that
\chi\geq\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot\sqrt{\lambda}\chi_{s}
. Then we have that
\mathsf{H}_{4,d,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{s}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{4,d,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
for
2\leq d\leq\ell
.

Proof.

The proof follows essentially the same argument as that of Claim
35
.
∎

Claim 47
.

Suppose that
m^{\prime}>6n\log q
and
\chi^{\prime}=\Omega(\sqrt{n\log q})
, and suppose the
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,m_{1},q,\chi_{s}}
assumption holds for some
m_{1}=\poly(Q_{0})
. Then we have
\mathsf{H}_{4,d,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{4,d,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
for
2\leq d\leq\ell
.

Proof.

The proof follows essentially the same argument as that of Claim
36
.
∎

Claim 48
.

For all
2\leq d\leq\ell
, we have
\mathsf{H}_{4,d,2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\equiv\mathsf{H}_{4,d,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Proof.

This follows directly from the fact that
\delta_{j},\delta_{j}^{\prime}
are sampled uniformly and independently over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
. Thus, replacing them with fresh uniform values does not alter the distributions of
y_{d,j}
and
y_{d,j}^{\prime}
.
∎

Claim 49
.

For all
2\leq d\leq\ell
, we have
\mathsf{H}_{4,d,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\equiv\mathsf{H}_{4,d+1,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Proof.

This follows directly by the construction of the two experiments.
\mathsf{H}_{4,d,3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
ends with
y_{d,j},y_{d,j}^{\prime}
sampled uniformly at random, which matches the initial sampling procedure of
\mathsf{H}_{4,d+1,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
for index
d
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
45
(Continued)
.
By Claims
46
through
49
, we obtain that
\mathsf{H}_{4,d,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{4,d+1,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
for all
2\leq d\leq\ell
.
By applying a standard hybrid argument, we can conclude that
\mathsf{H}_{4}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\equiv\mathsf{H}_{4,2,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{4,\ell+1,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\equiv\mathsf{H}_{5}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, completing the proof.
∎

Proof of Claim
27
(Continued)
. By combining Lemmas
31
,
32
,
33
, and
41
, and applying a standard hybrid argument, we conclude that
\mathsf{H}_{0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{5}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, thereby completing the proof.
∎

VI-C

Parameters

Let
\lambda
be the security parameter.

•

We set the lattice dimension
n=\lambda^{1/\epsilon}
and the modulus
q=2^{\tilde{O}(n^{\epsilon})}=2^{\tilde{O}(\lambda)}
for some constant
\epsilon>0
, where
\tilde{O}(\cdot)
suppresses constant and logarithmic factors. Then
m=n\lceil\log q\rceil=\tilde{O}(\lambda^{1+1/\epsilon})
.

•

We set
L=2^{\lambda}
to support ciphertext policies of arbitrary polynomial size, i.e.,
\ell=\poly(\lambda)
.

•

For the static security (Theorem
25
), we set
m^{\prime}=\tilde{O}(\lambda^{1+1/\epsilon}),Q_{0}=\poly(\lambda)
and
\chi^{\prime}=\Theta(\lambda^{1+1/\epsilon})
. The construction relies on the
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,m_{1},q,\chi_{s}}
assumption and the
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}_{n,m,m^{\prime},q,\chi,\chi}
assumption, where the noise parameter
\chi_{s}=\chi_{s}(\lambda)
is set polynomial-bounded and
\chi
satisfies the bound
\chi\geq\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot(\sqrt{\lambda}(m+\ell)\chi_{s}+\lambda m\chi^{\prime}\chi_{s})
. This requirement is satisfied by setting
\chi=2^{\tilde{O}(n^{\epsilon})}
.

•

To ensure correctness (Theorem
24
), we require
\chi>\sqrt{n\log q}\cdot\omega(\sqrt{\log n})
, which is also satisfied by setting
\chi=2^{\tilde{O}(n^{\epsilon})}
. In addition, we have the relationship
B_{0}=\sqrt{\lambda}\chi m+\lambda\chi\chi^{\prime}m^{\prime}+\lambda\chi^{2}mL
, which implies
B_{0}=2^{\tilde{O}(n^{\epsilon})}
. Then we obtain
B_{0}/(\sqrt{n}\chi_{s})=\mathrm{superpoly}(\lambda)
, which aligns with the convention in the
\mathsf{NIPFE}
scheme as in
[
11
]
.

We summarize with the following instantiation

Corollary 1

(
(B_{0},\chi_{s})
-
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
for Subset Policies in the Random Oracle Model)
.

Let
\lambda
be a security parameter. Assuming the polynomial hardness of
\mathsf{LWE}
and of the
\mathsf{evIPFE}
(defined in Section
IV
), both holding under a sub-exponential modulus-to-noise ratio, there exists a statically secure
(B_{0},\chi_{s})
-
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
scheme for subset policies in the random oracle model, with
B_{0}/\chi_{s}=\mathrm{superpoly}(\lambda)
.

VII
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
Scheme from
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
Assumption (in the Random Oracle Model)

We observe that the
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
construction presented in Construction
1
also naturally satisfies the syntax of a
(B_{0},B_{1})
-
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
scheme over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
for subset policies. Accordingly, we reinterpret the scheme in Construction
1
as an
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
scheme, and denote it by

\Pi_{\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}}=(\mathsf{GlobalSetup},\mathsf{AuthSetup},\mathsf{Keygen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec}),

where all algorithms are identical to those defined in Construction
1
.

In the following, we present the approximate correctness and static security properties of this scheme.

VII-A

Correctness

Theorem 50

(Correctness)
.

Let
\chi_{0}=\sqrt{n\log q}\cdot\omega(\sqrt{\log n})
be a polynomial such that Lemma
7
holds. Suppose that the lattice parameters
n,q,\chi
are such that
\chi\geq\chi_{0}(n,q)
. Then the scheme
\Pi_{\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}}
in Construction
1
is
correct
as a
(B_{0},B_{1})
-
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
scheme, where the parameter
B_{0}=\sqrt{\lambda}\chi m+\lambda\chi\chi^{\prime}m^{\prime}+\lambda\chi^{2}m\ell.

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as Theorem
24
.
∎

VII-B

Security

Theorem 51

(Static Security)
.

Let
\chi_{0}(n,q)=\sqrt{n\log q}\cdot\omega(\sqrt{\log n})
be a polynomial such that Lemma
7
holds. Let
Q_{0}
be an upper bound on the number of secret-key queries submitted by the adversary
\mathcal{A}
. Suppose that the following conditions hold:

•

m^{\prime}>6n\log q
.

•

Let
\chi^{\prime}
be an error distribution parameter such that
\chi^{\prime}=\Omega(\sqrt{n\log q})
.

•

Let
\chi
be an error distribution parameter such that
\chi\geq\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot\max\{B_{1},\sqrt{\lambda}\chi_{s},\chi_{0}\}
, where
\chi_{s}
is an noise parameter such that
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,m_{1},q,\chi_{s}}
assumption holds for some
m_{1}=\poly(m,m^{\prime},Q_{0})
.

•

The assumption
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}_{n,m,m^{\prime},q,\chi,\chi}
holds.

Then, Construction
1

\Pi_{\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}}
is
statically secure
as a
(B_{0},B_{1})
-
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
scheme.

Proof.

We prove the static security of
\Pi_{\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}}
under the standard static security model by defining two experiments
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1}
, corresponding to challenge bits
0
and
1
, respectively. The goal is to show that no efficient adversary can distinguish between these two games with non-negligible advantage. We begin by giving the definition of the structure of Game
\mathsf{H}_{b}
for
b\in\{0,1\}
.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{b}
.
This experiment corresponds to the real static security game in which the challenger encrypts the plaintext
\mathbf{u}_{b}
exactly as specified in Construction
1
, where
b\in\{0,1\}
. The detailed structure of the experiment is as follows.

At the beginning of the experiment, the adversary
\mathcal{A}
specifies the following parameters:

•

A set of corrupt authorities
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{AU}
along with their public keys:
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}=(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}})
for each
\mathsf{aid}\in\mathcal{C}
.

•

A set of non-corrupt authorities
\mathcal{N}\subseteq\mathcal{AU}
, satisfying
\mathcal{N}\cap\mathcal{C}=\varnothing
.

•

A pair of challenge messages
\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{u}_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
along with a designated authority set
A^{*}\subseteq\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{N}
satisfying
(A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C})\subsetneqq A^{*}
.

•

A set of secret-key queries
\mathcal{Q}=\{(\mathsf{gid},A,\mathbf{v})\}
, where
A\subseteq\mathcal{N}
. For each
(\mathsf{gid},A,\mathbf{v})\in\mathcal{Q}
, exactly one of the following conditions must hold:

–

Type I secret-key queries
:
(A\cup\mathcal{C})\cap A^{*}\subsetneqq A^{*}
;

–

Type II secret-key queries
:
(A\cup\mathcal{C})\cap A^{*}=A^{*}
and
\|(\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\|\leq B_{1}
.

To simulate the random oracle, the challenger initializes an empty table
\mathsf{T}:\mathcal{GID}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}}
. This table will be used to store and consistently respond to all queries made to the random oracle during the experiment. The challenger then processes the adversary’s queries as follows.

•

Public keys for non-corrupt authorities:
For each non-corrupt authority
\mathsf{aid}\in\mathcal{N}
, the challenger samples
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{td}_{\mathsf{aid}})\leftarrow\mathsf{TrapGen}(1^{n},1^{m},q),\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m^{\prime}}
, and
\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
. The public key associated with
\mathsf{aid}
is then set as
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}=(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}})
.

•

Secret-key queries:
For each secret-key query
(\mathsf{gid},A,\mathbf{v})\in\mathcal{Q}
, the challenger first computes
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\leftarrow\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})
, then samples
\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\leftarrow\mathsf{SamplePre}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{td}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v})+\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}},\chi)
for each
\mathsf{aid}\in A
. The resulting secret key is set as
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}=\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}
.

•

Challenge ciphertext:
The challenger first samples
\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n},\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m}
for each
\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}
. Then it samples
\mathbf{e}_{2}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m^{\prime}}
, and
\mathbf{e}_{3}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m}
. The challenge ciphertext is constructed as
\mathsf{ct}=\left(\{\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}},\mathbf{c}_{2}^{\top},\mathbf{c}_{3}^{\top}\right)
, where

\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}=\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top},\mathbf{c}_{2}^{\top}=\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}}\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top},\mathbf{c}_{3}^{\top}=\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}}\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}_{b}^{\top}\mathbf{G}.

•

Random oracle queries:
Upon receiving a query
(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})\in\mathcal{GID}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, the challenger first checks whether the input
(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})
has been queried before, either during previous random oracle queries by the adversary or during processing the secret-key queries. If it has, the challenger retrieves and returns the stored value
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}
from the table
\mathsf{T}
. If the input is new, the challenger samples
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}
, stores the mapping
(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})\mapsto\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}
to the table
\mathsf{T}
, and then responds with
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}
.

At the end of the game, the adversary outputs a bit
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
as its guess for
b
, which is taken as the output of the experiment.

To establish the static security of Construction
1
as a
(B_{0},B_{1})
-
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
scheme, it suffices to show that games
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1}
are computationally indistinguishable. Suppose, for contradiction, that there exists an
efficient
adversary
\mathcal{A}
that can distinguish between
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1}
with non-negligible advantage. We will show that such an adversary can be used to break the
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption, thereby contradicting its assumed hardness.

We construct a sampling algorithm
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}
that uses
\mathcal{A}
as a subroutine for the
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption. On input the global parameter
\mathsf{gp}=(1^{\lambda},q,1^{m},1^{m^{\prime}},1^{\chi},1^{\chi})
, the sampling algorithm
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathsf{gp})
proceeds as follows:

1.

Let
\kappa=\kappa(\lambda)
be an upper bound on the number of random bits used by the adversary
\mathcal{A}
. Sample
\mathbf{r}\xleftarrow{\$}\{0,1\}^{\kappa}
and run
\mathcal{A}(1^{\lambda};\mathbf{r})
.

2.

The adversary
\mathcal{A}
outputs a set of corrupt authorities
\mathcal{C}
, a set of non-corrupt authorities
\mathcal{N}
, a set of secret-key queries
\mathcal{Q}=\{(\mathsf{gid},A,\mathbf{v})\}
, a pair of challenge messages
\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{u}_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and a set of authorities
A^{*}
associated with the challenge ciphertext. These outputs correspond to the queries submitted by the adversary in the static security game for

\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
.

3.

Let
\ell=|A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}|
and denote the set
A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}=\{\mathsf{aid}_{1}^{*},\ldots,\mathsf{aid}_{\ell}^{*}\}
.

4.

Partition the set
\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{Q}_{I}\cup\mathcal{Q}_{II}
, where
\mathcal{Q}_{I},\mathcal{Q}_{II}
contain all secret-key queries of Type I and Type II, respectively. Let
|\mathcal{Q}_{I}|=Q
,
|\mathcal{Q}_{II}|=Q^{\prime}
and write

\mathcal{Q}_{I}=\{(\mathsf{gid}_{1},A_{1},\mathbf{v}_{1}),\ldots,(\mathsf{gid}_{Q},A_{Q},\mathbf{v}_{Q})\},\quad\mathcal{Q}_{II}=\{(\mathsf{gid}_{1}^{\prime},A_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathsf{gid}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathcal{A}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime})\}.

5.

For each
i\in[\ell]
, let
N_{i}\in[Q]
denote the number of Type I secret-key queries in which the challenge authority
\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}
appears. Suppose that authority
\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}
is contained in the set
A_{j_{1}^{(i)}},\ldots,A_{j_{N_{i}}^{(i)}}
for some indices
j_{1}^{(i)},\ldots,j_{N_{i}}^{(i)}\in[Q]
, listed in increasing order. Define the mapping
\rho_{i}:[N_{i}]\rightarrow[Q]
by setting
\rho_{i}(k)=j_{k}^{(i)}
. That is,
\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}
appears exactly in the set
A_{\rho_{i}(1)},\ldots,A_{\rho_{i}(N_{i})}
with the indices ordered increasingly.

6.

For each
i\in[Q]
, sample
\mathbf{r}_{i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}
. For each
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
, sample
\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}
.

7.

Finally,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}
outputs

\displaystyle 1^{\ell},\{1^{N_{i}+Q^{\prime}}\}_{i\in[\ell]};

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(N_{1})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(N_{1})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime});

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{2}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{2}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{2}(N_{2})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{2}(N_{2})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime});

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{\ell}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{\ell}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{\ell}(N_{\ell})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{\ell}(N_{\ell})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime});

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{u}_{1}.

We remark that the sampling algorithm
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}
defined above does not explicitly guarantee that each tuple
(\mathbf{r}_{i},\mathbf{v}_{i})
or
(\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})
is non-zero, as required in Assumption
4
. However, since each
\mathbf{r}_{i},\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}
component in the tuple is sampled from discrete Gaussian distribution
D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}
, the probability that any of them equals the all-zero vector is
negligible
. Specifically, by a standard tail bound argument, we can upper bound the probability that a sample equals the all-zero vector as follows:

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{0}_{m^{\prime}}:\mathbf{r}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m^{\prime}}}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\|\mathbf{x}\|^{2}/\chi^{\prime 2}}}=\left(\frac{1}{\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi|x|^{2}/\chi^{\prime 2}}}\right)^{m^{\prime}}

\displaystyle<\left(\frac{1}{1+2\mathrm{e}^{-\pi/\chi^{\prime 2}}}\right)^{m^{\prime}}\leq(1-c\mathrm{e}^{-\pi/\chi^{\prime 2}})^{m^{\prime}}\leq\exp\left(-c\cdot m^{\prime}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi/\chi^{\prime 2}}\right),

for some constant
c>0
. When
\chi^{\prime}
is taken to be polynomial in
m^{\prime}
, the probability is
\mathrm{e}^{-\Omega(m^{\prime})}
, which is negligible in
m^{\prime}
. Therefore, we may safely ignore the negligible probability that the sampling algorithm outputs an all-zero tuple
(\mathbf{r}_{i},\mathbf{v}_{i})
or
(\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})
.

We now claim that for all efficient distinguishers
\mathcal{D}
, the advantage
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{D}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}(\lambda)
(cf. Assumption
4
) for sampling algorithm
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}
is negligible in
\lambda
. More precisely,

Claim 52
.

Let
Q_{0}
be an upper bound on the number of secret-key queries submitted by the adversary
\mathcal{A}
. Suppose that the following conditions hold:

•

m^{\prime}>6n\log q
.

•

Let
\chi^{\prime}
be an error distribution such that
\chi^{\prime}=\Omega(\sqrt{n\log q})
.

•

Let
\chi
be an error distribution parameter such that
\chi\geq\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot\max\{B_{1},\sqrt{\lambda}\chi_{s}\}
, where
\chi_{s}
is an error parameter such that
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,m_{1},q,\chi_{s}}
assumption holds for some
m_{1}=\poly(m,m^{\prime},Q_{0})
.

Then, for every efficient distinguisher
\mathcal{D}
, there exists a negligible function
\negl(\cdot)
such that for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{D}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}(\lambda)=\negl(\lambda)
, where
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{D}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
denotes the advantage of the distinguisher
\mathcal{D}
in the
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption described in Assumption
4
.

Assuming Claim
27
, we proceed with the proof of Theorem
51
. For clarity, the proof of Claim
27
is postponed to the end of this section.

Proof of Theorem
51
(Continued).

To complete the proof, we show that if there exists an
efficient
adversary
\mathcal{A}
that can distinguish between
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1}
with non-negligible advantage, then we can construct an
efficient
algorithm
\mathcal{B}
that breaks the
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption with respect to the sampling algorithm
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}
. The algorithm
\mathcal{B}
proceeds as follows:

1.

Algorithm
\mathcal{B}
receives an
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
challenge

(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}],\mathbf{y}_{2}^{\top},\{\mathbf{K}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell]}),

where
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}\in\{0,1\}^{*},\mathbf{A}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\mathbf{y}_{1,i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\mathbf{K}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m\times(N_{i}+Q^{\prime})}
for each
i\in[\ell]
,
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell\times m^{\prime}},\mathbf{P}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell\times m},\mathbf{y}_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}+m}
. Recall that in the public-coin model,
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}
contains all the randomness used by sampling algorithm
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

2.

For each
i\in[\ell]
, algorithm
\mathcal{B}
parses the matrix
\mathbf{K}_{i}
as

\mathbf{K}_{i}=[\mathbf{K}_{i}^{(1)}\mid\mathbf{K}_{i}^{(2)}],

where
\mathbf{K}_{i}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m\times N_{i}}
and
\mathbf{K}_{i}^{(2)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m\times Q^{\prime}}
. Let
\mathbf{k}_{i,j},\mathbf{k}_{i,j}^{\prime}
denote the
j
-th column vectors of
\mathbf{K}_{i}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{K}_{i}^{(2)}
, respectively.

3.

Algorithm
\mathcal{B}
runs algorithm
\mathcal{A}
, using the appropriate portion of the public randomness
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}
—specifically, the same portion used internally by
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}
to simulate
\mathcal{A}
. The adversary
\mathcal{A}
outputs the following queries:

•

A set of corrupt authorities
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{AU}
, along with their public keys
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}=(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}})
for all
\mathsf{aid}\in\mathcal{C}
.

•

A set of non-corrupt authorities
\mathcal{N}\subseteq\mathcal{AU}
, satisfying
\mathcal{N}\cap\mathcal{C}=\varnothing
.

•

A pair of challenge messages
\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{u}_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, and a ciphertext authority set
A^{*}\subseteq\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{N}
such that
(A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C})\subsetneqq A^{*}
.

•

A set of secret-key queries
\mathcal{Q}
, where each query
(\mathsf{gid},A,\mathbf{v})
is such that
A\subseteq\mathcal{N}
and satisfies exactly one of the following two conditions:

–

Type I:
(A_{i}\cup\mathcal{C})\cap A^{*}\subsetneqq A^{*}
.

–

Type II:
(A_{i}\cup\mathcal{C})\cap A^{*}=A^{*}
and
\|(\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{i}\|\leq B_{1}
.

4.

Algorithm
\mathcal{B}
runs sampling algorithm
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathsf{gp};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}})
, which outputs

\displaystyle 1^{\ell},\{1^{N_{i}+Q^{\prime}}\}_{i\in[\ell]};

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(N_{1})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(N_{1})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime});

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{2}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{2}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{2}(N_{2})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{2}(N_{2})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime});

\displaystyle\cdots;

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{\ell}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{\ell}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{\ell}(N_{\ell})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{\ell}(N_{\ell})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}).

Since the randomness used to simulate
\mathcal{A}
in Step 3) comes from a specific portion of
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}
, the values produced here—namely, the vectors
\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{Q},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}
—align exactly with those generated during the simulation of
\mathcal{A}
in Step 3). This ensures that algorithm
\mathcal{B}
’s internal simulation of
\mathcal{A}
is consistent with the actual input instance of the
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
challenge.

5.

From the construction of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}
, we have that
\ell=|A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}|
, i.e., the number of non-corrupt authorities appearing in the challenge ciphertext. Let
A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}=\{\mathsf{aid}_{1}^{*},\ldots,\mathsf{aid}_{\ell}^{*}\}
. Algorithm
\mathcal{B}
first sets
\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}\leftarrow\mathbf{A}_{i}
for each
i\in[\ell]
, and parses
[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}]
as

[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}_{1}^{*}}&\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}_{1}^{*}}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}_{\ell}^{*}}&\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}_{\ell}^{*}}\end{array}\right]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell\times(m^{\prime}+m)},

where
\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m^{\prime}}
and
\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
for each
\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}
.

6.

Algorithm
\mathcal{B}
partitions the set of secret-key queries
\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{Q}_{I}\cup\mathcal{Q}_{II}
, where
\mathcal{Q}_{I},\mathcal{Q}_{II}
contain all secret-key queries of Type I and Type II, respectively. Let
|\mathcal{Q}_{I}|=Q
,
|\mathcal{Q}_{II}|=Q^{\prime}
, and denote

\mathcal{Q}_{I}=\{(\mathsf{gid}_{1},A_{1},\mathbf{v}_{1}),\ldots,(\mathsf{gid}_{Q},A_{Q},\mathbf{v}_{Q})\},\quad\mathcal{Q}_{II}=\{(\mathsf{gid}_{1}^{\prime},A_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathsf{gid}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},A_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime})\}.

For each
i\in[Q]
, it partitions
A_{i}=A_{i,\mathsf{chal}}\cup\bar{A}_{i,\mathsf{chal}}
, where
A_{i,\mathsf{chal}}
consists of the authorities in
A_{i}
that appear in the ciphertext, i.e.
A_{i,\mathsf{chal}}=A_{i}\cap A^{*}
.

7.

Algorithm
\mathcal{B}
initializes an empty table
\mathsf{T}:\mathcal{GID}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}}
. This table will be used to store and consistently respond to all queries made to the random oracle during the experiment.

8.

The algorithm
\mathcal{B}
responds to the queries as follows:

•

Public keys for non-corrupt authorities
:

–

For each
\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}
, algorithm
\mathcal{B}
sets
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}\leftarrow(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}},\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}})
.

–

For authorities
\mathsf{aid}\in\mathcal{N}\setminus A^{*}
, algorithm
\mathcal{B}
samples
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{td}_{\mathsf{aid}})\leftarrow\mathsf{TrapGen}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
,
\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m^{\prime}}
and
\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
, and sets the public key
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}\leftarrow(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}})
.

•

Secret keys
: The algorithm
\mathcal{B}
responds to each secret-key query depending on its type:

–

Type I
: For a Type I secret-key query
(\mathsf{gid}_{k},A_{k},\mathbf{v}_{k})
, recall that
A_{k}
is partitioned as
A_{k}=A_{k,\mathsf{chal}}\cup\bar{A}_{k,\mathsf{chal}}
, where
A_{k,\mathsf{chal}}=A_{k}\cap A^{*}
.

*

For each
\mathsf{aid}_{i^{*}}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}
, recall that the number of secret-key queries involving
\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}
is the parameter
N_{i}
. Let
\rho(\cdot)
be the index mapping previously defined in the proof of Lemma
51
. For each
j\in[N_{i}]
, set
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*},\mathsf{gid}_{\rho_{i}(j)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)}}\leftarrow\mathbf{k}_{i,j}
. Then the algorithm
\mathcal{B}
checks if the table
\mathsf{T}
has ever recorded the image of
(\mathsf{gid}_{\rho_{i}(j)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)})
, if not, store the mapping
(\mathsf{gid}_{\rho_{i}(j)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)})\mapsto\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i}(j)}
to the table.

*

For each
k\in[Q]
, if
A_{k,\mathsf{chal}}=\varnothing
, then sample
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid}_{k},\mathbf{v}_{k}}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}
, and add the mapping
(\mathsf{gid}_{k},\mathbf{v}_{k})\mapsto\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid}_{k},\mathbf{v}_{k}}
to the table. At this point, the table contains the image of all pairs
(\mathsf{gid}_{k},\mathbf{v}_{k})
for each
k\in[Q]
.

*

For each
k\in[Q]
, for each
\mathsf{aid}\in\bar{A}_{k,\mathsf{chal}}
, compute

\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid}_{k},\mathbf{v}_{k}}\leftarrow\mathsf{SamplePre}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{td}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{k})+\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{gid}_{k},\mathbf{v}_{k})),

where the value
\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{gid}_{k},\mathbf{v}_{k})
is retrieved from the table
\mathsf{T}
.

–

Type II
: For a Type II query
(\mathsf{gid}_{j}^{\prime},A_{j}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})
, recall that
A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}=\{\mathsf{aid}_{1}^{*},\ldots,\mathsf{aid}_{\ell}^{*}\}\subseteq A_{j}^{\prime}
.

*

For each
i\in[\ell],j\in[Q^{\prime}]
, set
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*},\mathsf{gid}_{j}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime}}\leftarrow\mathbf{k}_{i,j}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
. Next, algorithm
\mathcal{B}
adds the mapping
(\mathsf{gid}_{j}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})\mapsto\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}
to the table
\mathsf{T}
.

*

For each
\mathsf{aid}\in A_{j}^{\prime}\setminus A^{*}
, algorithm
\mathcal{B}
computes

\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid}_{j}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime}}\leftarrow\mathsf{SamplePre}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{td}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime},\chi)

efficiently
.

•

Challenge ciphertext
: Algorithm
\mathcal{B}
parses
\mathbf{y}_{2}
as
\mathbf{y}_{2}^{\top}=[\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{2}^{\top}\mid\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{3}^{\top}]
where
\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}},\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
. It constructs the ciphertext as follows:
For each
\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C}
, sample
\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and
\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m}
, then set
\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
. For
\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}
, set
\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}\leftarrow\mathbf{y}_{1,i}
. Finally, algorithm
\mathcal{B}
constructs the ciphertext

\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\left(\{\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}},\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C}}\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{2}^{\top},\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C}}\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{3}^{\top}\right).

•

Random oracle queries:
Upon receiving a query
(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})\in\mathcal{GID}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, algorithm
\mathcal{B}
checks whether the input
(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})
has been queried before—either during the adversary’s direct random oracle queries or implicitly through processing the secret-key queries. If so, algorithm
\mathcal{B}
retrieves and responds with the stored value
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}
from the table
\mathsf{T}
. If the input is new, the challenger samples
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\mapsto D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m^{\prime}}
, records the mapping
(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})\rightarrow\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}
to the table
\mathsf{T}
, and then replies with
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}
.

9.

Finally, algorithm
\mathcal{B}
outputs whatever algorithm
\mathcal{A}
outputs.

The distributions of the public keys for non-corrupt authorities are exactly the same as those in
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1}
, as they are uniformly generated. We now examine the responses to the secret-key queries:

•

For each
\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{k}_{i,j}

\displaystyle\leftarrow(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}})_{\chi}^{-1}(\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)})+\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i}(j)}),\quad\text{for all }j\in[N_{i}],

\displaystyle\mathbf{k}_{i,j}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leftarrow(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}})_{\chi}^{-1}(\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}),\quad\text{for all }j\in[Q^{\prime}].

This perfectly matches the distribution of
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*},\mathsf{gid}_{\rho_{i}(j)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)}}
and
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*},\mathsf{gid}_{j}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime}}
in the actual game, respectively.

•

For each
\mathsf{aid}\in A_{j}\setminus A^{*}
for some
j\in[Q]
, the secret key
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid}_{j},\mathbf{v}_{j}}
is generated using
\mathsf{SamplePre}
, identical to the procedure in
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1}
. The same argument also applies to
\mathsf{aid}\in A_{j}^{\prime}\setminus A^{*}
for
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
.

Finally, we analyze the distribution of the challenge ciphertext.
Observe that for each
i\in[\ell]
,
\mathbf{y}_{1,i}^{\top}=\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{1,i}^{\top}
and
\mathbf{y}_{2}^{\top}=\mathbf{s}^{\top}[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}+[\mathbf{0}_{m^{\prime}}^{\top}\mid\mathbf{u}_{b}^{\top}\mathbf{G}]
for some
\mathbf{s}^{\top}=[\mathbf{s}_{1}^{\top}\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{s}_{\ell}^{\top}]\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell}
, and we write
\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}
as
[\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\top}\mid\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{3}^{\top}]
where
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}},\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}^{\top}=\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top}=\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}+\mathbf{e}_{1,i}^{\top},\text{ for each }\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N},

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{2}^{\top}=\sum_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}}\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}+\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\top},

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{3}^{\top}=\sum_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}}\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}+\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{3}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}_{b}^{\top}\mathbf{G}.

From the specification of algorithm
\mathcal{B}
, the resulting ciphertext is constructed as:

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}^{\top}=\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}+\mathbf{e}_{1,i}^{\top},\quad\text{ for each }\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N},

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}=\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top},\quad\text{ for each }\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C},

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{2}^{\top}=\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C}}\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\sum_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}}\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}+\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{2}^{\top},

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{3}^{\top}=\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{C}}\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\sum_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}\in A^{*}\cap\mathcal{N}}\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}_{i}^{*}}+\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{3}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}_{b}^{\top}\mathbf{G}.

Therefore, the full ciphertext generated by
\mathcal{B}
is identically distributed to that in the experiment
\mathsf{H}_{b}
.

Therefore, the advantage
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathcal{S_{\mathcal{A}}},\mathcal{B}}^{\mathsf{Post}}
in the
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption with respect to the sampling algorithm
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}
is non-negligible. By the
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption, this implies the existence of another efficient algorithm
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, such that the advantage
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathcal{S_{\mathcal{A}}},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
is also non-negligible, thereby contradicting the indistinguishability guarantee asserted in Claim
52
.
Hence, under the
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption, no efficient adversary can distinguish between
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1}
with non-negligible advantage. This concludes the proof of Theorem
51
.
∎

We now provide the proof of Claim
52
, which was used in the proof of Theorem
51
.

Proof of Claim
52
.

We prove the claim via a sequence of hybrid games, gradually transitioning from the real
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
experiment to a game independent of the challenge bit
b
. In the following, we define a series of hybrid experiments
\mathsf{H}_{0,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}},\mathsf{H}_{1,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}},\mathsf{H}_{2,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}},\mathsf{H}_{3,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
for
b\in\{0,1\}
, where
\mathsf{H}_{0,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
corresponds to the original
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
game, and
\mathsf{H}_{3,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
is constructed to be independent of
b
. By showing that each adjacent pair of hybrids is indistinguishable, we can conclude that
\mathsf{H}_{0,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{0,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, thereby completing the proof.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{0,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

This experiment corresponds to the real game in the
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption. Specifically, the challenger proceeds as follows:

1.

Let
\kappa=\kappa(\lambda)
be an upper bound on the number of random bits used by the adversary
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}
. Sample
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}}\xleftarrow{\$}\{0,1\}^{\kappa}
.

2.

Run the sampling algorithm
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathsf{gp};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}})
which outputs

\displaystyle 1^{\ell},\{1^{N_{i}+Q^{\prime}}\}_{i\in[\ell]};

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{1}(N_{1})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{1}(N_{1})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime});

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{2}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{2}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{2}(N_{2})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{2}(N_{2})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime});

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{\ell}(1)},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{\ell}(1)}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{\ell}(N_{\ell})},\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{\ell}(N_{\ell})}),(\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime});

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{u}_{1}.

3.

Sample
\mathbf{B}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell\times m^{\prime}},\mathbf{P}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell\times m}
, and parse the matrices

\mathbf{B}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{B}_{\ell}\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{P}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{P}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{P}_{\ell}\end{pmatrix},

where
\mathbf{B}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m^{\prime}},\mathbf{P}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
for each
i\in[\ell]
.

4.

For each
i\in[\ell]
, define:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{(1)}\leftarrow\left[\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(1)})+\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i}(1)}\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(N_{i})})+\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i}(N_{i})}\right]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times N_{i}}.

and similarly define

\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{(2)}=\left[\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime})+\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime}\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime})+\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{r}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}\right]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times Q^{\prime}}.

Set
\mathbf{Q}_{i}=[\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{(1)}\mid\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{(2)}]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times(N_{i}+Q^{\prime})}
. The construction of each matrix
\mathbf{Q}_{i}
matches that specified in Assumption
4
.

5.

Then the challenger samples
\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{\ell}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and sets
\mathbf{s}^{\top}=\left[\mathbf{s}_{1}^{\top}\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{s}_{\ell}^{\top}\right]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell}
. For each
i\in[\ell]
, it samples
\mathbf{e}_{1,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m},\mathbf{e}_{3,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{N_{i}+Q^{\prime}}
. Then it samples
\mathbf{e}_{2}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m^{\prime}+m}
.

6.

Next, the challenger samples
(\mathbf{A}_{1},\mathsf{td}_{1}),\ldots,(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathsf{td}_{\ell})\leftarrow\mathsf{TrapGen}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
. It computes the following values :

•

\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{1,i}^{\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
for each
i\in[\ell]
.

•

\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}^{\top}[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}+[\mathbf{0}_{m^{\prime}}^{\top}\mid\mathbf{u}_{b}^{\top}\mathbf{G}]=\left[\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{i}\,\Bigg|\,\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i}+\mathbf{u}_{b}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}+m}.

•

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{N_{i}+Q^{\prime}}
for each
i\in[\ell]
.

The component
\mathbf{z}_{3,i}
and
\mathbf{e}_{3,i}
are parsed as

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{\top}=[\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}\mid\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{N_{i}+Q^{\prime}},\quad\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{\top}=[\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}\mid\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{N_{i}+Q^{\prime}},

where
\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)},\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{N_{i}}
and
\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)},\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(2)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{Q^{\prime}}
. For clarity, define
\mathbf{t}_{i,j}=\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)})+\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i}(j)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and
y_{i,j}=\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{t}_{i,j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
for each
i\in[\ell]
and
j\in[N_{i}]
. Similarly define
\mathbf{t}_{i,j}^{\prime}=\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and
y_{i,j}^{\prime}=\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{t}_{i,j}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
for each
i\in[\ell]
and
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
.

In summary, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{(1)}+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}=\left[\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{t}_{i,1}\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{t}_{i,N_{i}}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}=\left[y_{i,1}\mid\cdots\mid y_{i,N_{i}}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{N_{i}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{(2)}+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}=\left[\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{t}_{i,1}^{\prime}\mid\cdots\mid\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{t}_{i,Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}=\left[y_{i,1}^{\prime}\mid\cdots\mid y_{i,Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{Q^{\prime}}.

7.

The challenger sends the tuple
(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top})\}_{i\in[\ell]},[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}],\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top},\{\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]})
to the distinguisher
\mathcal{D}
.

8.

The distinguisher
\mathcal{D}
outputs a bit
\hat{b}\in\{0,1\}
, which is taken as the output of the experiment.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{1,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
The experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{0,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, except for how the values
\mathbf{z}_{1,i},\mathbf{z}_{2},\mathbf{z}_{3,i}
for each
i\in[\ell]
are generated. The changes are as follows:

•

\mathbf{z}_{1,i}
: For each
i\in[\ell]
, sample
\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{1,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}^{m}
, and compute
\boxed{\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{1,i}^{\top}+\mathbf{e}_{1,i}^{\top}}
.

•

\mathbf{z}_{2}
: For each
i\in[\ell]
, sample
\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}^{m^{\prime}}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\prime}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi_{s}}^{m}
, then compute
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\top}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}
. Finally set

\boxed{\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}\leftarrow\left[\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\,\Bigg|\,\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}+\mathbf{u}_{b}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}=\left[\sum_{i\in[\ell]}(\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\top})\,\Bigg|\,\sum_{i\in[\ell]}(\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top})+\mathbf{u}_{b}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}}.

•

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)}
: For each
i\in[\ell]
and
j\in[N_{i}]
, compute

\displaystyle y_{i,j}

\displaystyle\leftarrow(\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top})\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)})+(\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\top})\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i}(j)}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i}(j)},

and set

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}\leftarrow\left[y_{i,1}\mid\cdots\mid y_{i,N_{i}}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{N_{i}}.

•

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)}
: For each
i\in[\ell]
and
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
, compute

\displaystyle y_{i,j}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leftarrow(\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top})\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+(\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2,i}^{\top})\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime},

and set

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}\leftarrow\left[y_{i,1}^{\prime}\mid\cdots\mid y_{i,Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{Q^{\prime}}.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{2,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
The experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{1,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, except for how the values
\mathbf{z}_{1,i},\mathbf{z}_{2},\mathbf{z}_{3,i}
for each
i\in[\ell]
are generated.

•

\mathbf{z}_{1,i}
: For each
i\in[\ell]
, sample
\boxed{\mathbf{z}_{1,i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}}
.

•

\mathbf{z}_{2}
: For each
i\in[\ell]
, sample
\boxed{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}}}
and
\boxed{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}}
. Then set

\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}\leftarrow\left[\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\,\Bigg|\,\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}+\mathbf{u}_{b}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}.

•

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)}
: For each
i\in[\ell]
and
j\in[N_{i}]
, compute

y_{i,j}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{\rho_{i}(j)})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{\rho_{i}(j)},

then set

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}\leftarrow\left[y_{i,1}\mid\cdots\mid y_{i,N_{i}}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(1)\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{N_{i}}.

•

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)}
: For each
i\in[\ell]
and
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
, compute

y_{i,j}^{\prime}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime},

then set

\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}\leftarrow\left[y_{i,1}^{\prime}\mid\cdots\mid y_{i,Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{(2)\top}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{Q^{\prime}}.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{3,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
The experiment is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{2,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, except it samples
\boxed{\mathbf{z}_{3,1}^{(1)}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{N_{1}}}
.

Lemma 53
.

Suppose that
\chi\geq\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot(\sqrt{\lambda}(m+\ell)\chi_{s}+\lambda m^{\prime}\chi^{\prime}\chi_{s})
. Then we have
\mathsf{H}_{0,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{s}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{1,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Proof.

The claim follows directly from a standard noise smudging argument, as in the proof of Lemma
31
.
∎

Lemma 54
.

Suppose that the assumption
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,m_{1},q,\chi_{s}}
holds for
m_{1}=\poly(m,m^{\prime})
. We have
\mathsf{H}_{1,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{2,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
for
b\in\{0,1\}
.

Proof.

The claim follows directly from a standard application of the
\mathsf{LWE}
indistinguishability argument, as in the proof of Lemma
32
.
∎

Lemma 55
.

Let
m^{\prime}>6n\log q
and
\chi^{\prime}=\Omega(\sqrt{n\log q})
. Suppose that
\chi_{s}
is an error parameter such that
\chi\geq\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot\sqrt{\lambda}\chi_{s}
, and that the
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,m_{1},q,\chi_{s}}
assumption holds for
m_{1}=\poly(Q_{0})
, where
Q_{0}
is the upper bound of the number of secret-key queries submitted by the adversary. We have that
\mathsf{H}_{2,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{3,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Proof.

This claim follows essentially the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
33
. Specifically, the only syntactic difference between the security model of the
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
scheme and that of the
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABevIPFE}
scheme lies in whether the Type II secret-key queries are determined solely by the adversary or jointly with the challenger. However, in the proof of
\mathsf{H}_{2,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{3,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
(and likewise
\mathsf{H}_{2}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{3}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
in Lemma
33
), the distribution of the components involving Type II secret-key queries remains unchanged throughout the transition. Therefore, the indistinguishability follows directly from the argument in Lemma
33
.
∎

Lemma 56
.

Suppose that
\chi>\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot B_{1}
. Then we have
\mathsf{H}_{3,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{s}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{3,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Proof.

We prove this by defining an intermediate hybrid experiment
\mathsf{H}_{3,b,\mathsf{mid}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
between
\mathsf{H}_{3,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{3,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Game
\mathsf{H}_{3,b,\mathsf{mid}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
The game is identical to
\mathsf{H}_{3,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, except for how the challenger samples
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime}
.
Specifically:

•

\mathbf{z}_{2}
: The challenger first samples
\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
, and sets
\boxed{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\leftarrow\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\prime}-\mathbf{u}_{b}^{\top}\mathbf{G}}
. Then it computes

\boxed{\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}\leftarrow\left[\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\,\Bigg|\,\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}+\mathbf{u}_{b}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}=\left[\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\top}\,\Bigg|\,\ \tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\prime}+\sum_{2\leq i\leq\ell}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,i}^{\prime\top}\right]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}}.

•

\mathbf{z}_{3,1}^{(2)}
: In particular, we have the affected component

y_{1,j}^{\prime}=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}=\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}-\mathbf{u}_{b}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime}.

Claim 57
.

For
b\in\{0,1\}
, the two experiments
\mathsf{H}_{3,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{3,b,\mathsf{mid}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
are identical, i.e.,
\mathsf{H}_{3,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\equiv\mathsf{H}_{3,b,\mathsf{mid}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Proof.

The only difference between Game
\mathsf{H}_{3,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and Game
\mathsf{H}_{3,b,\mathsf{mid}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
lies in how the challenger samples
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime}
. In Game
\mathsf{H}_{3,b,\mathsf{mid}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime}
is generated by first sampling an independent uniform vector
\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
and then shifted by
-\mathbf{u}_{b}^{\top}\mathbf{G}
, the resulting
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime}
is uniform and independent of other components, which matches exactly the distribution of
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{H}_{3,b}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
∎

Claim 58
.

Suppose that
\chi\geq\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot B_{1}
. Then we have
\mathsf{H}_{3,0,\mathsf{mid}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{s}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{3,1,\mathsf{mid}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.

Proof.

Since in experiment
\mathsf{H}_{3,b,\mathsf{mid}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
, the components
\mathbf{z}_{1,i},\mathbf{z}_{2},\mathbf{z}_{3,i}\ (i\neq 1)
are sampled independently of the choice of the challenge bit
b
, it suffices to consider the distribution of
\mathbf{z}_{3,1}
in the two experiments. In
\mathsf{H}_{3,0,\mathsf{mid}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
,
\mathbf{z}_{3,1}^{(2)\top}=[y_{1,1}^{\prime}\mid\cdots\mid y_{1,Q^{\prime}}^{\prime}]+\mathbf{e}_{3,1}^{(2)\top}
. Specifically, for each
j\in[Q^{\prime}]
, the
j
-th entry of
\mathbf{z}_{3,1}^{(2)}
is given by

\displaystyle y_{1,j}^{\prime}+e_{3,1,j}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}+e_{3,1,j}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=(\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}-\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{G})\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}+e_{3,1,j}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}+e_{3,1,j}^{(2)}-\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}+e_{3,1,j}^{(2)}-(\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\top}+(\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1})^{\top})\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\overset{s}{\approx}\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}+e_{3,1,j}^{(2)}-\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\tilde{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}_{2,1}^{\prime\top}-\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{G})\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2,1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime}+e_{3,1,j}^{(2)}.

(9)

The approximate identity holds due to the following reason: for each Type II secret-key query
(\mathsf{gid}_{j}^{\prime},A_{j}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime})
, we have
\|(\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime}\|\leq B_{1}
by the bounded inner product condition. Since
\chi\geq\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot B_{1}
, Lemma
5
implies that the noise term statistically hides the difference, i.e.,

(\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime}+e_{3,1,j}^{(2)}\overset{s}{\approx}e_{3,1,j}^{(2)}.

Moreover, the expression (
9
) matches exactly the distribution of
y_{1,j}^{\prime}+e_{3,1,j}^{(2)}
in experiment
\mathsf{H}_{3,1,\mathsf{mid}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
. Since the distribution of
\mathbf{z}_{3,1}^{(1)}
remains unchanged across the two experiments, the distributions of
\mathbf{z}_{3,1}
are statistically indistinguishable between
\mathsf{H}_{3,0,\mathsf{mid}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{3,1,\mathsf{mid}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
56
(Continued).
By Claims
57
and
58
, and by applying a standard hybrid argument, we conclude that
\mathsf{H}_{3,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{3,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
.
∎

Proof of Claim
52
(Continued)
. Recall that our goal in Claim
52
is to show
\mathsf{H}_{0,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}\overset{c}{\approx}\mathsf{H}_{0,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
. By Lemmas
53
\sim
56
and standard hybrid argument, we conclude that no efficient distinguisher can distinguish between experiments
\mathsf{H}_{0,0}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{0,1}^{\mathsf{Pre}}
with non-negligible advantage. Specifically,

\displaystyle(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}],\mathbf{s}^{\top}[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}+[\mathbf{0}\mid\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{G}],\{\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]})

\displaystyle\overset{c}{\approx}\

\displaystyle(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}],\mathbf{s}^{\top}[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}]+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}+[\mathbf{0}\mid\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{G}],\{\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]}).

∎

VII-C

Parameters

Let
\lambda
be the security parameter.

•

We set the lattice dimension
n=\lambda^{1/\epsilon}
and the modulus
q=2^{\tilde{O}(n^{\epsilon})}=2^{\tilde{O}(\lambda)}
for some constant
\epsilon>0
, where
\tilde{O}(\cdot)
suppresses constant and logarithmic factors. Then
m=n\lceil\log q\rceil=\tilde{O}(\lambda^{1+1/\epsilon})
.

•

We set
L=2^{\lambda}
to support ciphertext policies of arbitrary polynomial size, i.e.,
\ell=\poly(\lambda)
.

•

For static security (Theorem
51
), we set
m^{\prime}=\tilde{O}(\lambda^{1+1/\epsilon}),Q_{0}=\poly(\lambda)
and
\chi^{\prime}=\Theta(\lambda^{1+1/\epsilon})
. We relies on the
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,m_{1},q,\chi_{s}}
assumption and the
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}_{n,m,m^{\prime},q,\chi,\chi}
assumption, where the noise parameter
\chi_{s}=\chi_{s}(\lambda)
is set polynomial-bounded and
\chi
satisfies the bound
\chi\geq\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot\max\{B_{1},\sqrt{\lambda}\chi_{s}\}
. By setting
B_{1}=\poly(\lambda)
, we can choose
\chi=2^{\tilde{O}(n^{\epsilon})}
.

•

To ensure correctness (Theorem
50
), we require
\chi>\sqrt{n\log q}\cdot\omega(\sqrt{\log n})
, which is also satisfied by setting
\chi=2^{\tilde{O}(n^{\epsilon})}
. In addition, the correctness bound requires
B_{0}=\sqrt{\lambda}\chi m+\lambda\chi\chi^{\prime}m^{\prime}+\lambda\chi^{2}mL
, which implies
B_{0}=2^{\tilde{O}(n^{\epsilon})}
.

We summarize with the following instantiation:

Corollary 2

(
(B_{0},B_{1})
-
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
for subset policies in the random oracle model)
.

Let
\lambda
be a security parameter. Assuming polynomial hardness of
\mathsf{LWE}
and the
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
, both holding under a sub-exponential modulus-to-noise ratio, there exists a statically secure
(B_{0},B_{1})
-
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABNIPFE}
scheme for subset policies in the random oracle model with
B_{0}/B_{1}=\mathrm{superpoly}(\lambda)
.

VIII
Noiseless
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
Scheme from
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
Assumption (in the Random Oracle Model)

In this section, we present a construction of a
noiseless

\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
scheme for subset policies in the random oracle model. This construction is obtained by applying the modulus-switching
technique to Construction
1
.

Construction 2

(
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
for subset policies in the random oracle model)
.

Let
\lambda
be the security parameter. Let
n,p
and
q
be lattice parameters, and define
m=n\lceil\log q\rceil
. Let
m^{\prime}
,
\chi
, and
\chi^{\prime}
be additional lattice parameters. Let
\mathcal{AU}=\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
be the universe of authority identifiers, and
\mathcal{GID}=\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
be the universe of global user identifiers. Let
\mathsf{H}:\mathcal{GID}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}}
be a hash function, modeled as a random oracle which outputs samples drawn from the discrete Gaussian distribution
D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}
, as in Construction
1
.

A multi-authority attribute-based (noiseless) inner-product functional encryption scheme (
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
) over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}
for subset policies consists of a tuple of efficient algorithms

\Pi_{\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}}=(\mathsf{GlobalSetup},\mathsf{AuthSetup},\mathsf{Keygen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec}).

The algorithms proceed as follows:

•

\mathsf{GlobalSetup}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow\mathsf{gp}
: The global setup algorithm takes as input the security parameter
\lambda
, and outputs the global parameters
\mathsf{gp}=(\lambda,n,m,m^{\prime},p,q,\chi,\chi^{\prime},\mathsf{H})
.

•

\mathsf{AuthSetup}(\mathsf{gp},\mathsf{aid})\rightarrow(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}})
: The authority setup algorithm takes as input the global parameters
\mathsf{gp}
and an authority identifier
\mathsf{aid}\in\mathcal{AU}
. It samples
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{td}_{\mathsf{aid}})\leftarrow\mathsf{TrapGen}(1^{n},1^{m},q),\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m^{\prime}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
. It outputs a public key
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}=(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}})
and a master secret key
\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}}=\mathsf{td}_{\mathsf{aid}}
.

•

\mathsf{Keygen}(\mathsf{gp},\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})\rightarrow\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}
: The key generation algorithm takes as input the global parameters
\mathsf{gp}
, the public key
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}=(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}})
, the authority’s master secret key
\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}}=\mathsf{td}_{\mathsf{aid}}
, the user identifier
\mathsf{gid}\in\mathcal{GID}
, the key vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}
(naturally lifted to
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
when required). It first computes
\mathbf{r}\leftarrow\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})
and then uses the trapdoor
\mathsf{td}_{\mathsf{aid}}
for
\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}}
to sample
\mathbf{k}\leftarrow\mathsf{SamplePre}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{td}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v})+\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}\mathbf{r},\chi)
. It outputs a secret key
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}=\mathbf{k}
.

•

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{gp},\{\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A},\mathbf{u})\rightarrow\mathsf{ct}
: The encryption algorithm takes as input the global parameters
\mathsf{gp}
, a set of public keys
\{\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A}=\{(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}})\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A}
associated with authorities
A\subseteq\mathcal{AU}
, and a plaintext vector
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}
. For each
\mathsf{aid}\in A
, it samples
\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n},\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m}
. It also samples
\mathbf{e}_{2}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m^{\prime}}
, and
\mathbf{e}_{3}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m}
. It outputs the ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
, where

\mathsf{ct}=\left(\left\{\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\right\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A},\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A}\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top},\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A}\mathbf{s}_{\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{aid}}+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}+\lceil\mathbf{u}^{\top}\rfloor_{p\rightarrow q}\mathbf{G}\right).

•

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{gp},\{\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v},\mathsf{ct})\rightarrow\Gamma
: The decryption algorithm takes as input the global parameters
\mathsf{gp}
, a collection of secret keys
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}=\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}
associated with authorities
\mathsf{aid}\in A
, a user identifier
\mathsf{gid}\in\mathcal{GID}
, a key vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}
, and a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}=(\{\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A},\mathbf{c}_{2}^{\top},\mathbf{c}_{3}^{\top})
. It computes
\mathbf{r}\leftarrow\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})
and outputs

\Gamma=\lceil\mathbf{c}_{3}^{\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v})+\mathbf{c}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{r}-\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A}\mathbf{c}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\rfloor_{q\rightarrow p}.

VIII-A

Correctness

Theorem 59

(Correctness)
.

Let
\chi_{0}=\sqrt{n\log q}\cdot\omega(\sqrt{\log n})
be a polynomial such that Lemma
7
holds. Suppose that the lattice parameters
n,m,m^{\prime},p,q,\chi,\chi^{\prime}
satisfy the following conditions:

•

\chi\geq\chi_{0}(n,q)
.

•

q>np^{2}+2pB_{0}
, where
B_{0}=\sqrt{\lambda}\chi m+\lambda\chi\chi^{\prime}m^{\prime}+\lambda\chi^{2}m\ell
.

Then the scheme
\Pi_{\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}}
in Construction
2
is
correct
as a noiseless
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
scheme.

Proof.

The proof idea follows the same structure as that of Theorem
24
.
Take any plaintext vector
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}
, any key vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}
, an arbitrary set of authorities
\{\mathsf{aid}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A}
, and an arbitrary user identifier
\mathsf{gid}\in\mathcal{GID}
. First, sample the global parameters
\mathsf{gp}\leftarrow\mathsf{GlobalSetup}(1^{\lambda})
, and generate the authority keys
(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}})\leftarrow\mathsf{AuthSetup}(\mathsf{gp},\mathsf{aid})
for each
\mathsf{aid}\in A
. Then, generate the secret keys
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}=\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Keygen}(\mathsf{gp},\mathsf{msk}_{\mathsf{aid}},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v})
for each
\mathsf{aid}\in A
, and obtain the resulting ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{gp},\{\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{aid}}\}_{\mathsf{aid}\in A},\mathbf{u})
.

The decryption algorithm outputs:

\Gamma=\left\lceil\lceil\mathbf{u}^{\top}\rfloor_{p\rightarrow q}\cdot\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v})+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{r}-\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A}\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\right\rfloor_{q\rightarrow p}.

Suppose that
\lceil\mathbf{u}^{\top}\rfloor_{p\rightarrow q}=\frac{q}{p}\mathbf{u}^{\top}+\delta_{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}
with
\|\delta_{\mathbf{u}}\|\leq 1/2
. Substituting into the expression for
\Gamma
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Gamma

\displaystyle=\left\lceil\frac{q}{p}\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v}+\delta_{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v})+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{r}-\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A}\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\right\rfloor_{q\rightarrow p}

\displaystyle=\frac{p}{q}\left(\frac{q}{p}\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v}+\delta_{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v})+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{r}-\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A}\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\right)+\delta^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v}+\dfrac{p}{q}\left(\delta_{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v})+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{r}-\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A}\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\right)+\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p},

for some
|\delta^{\prime}|\leq 1/2
.
Since both
\Gamma
and
\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v}
lie in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, we have

\Gamma=\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v}
if and only if

|e^{\prime}|=\left|\dfrac{p}{q}\left(\delta_{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\top}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v})+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{r}-\sum_{\mathsf{aid}\in A}\mathbf{e}_{1,\mathsf{aid}}^{\top}\mathbf{k}_{\mathsf{aid},\mathsf{gid},\mathbf{v}}\right)+\delta^{\prime}\right|<1.

According to the noise bounds analysis in Theorem
24
, we can get the upper bound

|e^{\prime}|\leq\frac{1}{2q}np^{2}+\frac{p}{q}B_{0}+\frac{1}{2},

where
B_{0}=\sqrt{\lambda}\chi m+\lambda\chi\chi^{\prime}m^{\prime}+\lambda\chi^{2}mL
. Thus, when
q>np^{2}+2pB_{0}
, it holds that the total rounding error
|e^{\prime}|<1
, which ensures that
\Gamma=\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v}
. Therefore,
\Pi_{\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}}
is correct as a noiseless
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
scheme.
∎

VIII-B

Static Security

The proof follows essentially the same structure and reduction strategy as that of Theorem
51
, and is therefore omitted.

Theorem 60

(Static Security)
.

Let
\chi_{0}(n,q)=\sqrt{n\log q}\cdot\omega(\sqrt{\log n})
be a polynomial such that Lemma
7
holds. Let
Q_{0}
be an upper bound on the number of secret-key queries submitted by the adversary
\mathcal{A}
. Suppose that the following conditions hold:

•

m^{\prime}>6n\log q
.

•

Let
\chi^{\prime}
be an error distribution such that
\chi^{\prime}=\Omega(\sqrt{n\log q})
.

•

Let
\chi
be an error distribution parameter such that
\chi\geq\max\{\chi_{0},\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot\sqrt{\lambda}\chi_{s}\}
, where
\chi_{s}
is an error parameter such that
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,m_{1},q,\chi_{s}}
assumption holds for some
m_{1}=\poly(m,m^{\prime},Q_{0})
.

•

The assumption
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}_{n,m,m^{\prime},q,\chi,\chi}
holds.

Construction
2
is
statically secure
as a noiseless
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
scheme.

VIII-C

Parameters

Let
\lambda
be the security parameter.

•

We set the lattice dimension
n=\lambda^{1/\epsilon}
and the modulus
q=2^{\tilde{O}(n^{\epsilon})}=2^{\tilde{O}(\lambda)}
for some constant
\epsilon>0
, where
\tilde{O}(\cdot)
suppresses constant and logarithmic factors. Then
m=n\lceil\log q\rceil=\tilde{O}(\lambda^{1+1/\epsilon})
.

•

We set
L=2^{\lambda}
to support ciphertext policies of arbitrary polynomial size, i.e.,
\ell=\poly(\lambda)
.

•

For static security (Theorem
60
), we set
m^{\prime}=\tilde{O}(\lambda^{1+1/\epsilon}),Q_{0}=\poly(\lambda)
and
\chi^{\prime}=\Omega(\lambda^{1+1/\epsilon})
. The construction relies on the
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,m_{1},q,\chi_{s}}
assumption and the
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}_{n,m,m^{\prime},q,\chi,\chi}
assumption, where the noise parameter
\chi_{s}=\chi_{s}(\lambda)
is polynomial-bounded and
\chi
must satisfy the bound
\chi\geq\lambda^{\omega(1)}\cdot\sqrt{\lambda}\chi_{s}
. We can set
\chi=2^{\tilde{O}(n^{\epsilon})}
.

•

To ensure correctness (Theorem
59
), we require
\chi>\sqrt{n\log q}\cdot\omega(\sqrt{\log n})
, which can also be satisfied by setting
\chi=2^{\tilde{O}(n^{\epsilon})}
. In addition, the correctness bound requires
q>np^{2}+2pB_{0}
where
B_{0}=\sqrt{\lambda}\chi m+\lambda\chi\chi^{\prime}m^{\prime}+\lambda\chi^{2}m\ell=2^{\tilde{O}(n^{\epsilon})}
, and
p=2^{\tilde{O}(n^{\epsilon})}
is chosen accordingly to meet this bound.

We summarize with the following instantiation:

Corollary 3

(Noiseless
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
for Subset Policies in the Random Oracle Model)
.

Let
\lambda
be a security parameter. Assuming polynomial hardness of
\mathsf{LWE}
and the
\mathsf{IND}\text{-}\mathsf{evIPFE}
, both holding under a sub-exponential modulus-to-noise ratio, there exists a statically-secure noiseless
\mathsf{MA}\text{-}\mathsf{ABIPFE}
scheme for subset policies in the random oracle model.

Appendix A
Hardness of Evasive
\mathsf{IPFE}

A-A

Evasive
\mathsf{LWE}
from
[
44
]

We recall the evasive
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption proposed in
[
44
]
.

Theorem 61

(Evasive
\mathsf{LWE}

[
44
]
)
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
be a security parameter, and let
n,q,m,K,\chi,\chi^{\prime}
be lattice parameters specified by
\lambda
. Denote
\mathsf{gp}=(1^{\lambda},q,1^{n},1^{m},1^{K},1^{\chi},1^{\chi^{\prime}})
. Let
\mathsf{Samp}
be a sampling algorithm, which takes as input the global parameter
\mathsf{gp}
, and outputs a matrix
\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times K}
, a set of matrices
\mathbf{Q}_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times k_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times k_{\ell}}
, and auxiliary information
\mathsf{aux}\in\{0,1\}^{*}
.

For two adversaries
\mathcal{A}_{0}
and
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, we define their advantage functions as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Samp},\mathcal{A}_{0}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\left|\mathrm{Pr}\left[\mathcal{A}_{0}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},\mathbf{C},\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top},\{\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]})=1\right]\right.

\displaystyle-\left.\mathrm{Pr}\left[\mathcal{A}_{0}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\bm{\delta}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},\mathbf{C},\bm{\delta}_{2}^{\top},\{\bm{\delta}_{3,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]})=1\right]\right|;

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Samp},\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{\mathsf{Post}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\left|\mathrm{Pr}\left[\mathcal{A}_{1}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},\mathbf{C},\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top},\{\mathbf{K}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell]})=1\right]\right.

\displaystyle-\left.\mathrm{Pr}\left[\mathcal{A}_{1}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\bm{\delta}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},\mathbf{C},\bm{\delta}_{2}^{\top},\{\mathbf{K}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell]})=1\right]\right|;

where the parameters are sampled as follows:

•

(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{Q}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{Q}_{\ell},\mathsf{aux})\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(\mathsf{gp}),

•

(\mathbf{A}_{1},\mathsf{td}_{1}),\ldots,(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathsf{td}_{\ell})\leftarrow\mathsf{TrapGen}(1^{n},1^{m},q),

•

\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{\ell}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n},\mathbf{s}^{\top}\leftarrow[\mathbf{s}_{1}^{\top}\mid\ldots\mid\mathbf{s}_{\ell}^{\top}]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell},

•

\mathbf{e}_{1,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{m},\mathbf{e}_{3,i}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{k_{i}}\text{ for each }i\in[\ell],\mathbf{e}_{2}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\chi}^{K},

•

\bm{\delta}_{1,i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\bm{\delta}_{3,i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{k_{i}}\text{ for each }i\in[\ell],\bm{\delta}_{2}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{K},

•

\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{1,i}^{\top},\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{3,i}^{\top}\text{ for each }i\in[\ell],\quad\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{C}+\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\top}
,

•

\mathbf{K}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{SamplePre}(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathsf{td}_{i},\mathbf{Q}_{i},\chi^{\prime})\text{ for each }i\in[\ell].

We say that the
\mathsf{evLWE}_{n,m,K,q,\chi,\chi^{\prime}}
assumption holds, if for all efficient samplers
\mathcal{S}
, the following implication holds: If there exists an efficient adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}
with a non-negligible advantage function
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Samp},\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{\mathsf{Post}}(\lambda)
, then there exists another efficient adversary
\mathcal{A}_{0}
with a non-negligible advantage function
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Samp},\mathcal{A}_{0}}^{\mathsf{Pre}}(\lambda)
.

A-B

Reduction from
\mathsf{evLWE}
(
[
44
]
) to
\mathsf{evIPFE}

Theorem 62
.

Suppose that the assumption
\mathsf{evLWE}_{n,m,K,q,\chi,\chi^{\prime}}
holds, where
K=m+m^{\prime}
, then the assumption
\mathsf{evIPFE}_{n,m,m^{\prime},q,\chi,\chi^{\prime}}
holds.

Proof.

Suppose, for contradiction, that
\mathsf{evIPFE}_{n,m,m^{\prime},q,\chi,\chi^{\prime}}
does not hold with respect to some sampler
\mathcal{S}=(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{v}},\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{u}})
. First, we begin by constructing a sampler
\mathsf{Samp}
for the
\mathsf{evLWE}_{n,m,K,q,\chi,\chi^{\prime}}
assumption. On input the global parameter
\mathsf{gp}=(1^{\lambda},q,1^{n},1^{m},1^{K},1^{\chi},1^{\chi^{\prime}})
, the sampler
\mathsf{Samp}
proceeds as follows.

1.

It runs
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{v}}(\mathsf{gp}^{\prime})
where
\mathsf{gp}^{\prime}=(1^{\lambda},q,1^{n},1^{m},1^{m^{\prime}},1^{\chi},1^{\chi^{\prime}})
, which outputs

\begin{array}[]{l}1^{\ell},\{1^{k_{i}}\}_{i\in[\ell]};\\
(\mathbf{r}_{1,1},\mathbf{v}_{1,1}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{1,k_{1}},\mathbf{v}_{1,k_{1}});\\
\cdots\\
(\mathbf{r}_{\ell,1},\mathbf{v}_{\ell,1}),\ldots,(\mathbf{r}_{\ell,k_{\ell}},\mathbf{v}_{\ell,k_{\ell}}).\end{array}

2.

It samples
\mathbf{C}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell\times K}
, and parses it as

\mathbf{C}=[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathbf{P}]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}\mathbf{B}_{1}&\mathbf{P}_{1}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
\mathbf{B}_{\ell}&\mathbf{P}_{\ell}\end{array}\right],

where
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell\times m^{\prime}},\mathbf{P}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell\times m}
and
\mathbf{B}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m^{\prime}},\mathbf{P}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
for each
i\in[\ell]
.

3.

It constructs each
\mathbf{Q}_{i}
as

\mathbf{Q}_{i}\leftarrow[\mathbf{B}_{i}\mid\mathbf{P}_{i}]\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}\mathbf{r}_{i,1}&\cdots&\mathbf{r}_{i,k_{i}}\\
\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{i,1})&\cdots&\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{i,k_{i}})\end{array}\right]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times k_{i}}

4.

It then outputs
(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{Q}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{Q}_{\ell},\mathsf{aux})
, where
\mathsf{aux}
consists of the randomness used by
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{v}}
.

We claim that the assumption
\mathsf{evLWE}_{n,m,m^{\prime},q,\chi,\chi^{\prime}}
does not hold with respect to the sampler
\mathsf{Samp}
. Suppose that there exists an adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}
such that breaks the
\mathsf{evIPFE}
assumption. We then construct an adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\prime}
that breaks the
\mathsf{evLWE}
assumption, using
\mathcal{A}_{1}
as a subroutine. The adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\prime}
proceeds as follows:

1.

Adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\prime}
begins by receiving a
\mathsf{evIPFE}
challenge
(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},\mathbf{C},\mathbf{y}_{2}^{\top},\{\mathbf{K}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell]})
, where
\mathbf{A}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\mathbf{z}_{1,i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\mathbf{K}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m\times k_{i}}
for each
i\in[\ell]
, and
\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\ell\times K},\mathbf{z}_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times K}
.

2.

It samples
\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pri}}\xleftarrow{\$}\{0,1\}^{\kappa}
, where
\kappa
denotes the upper bounds of the random bits used by
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{u}}
, runs
\mathbf{u}\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathsf{gp},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}};\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pri}})
, and sends the tuple

(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},\mathbf{C},\mathbf{y}_{2}^{\top}+[\mathbf{0}_{m^{\prime}}\mid\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G}],\{\mathbf{K}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell]})

to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

3.

Finally, adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\prime}
outputs whatever
\mathcal{A}_{1}
outputs.

By the definition of
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathsf{Samp}
above, adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\prime}
simulates perfectly the challenger of the
\mathsf{evLWE}
distinguishing game. The tuple sent to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
has the same distribution as the two possible types of
\mathsf{evIPFE}
challenge instances, depending on which one is received.

It remains to show that the parameters provided by
\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\prime}
satisfies the precondition of the
\mathsf{evLWE}
assumption, i.e.,

(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},\mathbf{C},\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top},\{\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]})\overset{c}{\approx}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\bm{\delta}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},\mathbf{C},\bm{\delta}_{2}^{\top},\{\bm{\delta}_{3,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]}),

then the corresponding
\mathsf{evIPFE}
precondition naturally holds, i.e.,

(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{z}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},\mathbf{C},\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{G},\{\mathbf{z}_{3,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]})\overset{c}{\approx}(1^{\lambda},\mathbf{r}_{\mathsf{pub}},\{\mathbf{A}_{i},\bm{\delta}_{1,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]},\mathbf{C},\bm{\delta}_{2}^{\top},\{\bm{\delta}_{3,i}^{\top}\}_{i\in[\ell]}),

where the parameters
\mathbf{z}_{1,i},\mathbf{z}_{2},\mathbf{z}_{3,i},\bm{\delta}_{1,i},\bm{\delta}_{2},\bm{\delta}_{3,i}
are sampled as in Theorem
61
. This equivalence of distributions follows directly from the parameter construction defined in the reduction procedure.
∎
